for a frequency vector  $f$  with length  $n$ , we define  $f_{p}(f) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} |f_{i}|^{p}$ . the  $l_{p}$  norm $^{2}$  of  $f$  is defined by  $l_{p}(f) = (f_{p}(f))^{1/p}$ . we also use the notation  $\| f \|_{p}$  to denote the  $l_{p}$  norm of  $f$ . the  $f_{p}$ -moment estimation problem is also often stated as the norm estimation problem, since an algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to one of these problems can be modified to output a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to the other using a constant rescaling of  $\varepsilon$  (for constant  $p$ ); thus we use these two equivalent problems interchangeably. the  $l_{2}$ -heavy hitters problem is to output all coordinates  $i$  such that  $f_{i} \geq \varepsilon \cdot l_{2}$ . the problem permits the output of coordinates  $j$  with  $f_{j} \leq \varepsilon \cdot l_{2}$  provided that  $f_{j} \geq \frac{1}{2} \cdot l_{2}$ . moreover, each coordinate output by the algorithm also requires an estimate to its frequency, with additive error at most  $\frac{1}{2} \cdot l_{2}$ .

definition 1.9  $((\varepsilon, m)$ -flip number). [bjwy20] for  $\varepsilon > 0$  and an integer  $m > 0$ , the  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -flip number of a sequence  $y_1, \ldots, y_m$  is the largest integer  $k$  such that there exist  $0 \leq i_1 < \ldots < i_k \leq m$  with  $y_{i_j - 1} \notin [(1 - \varepsilon)y_{i_j}, (1 + \varepsilon)y_{i_j}]$  for all integers  $j \in [2, k]$ .

definition 1.10 (( $\varepsilon, m$ -twist number). for  $\varepsilon > 0$  and an integer  $m > 0$ , the  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number for a non-negative function  $f$  on a stream of updates  $1 = u_1, \ldots, u_m$  is the largest integer  $k$  such that there exist  $0 \leq i_1 < \ldots < i_k \leq m$  with for each  $j \in [2, k]$ , either  $f(u_1 : u_{j-1}) \notin [(1 - \varepsilon)f(u_1 : u_j), (1 + \varepsilon)f(u_1 : u_j)]$  or  $f(u_{j-1} : u_j) \geq \varepsilon f(1 : u_{j-1})$ .

definition 1.11 (strong tracking). let  $f^{(1)}, \ldots, f^{(m)}$  be the frequency vectors induced by a stream of length  $m$  and let  $g: \mathbb{r}^n \to \mathbb{r}$  be a function on frequency vectors. an algorithm  $a$  provides  $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ -strong  $g$ -tracking if at each time step  $t \in [m]$ ,  $a$  outputs an estimate  $x_t$  such that

$$
| x _ {t} - g (f ^ {(t)}) | \leq \varepsilon | g (f ^ {(t)}) |
$$

for all  $t \in [m]$  with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ .

observation 1.12. [bjwy20] for  $p > 0$ , the  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number of  $\| x \|_p^p$  in the insertion-only model is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log m\right)$  for  $p \leq 2$  and  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{p}{\varepsilon} \log m\right)$  for  $p > 2$ .

# 2 framework for adversarily robust streaming algorithms

in this section, we describe a general framework for adversarially robust streaming algorithms, using the sketch stitching and granularity changing techniques. we first require the following specific form of a difference estimator.

definition 2.1 (fixed-prefix difference estimator). given a stream  $s$ , a fixed time  $t_1$ , and a splitting time  $t_2$  that is only revealed at time  $t_2$ , let frequency vector  $v$  be induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_1$  to  $t_2$  and frequency vector  $w_t$  be induced by updates from time  $t_2$  to  $t$  exclusive. given an accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon > 0$  and a failure probability  $\delta \in (0,1)$ , a streaming algorithm  $\mathcal{b}(t_1, t_2, t, \gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$  is a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator for a function  $f$  if, with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ , it outputs an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f(v)$  approximation to  $f(v + w_t) - f(v)$  simultaneously for all  $t \geq t_2$  with  $f(v + w_t) - f(v) \leq \gamma \cdot f(v)$  and  $f(w_t) \leq \gamma f(v)$  for a ratio parameter  $\gamma \in (0,1]$ .

we shall use the shorthand "difference estimator" terminology to refer to definition 1.1 until section 8; in section 8 we will introduce an additional notion of a difference estimator for when  $f(v)$  can change.

it is easy to see that difference estimators offer an immediate means to approximation algorithms.

**framework 1.2.** given a monotonic function  $f$ , frequency vectors  $u, v_1, \ldots, v_k$ , a  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)$  multiplicative approximation to  $f(u)$ , and  $\left(\gamma_i, \varepsilon_i, \frac{1}{3k}\right)$ -difference estimators to  $f(u + v_1 + \ldots + v_i) - f(u + v_1 + \ldots + v_{i-1})$  for  $i \in \{1, \ldots, k\}$  (and  $v_0 = 0$ ) such that  $\prod_{i=1}^k (1 + \gamma_i) \leq 2$  and  $\sum_{i=1}^k \varepsilon_i \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{4}$ , then their sum gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f(u + v_1 + \ldots + v_i)$  with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$ .

proof. since  $f(u + v_{1} + \ldots + v_{i}) = f(u + v_{1} + \ldots + v_{i - 1}) + (f(u + v_{1} + \ldots + v_{i - 1}) - f(u + v_{1} + \ldots + v_{i}))$  and the difference is bounded by  $\gamma_{i} \cdot f(u + v_{1} + \ldots + v_{i - 1})$  by the definition of difference estimator, then we have the invariant  $f(u + v_{1} + \ldots + v_{i}) \leq (1 + \gamma_{i}) f(u + v_{1} + \ldots + v_{i - 1}) \leq 2f(u)$  for all  $i \in \{1, \ldots, k\}$  by induction. thus the sum of the additive errors from each difference estimator is at most  $\sum_{i = 1}^{k} \varepsilon_{i} \cdot f(u + v_{1} + \ldots + v_{i - 1}) \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{4} \cdot 2f(u) = \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(u)$ . similarly the additive error due to the  $(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{2})$ -approximation to  $f(u)$  is also  $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(u)$ , so that by monotonicity of  $f$  and a telescoping argument the sum gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  multiplicative approximation to  $f(u + v_{1} + \ldots + v_{i})$ . since each difference estimator fails with probability at most  $\frac{1}{3k}$ , then by a union bound, the total probability of failure is at most  $\frac{1}{3}$ .

theorem 1.3. suppose each  $(\gamma_i, \varepsilon, \frac{1}{3k})$ -difference estimator uses space  $\frac{\gamma_i}{\varepsilon^2} \cdot s$  and  $s$  is a monotonic function in  $k := \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ ,  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon_i}$ , and the input size. if  $\gamma_i \leq \frac{1}{2^i}$  and there are  $n_i \leq 2^i$  instances of  $(\gamma_i, \varepsilon, \frac{1}{3k})$ -difference estimators, then the total space is at most  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{s \log n}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$ .

proof. the total space is  $\sum_{i=1}^{k} n_i \cdot \frac{\gamma_i}{\varepsilon^2} \cdot s$ . since  $n_i \leq 2^i$ ,  $\gamma_i \leq \frac{1}{2^i}$ , and  $k = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ , then the total space is at most  $k \cdot \frac{s}{\varepsilon^2} \leq \mathcal{o}(s \log n) \varepsilon^2$ .

# 2.1 algorithm

we first describe a simplified version of our adversarially robust framework that adapts the usage of difference estimators. to achieve a robust  $(1 + \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon))$ -approximation, [bjwy20] used a "switch-a-sketch" technique that maintains  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -flip number  $\xi$  (recall definition 1.9) independent subroutines that each provide a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  to a function  $f$  evaluated on the frequency vector induced by the stream, with high probability. for the remainder of the discussion, we assume the  $\xi = \omega\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)$ , which is true for many important functions  $f$ , especially the important  $f_{p}$  moments and moreover, that the twist number  $\lambda = \xi$ , which is true for insertion-only streams. to prevent an adversary from affecting the output of the algorithm, each subroutine is effectively only used once. the output of the  $i$ -th subroutine is only used the first time the true output of the subroutine is at least  $(1 + \varepsilon)^{i}$ . the algorithm of [bjwy20] then repeatedly outputs this value until the  $(i + 1)$ -st subroutine is at least  $(1 + \varepsilon)^{i + 1}$ , at which point the algorithm switches to using the output of the  $(i + 1)$ -st subroutine instead. hence, the adversary information-theoretically knows nothing about the internal randomness of the  $i$ -th subroutine until the output is at least  $(1 + \varepsilon)^{i}$ . however, due to monotonicity of  $f$  and correctness of the oblivious  $(i + 1)$ -st instance, whatever knowledge the adversary gains about the  $i$ -th instance does not impact the internal randomness of future instances. intuitively, the switch-a-sketch approach uses a sketch once and switches to another sketch once the estimated  $f$  has increased by  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ .  $f$  can only increase  $\lambda$  times by definition of the  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number. since  $\lambda = \omega\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)$ , this approach generally achieves  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon^3}$  space dependency.

algorithm 1 framework for robust algorithms on insertion-only streams  
input: stream  $u_{1},\ldots ,u_{m}\in [n]$  of updates to coordinates of an underlying frequency vector, accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$ $(\gamma ,\varepsilon ,\delta)$  -difference estimator  $\mathcal{b}$  for  $f$  with space dependency  $\frac{\gamma^c}{\varepsilon^2}$  for  $c\geq 1$  , oblivious strong tracker  $\mathcal{a}$  for  $f$    
output: robust  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  -approximation to  $f$    
1:  $\delta \leftarrow \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)}$ $\zeta \gets \frac{2}{2(c - 1) / 4 - 1}$ $\eta \leftarrow \frac{\varepsilon}{64\zeta}$ $\beta \leftarrow \lceil \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon} \rceil$    
2:  $a\gets 0,\varphi \gets 2^{(c - 1) / 4},\gamma_j\gets 2^{j - 1}\eta$    
3: for  $j\in [\beta ]$ $\eta_j\gets \frac{\eta}{\beta}$  if  $c = 1$ $\eta_j\gets \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta - j}}$  if  $c > 1$  . ▷accuracy for each difference estimator   
4: for each update  $u_{t}\in [n],t\in [m]$  do   
5:  $x\gets a_{a + 1}(1,t,\eta ,\delta)$    
6: if  $x > 2^a$  then ▷switch sketch at top layer   
7:  $a\gets a + 1,b\gets 0,z_a\gets x,t_{a,j}\gets t$  for  $j\in [\beta ]$    
8:  $x\gets$  estimatef ▷compute estimator  $x$  for  $f$  using unrevealed sketch   
9: if  $x > \left(1 + \frac{(b + 1)\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot z_a$  then ▷switch sketch at lower layer   
10:  $b\gets b + 1,k\gets \operatorname {lsb}(b,1),j\gets \lfloor \frac{b}{2^k}\rfloor$    
11:  $z_{a,k}\gets \mathcal{b}_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\gamma_k,\eta_k,\delta)$  ▷freeze old sketch   
12:  $t_{a,j}\gets t$  for  $j\in [k]$  . ▷update difference estimator times   
13: return  $(1 + \frac{b\varepsilon}{8})\cdot z_{a}$  ▷output estimate for round  $t$

we first observe that if we instead use the switch-a-sketch technique each time  $f$  increases by a power of 2, then we only need to switch  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  sketches. effectively, this follows from setting  $\varepsilon = \mathcal{o}(1)$  in the value of  $\lambda$ . let  $t_i$  be the first time  $f$  of the stream surpasses  $2^i$ . the challenge is

algorithm 2 subroutine estimatef of algorithm 1  
1:  $x\gets z_{a},k\gets$  numbits  $(b + 1)$ $z_{i}\leftarrow \mathrm{lsb}(b + 1,k + 1 - i)$  for  $i\in [k]$ $\triangleright z_1 > \ldots >z_k$  are the nonzero bits in the binary representation of  $b + 1$    
3: for  $1\leq j\leq k - 1$  do  $\triangleright$  compile previous frozen components for estimator  $x$    
4:  $x\gets x + z_{a,j}$    
5:  $j\gets \lfloor \frac{b + 1}{2^zk}\rfloor$    
6:  $x\gets x + \mathcal{b}_{a,j}\left(1,t_{a,z_k},t,\gamma_{z_k},\eta_{z_k},\delta\right)$  use unrevealed sketch for last component   
7: return  $x$

then achieving a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f$  at the times between each  $2^i$  and  $2^{i+1}$ . let  $u$  be the underlying frequency vector at time  $t_i$ , so that  $f(u) \geq 2^i$ . if  $v$  is the underlying frequency vector at some time between  $t_i$  and  $t_{i+1}$ , then we can decompose  $f(v) = f(u) + \sum_{j=1}^{\beta} (f(u_j) - f(u_{j-1}))$ , where we use the convention that  $u_0 = u$  and  $u_\beta = j$ . moreover, we assume that  $f(u_j) - f(u_{j-1}) \leq \gamma \cdot f(v)$  for  $\gamma \leq \frac{1}{2^j}$ .

our key observation is that because we only care about a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f(v)$ , we do not need a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to each of the differences  $f(u_{j}) - f(u_{j-1})$ , which may be significantly smaller than  $f(v)$ . for example, note that a  $(2^{j} \cdot \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f(u_{j}) - f(u_{j-1})$  only equates to an additive  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon \cdot f(v)\right)$  error, since  $f(u_{j}) - f(u_{j-1}) = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{2^{j}}\right) \cdot f(v)$ . we require  $\frac{1}{\gamma}$  instances of algorithms with such accuracies, to account for the various possible vectors  $v$ . thus if there exists an algorithm that uses  $\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}} s(n)$  bits of space to output additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f(v)$  error, then the space required across the level  $j$  estimators is  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}} s(n)$  bits of space. since there are at most  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  levels, then we do not incur any additional factors in  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon}$ . recall that a difference estimator (definition 1.1) to  $f$  serves exactly this purpose!

it is not obvious how to obtain a difference estimator for various functions  $f$ . however, for the purposes of a general framework, the theoretical assumption of such a quantity suffices; we shall give explicit difference estimators for specific functions  $f$  of interest. the framework appears in full in algorithm 1.

interpretation of algorithm 1. we now translate between the previous intuition and the pseudocode of algorithm 1. the streaming algorithm  $\mathcal{a}_{a + 1}$  attempts to notify the algorithm whenever the value of  $f$  on the underlying frequency vector induced by the stream has increased by a power of two. the role of  $a$  serves as a counter, ensuring that a new instance of  $\mathcal{a}$  is used each time the value of  $a$  has increased. since  $a$  increases when  $\mathcal{a}_{a + 1} > 2^a$ , i.e., the value of  $f$  has increased by roughly a factor of two, then the instances  $\mathcal{a}$ , indexed by  $a$ , perform sketch switching with the finest accuracy. thus, algorithm 1 defines  $z$  to be the value of  $\mathcal{a}_a$  when it is revealed and never uses  $\mathcal{a}_a$  again.

it remains for algorithm 1 to stitch the sketches between the times when  $a$  increases. suppose  $t_a$  is the time when  $z$  was most recently defined. then algorithm 1 uses the variables  $z_{a,k}$  to stitch together the sketches of the difference estimators  $\mathcal{b}$  for  $f(1:t) - f(1:t_a)$ . each variable  $z_{a,k}$  refers to a separate granularity of the difference estimator  $\mathcal{b}$ . like the streaming algorithm  $\mathcal{a}$ , the difference estimators  $\mathcal{b}$  are indexed by the counter  $a$  to distinguish between the instances required for the sketch switching technique. additionally, the difference estimators  $\mathcal{b}$  for the variable  $z_{a,k}$  are indexed by a counter  $j$  that represents the number of times a level  $k$  difference estimator is required throughout the times  $t$  that we roughly have  $2^a < f(1:t) < 2^{a+1}$ . the subroutine

estimatef then stitches together the values  $z_{a,k}$  to form an estimate  $x$  of  $f(1:t)$ . similarly, algorithm 1 stitches together the values  $z_{a,k}$  to form an estimate  $x$  of  $f(1:t)$ , which it then rounds as the output. the actual stitching process requires partitioning  $f(1:t) - f(1:t_a)$ , so that the appropriate granularities may be used for each difference estimator. this is monitored by the nonzero bits in the binary representation of the counter  $b$ , which roughly denotes the ratio of  $f(1:t) - f(1:t_a)$  to  $f(1:t_a)$ . now of course an inconvenience in the actual analysis is that because  $\mathcal{b}$  and  $\mathcal{a}$  are approximation algorithms, then we do not exactly have  $2^a < f(1:t) < 2^{a+1}$ , although this is true up to a multiplicative  $(1 + \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon))$ -factor.

# 2.2 analysis

we first show correctness of algorithm 1 at times where the counter  $a$  increases. this allows us to subsequently focus on times  $t \in (t_i, t_{i+1})$  between points in the stream where the counter increases.

lemma 2.2 (correctness when  $f$  doubles). let  $f$  be a monotonic function with  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number  $\lambda = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n\right)$  for  $\log m = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ . for any integer  $i > 0$ , let  $t_i$  be the update time during which the counter  $a$  in algorithm 1 is first set to  $i$ . then algorithm 1 outputs  $a\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)$ -approximation to  $f$  at all times  $t_i$ , with probability at least  $1 - \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\delta \log n}{\varepsilon}\right)$ .

proof. note that by definition, the counter  $a$  in algorithm 1 is incremented from  $i - 1$  to  $i$  in round  $t_i$ . thus all previous outputs of algorithm 1 have not used the subroutine  $\mathcal{a}_i$ . hence, the adversarial input is independent of the randomness of  $\mathcal{a}_i$ . thus by the correctness guarantee of the algorithm,  $\mathcal{a}_i$  outputs a  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)$ -approximation to  $f$  at time  $t_i$ , with probability  $1 - \delta$ . since  $f$  has twist number  $\lambda = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n\right)$ , then there are at most  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n\right)$  update times  $t_i$  in which the counter  $a$  is increased. by a union bound, algorithm 1 outputs a  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)$ -approximation to  $f$  at all times  $t_i$ , with probability at least  $1 - \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\delta \log n}{\varepsilon}\right)$ .

for any integer  $i > 0$ , let  $t_i$  be the update time during which the counter  $a$  in algorithm 1 is first set to  $i$ . since lemma 2.2 shows correctness at all times  $t_i$ , it remains to analyze the behavior of algorithm 1 between each of the times  $t_i$  and  $t_{i+1}$ .

lemma 2.3 (geometric bounds on splitting times). for each integer  $j \geq 0$  and a fixed integer  $i$ , let  $u_{i,j}$  be the last round for which  $z_{i,j}$  in algorithm 1 is defined. with probability at least  $1 - \delta \cdot \text{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$ , we have  $f(1, u_{i,j}) - f(1, t_{i,j}) \leq \frac{2^j \varepsilon}{64} \cdot f(1, t_i)$  for each positive integer  $j > 1$ .

proof. as before, let  $t_i$  be the update time during which the counter  $a$  in algorithm 1 is first set to  $i$  for any integer  $i > 0$ . let  $t_{i,j}$  be the first round such that the counter  $b$  in algorithm 1 is first set to  $j$ , and we use the convention that  $t_{i,0} = t_i$ . let  $\mathcal{e}_1$  be the event that algorithm 1 outputs a  $(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{32})$ -approximation to the value of  $f$  at all times  $t_i$ , so that by lemma 2.2,  $\mathcal{e}_1$  holds with probability at least  $1 - \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\delta\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)$ . for the remainder of the proof, we fix the integer  $i$  and show correctness between  $t_i$  and  $t_{i+1}$ . note that conditioning on  $\mathcal{e}_1$ , we have correctness at times  $t_i$  and  $t_{i+1}$ .

let  $\mathcal{e}_2$  be the event that the first poly  $\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)$  instances of  $\mathcal{b}$  and  $\mathcal{a}$  in algorithm 1 are correct, so that  $\mathbf{pr}[\mathcal{e}_2]\geq 1 - \delta \cdot \mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)$  by a union bound. we show by induction on  $b$  that conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}_1$  and  $\mathcal{e}_2$ , we have that  $f(1,u_{i,j}) - f(1,t_{i,j})\leq \frac{1}{2^{\beta - j - 3}} f(1,t_i)$  for  $j\geq 1$ .

for the base case  $b = 1$ , only  $z_{i,1}$  is defined. suppose by way of contradiction that  $f(1,u_{i,1}) - f(1,t_{i,1}) > \frac{1}{2^{\beta - j - 3}}\cdot f(1,t_i)$ . since  $\beta = \left\lceil \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil$ , then  $f(1,u_{i,1}) - f(1,t_{i,1}) > \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot f(1,t_i)$ . observe that  $\mathcal{b}_{i,1}(1,t_{i,1},u_{i,1},\gamma_1,\eta_1,\delta)$  gives an  $\eta_1\cdot f(1,t_i)$  additive approximation to  $f(1,u_{i,1}) - f(1,t_{i,1})$ . hence, the estimate  $z_{i,1}$  of  $f(1,u_{i,1}) - f(1,t_{i,1})$  from estimatef is at least

$$
z _ {i, 1} \geq f (1, u _ {i, 1}) - f (1, t _ {i, 1}) - \eta_ {1} \cdot f (1, t _ {i}) > \frac {\varepsilon}{3} \cdot f (1, t _ {i}),
$$

since  $\eta_{1} = \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta - 1}} < \frac{\varepsilon}{3}$  for  $\eta = \frac{\varepsilon}{64\zeta}$  and  $\varphi > 1$ . but this contradicts the fact that the counter  $b$  is set to  $j = 1$ , since

$$
z _ {i} + z _ {i, 1} > \left(1 + \frac {\varepsilon}{4}\right) z _ {i},
$$

since  $z_{i} \leq (1 + \varepsilon) \cdot f(1, t_{i})$  conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}_{1}$ . thus we have  $f(1, u_{i,1}) - f(1, t_{i,1}) > \frac{1}{2^{\beta - j - 3}} \cdot f(1, t_{i})$ , which completes the base case  $j = 1$ .

for a fixed value  $\ell > 0$  of the counter  $b$ , let  $k = \mathrm{numbits}(\ell)$  and for  $x \in [k]$ , let  $z_{x} = \mathrm{lsb}(\ell, k - x + 1)$ , so that  $z_{1} > \ldots > z_{k}$  are the nonzero bits of  $\ell$ . let  $u = \sum_{x=1}^{k-1} 2^{z_{x}-1} \cdot z_{x}$  be simply the decimal representation of the binary number resulting from changing the least significant bit of  $\ell$  to zero. moreover, if  $x_{u}$  is the output at time  $t_{i}^{(u)}$ , then we have  $x_{u} \geq \left(1 + \frac{u\varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot z_{i}$ , since the counter  $b$  is first set to  $u$  at time  $t_{i}^{(u)}$ . note that  $z_{i,z_{x}}$  has not been changed from time  $t_{i}^{(u)}$  to  $t_{i}^{(\ell)}$  for all  $x \in [k-1]$ . since  $x_{\ell} = \sum_{x=1}^{k} z_{i,z_{x}}$ , then we have

$$
x _ {\ell} = z _ {i, z _ {k}} + x _ {u} \geq z _ {i, z _ {k}} + \left(1 + \frac {u \varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot z _ {i}.
$$

suppose by way of contradiction that  $f(1, u_{i, z_k}) - f(1, t_{i, z_k}) > \frac{1}{2^{\beta - z_k - 3}} \cdot f(1, t_i)$ . recall that  $z_{i, z_k}$  is an additive  $2\eta_{z_k}f(1, t_i)$  approximation to  $f(1, u_{i, z_k}) - f(1, t_{i, z_k})$ . hence we have

$$
z _ {i, z _ {k}} > \frac {1}{2 ^ {\beta - z _ {k} - 3}} \cdot f (1, t _ {i}) - 2 \eta_ {z _ {k}} f (1, t _ {i}) > \frac {2 ^ {z _ {k}} \cdot \varepsilon}{3} \cdot f (1, t _ {i}),
$$

since  $\beta = \left\lceil \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon} \right\rceil$  and  $\eta_{z_k} = \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta - z_k}} < \frac{\varepsilon}{3}$  for  $\varphi > 1$ . then we have

$$
x _ {\ell} = z _ {i, z _ {k}} + x _ {u} > \frac {2 ^ {z _ {k}} \cdot \varepsilon}{3} \cdot f (1, t _ {i}) + \left(1 + \frac {u \varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot z _ {i} > \left(1 + \frac {\ell \varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot z _ {i},
$$

for sufficiently small constant  $\varepsilon > 0$ , since  $2^{z_k} \geq (u - \ell)$  and  $f(1, t_i) \geq (1 - \varepsilon)z_i$  conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}_1$ . but this contradicts the fact that the counter  $b$  is set to  $\ell$  at time  $t_i^{(\ell)}$ , since

$$
x _ {\ell} > \left(1 + \frac {\ell \varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot z _ {i}.
$$

thus we have  $f(1, u_{i,z_k}) - f(1, t_{i,z_k}) \leq \frac{1}{2^{\beta - z_k - 3}} \cdot f(1, t_i)$ , which completes the inductive step. the result then follows from observing that for  $\beta = \lceil \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon} \rceil$ , we have that  $\frac{1}{2^{\beta - j - 3}} f(1, t_i) \leq \frac{2^j\varepsilon}{64}$ .

lemma 2.4 (correctness on non-adaptive streams). with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ -poly  $\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$ , algorithm 1 outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f$  at all times on a non-adaptive stream.

proof. as before, let  $t_i$  be the update time during which the counter  $a$  in algorithm 1 is first set to  $i$  for any integer  $i > 0$ . for each integer  $j \geq 0$ , let  $u_{i,j}$  be the last round for which  $z_{i,j}$  in algorithm 1 is defined. let  $t_{i,\ell}$  be the first round such that the counter  $b$  in algorithm 1 is first set to  $\ell$ , and we use the convention that  $t_{i,0} = t_i$ . let  $\mathcal{e}_1$  be the event that algorithm 1 outputs a  $(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{32})$ -approximation to the value of  $f$  at all times  $t_i$ , so that by lemma 2.2,  $\mathcal{e}_1$  holds with probability at least  $1 - \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\delta\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)$ . for the remainder of the proof, we fix the integer  $i$  and show correctness between  $t_i$  and  $t_{i+1}$ . note that conditioning on  $\mathcal{e}_1$ , we have correctness at times  $t_i$  and  $t_{i+1}$ . by the monotonicity of  $f$ , it suffices to show correctness at all times  $t_{i,\ell}$  for each value  $\ell$  obtained by the counter  $b$  between times  $t_i$  and  $t_{i+1}$ .

let  $\ell$  be a fixed value of the counter  $b$ ,  $k = \mathrm{numbits}(\ell)$ , and  $z_{x} = \operatorname{lsb}(\ell, x)$  for  $x \in [k]$  so that  $z_{1} > \ldots > z_{k}$  are the nonzero bits in the binary representation of  $\ell$ . by lemma 2.3, we have for all  $x \in [k]$

$$
f (1, u _ {i, z _ {x}}) - f (1, t _ {i, z _ {x}}) \leq \frac {1}{2 ^ {\beta - x - 3}} f (1, t _ {i}),
$$

with probability at least  $1 - \delta \cdot \mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$ . since  $u_{i,z_x}$  is the last round for which  $z_{i,z_x}$  in algorithm 1 is defined and all times  $t_{i,j}$  for  $j \leq z_x$  are reset at that round, then we have  $u_{i,z_x} = t_{i,z_{x+1}}$  for all  $x \in [k]$ . thus since  $u_{i,z_k} = t_{i,\ell}$ , we can decompose

$$
f (1, t _ {i, \ell}) = \sum_ {x = 1} ^ {k} \big (f (1, u _ {i, z _ {x}}) - f (1, u _ {i, z _ {x - 1}}) \big) = \sum_ {x = 1} ^ {k} \big (f (1, u _ {i, z _ {x}}) - f (1, t _ {i, z _ {x}}) \big).
$$

recall that the value  $x_{\ell}$  of  $x$  output by estimatef at time  $t_{i,\ell}$  satisfies

$$
x _ {\ell} = z _ {i} + \sum_ {x = 1} ^ {k} z _ {i, z _ {x}}.
$$

by lemma 2.2,  $z_{i}$  incurs at most  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)$  multiplicative error to  $f(1, t_{i,\ell})$  since  $f(1, t_{i,\ell}) \leq 4f(t_{i})$ . moreover,  $z_{i, z_k}$  is an additive  $2\eta_{z_k} \cdot f(1, t_i)$  approximation to  $f(1, u_{i, z_k}) - f(1, t_{i, z_k})$ . hence, the total additive error of  $x_{\ell}$  to  $f(1, t_{i,\ell})$  is at most

$$
\frac {\varepsilon}{4} \cdot f (1, t _ {i, \ell}) + \sum_ {x = 1} ^ {k} 2 \eta_ {z _ {x}} \cdot f (1, t _ {i}).
$$

now if the  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator uses space dependency  $\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2}$ , then  $\eta_x = \frac{\eta}{\beta}$  for all  $x \in [\beta]$ , so that

$$
\sum_ {x = 1} ^ {k} 2 \eta_ {z _ {x}} \cdot f (1, t _ {i}) \leq \sum_ {x \in [ \beta ]} \eta_ {x} \cdot f (1, t _ {i}) \leq \frac {\varepsilon}{3 2} \cdot f (1, t _ {i})
$$

and the error is at most  $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(1, t_{i,\ell})$ . on the other hand, if the  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator uses space dependency  $\frac{\gamma^c}{\varepsilon^2}$  for  $c > 1$ , then  $\eta_x = \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta - x}}$  for  $\varphi = 2^{(c - 1) / 4}$  implies that the error is at most

$$
\sum_ {x = 1} ^ {k} 2 \eta_ {z _ {x}} \cdot f (1, t _ {i}) \leq \frac {\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f (1, t _ {i, \ell}).
$$

therefore,  $x_{\ell}$  is a  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)$ -approximation to  $f(1,t_{i,\ell})$

note that the final output at time  $t_{i,\ell}$  by algorithm 1 further incurs additive error at most  $\frac{\varepsilon}{8} \cdot z_i$  due to the rounding  $\left(1 + \frac{b\varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot z_a$  in the last step. by lemma 2.2,  $z_i \leq \left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot f(1, t_{i,\ell})$ . hence for sufficiently small constant  $\varepsilon > 0$ , we have that algorithm 1 outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f(1, t_{i,\ell})$ , with probability at least  $1 - \delta \cdot \text{poly} \left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$ .

theorem 2.5 (framework for adversarially robust algorithms on insertion-only streams). let  $\varepsilon, \delta > 0$  and  $f$  be a monotonic function with  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number  $\lambda = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)$  on a stream of length  $m$ , with  $\log m = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ . suppose there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f$  that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma^c}{\varepsilon^2} \cdot s_1(n, \delta, \varepsilon) + s_2(n, \delta, \varepsilon)\right)$  bits of space for some constant  $c \geq 1$  and a strong tracker for  $f$  that use  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \cdot s_1(n, \delta, \varepsilon) + s_2(n, \delta, \varepsilon)\right)$  bits of space and functions  $s_1, s_2$  that depend on  $f$ . then there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation for  $f$  that succeeds with constant probability. for  $c > 1$ , the algorithm uses

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log n \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log n \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {2} n\right)
$$

bits of space, where  $\delta' = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)}\right)$ . for  $c = 1$ , the algorithm uses

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log n \log^ {3} \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log n \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {2} n\right)
$$

bits of space.

proof. consider algorithm 1. lemma 2.4 proves correctness over non-adaptive streams. we now argue that lemma 2.4 holds over adversarial streams. the claim follows from a similar argument to the switch-a-sketch technique of [bjwy20]. observe that each time either counter  $a$  or  $b$  increases, the subroutine estimatef causes algorithm 1 to use new subroutines  $\mathcal{b}_{i,j}$  and  $\mathcal{a}_i$  that have not previously been revealed to the adversary. thus the adversarial input is independent of the randomness of  $\mathcal{b}_{i,j}$  and  $\mathcal{a}_i$ . hence by the correctness guarantee over non-adaptive streams, algorithm 1 outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation at all times.

we now analyze the space complexity of algorithm 1 for a difference estimator with space dependency  $\frac{\gamma^c}{\varepsilon^2}$  with  $c > 1$ . recall that estimatef uses instance  $j = \left\lfloor \frac{b + 1}{2^k} \right\rfloor$  for  $\mathcal{b}_{i,j}$  for each ratio parameter  $\gamma_k = 2^{k - 1}\eta$  and accuracy parameter  $\eta_k = \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta - k}}$ , where  $k \in [\beta]$  for  $\beta = \mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ ,  $\varphi = 2^{(c - 1)/4}$ , and  $\eta = \frac{\varepsilon}{64c}$ .

by assumption, a single instance of  $\mathcal{b}$  uses

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma^ {c}}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta , \varepsilon) + s _ {2} (n, \delta , \varepsilon)\right)
$$

bits of space. since we require correctness of poly  $\left(\log n, \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  instances of  $\mathcal{b}$ , it suffices to set  $\delta' = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)}\right)$ . for granularity  $k$ , we have  $j = \left\lfloor \frac{b + 1}{2^k} \right\rfloor$  for  $\mathcal{b}_{i,j}$  so that there are at most  $\frac{c_2}{2^{k\varepsilon}}$  instances of  $\mathcal{b}_{i,j}$ , for some absolute constant  $c_2 > 0$ . thus for fixed  $a$ ,  $\beta \leq \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon} + 1$ ,  $\gamma_k = 2^{k-1}\eta$ , and accuracy parameter  $\eta_k = \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-k}}$ , the total space for each  $\mathcal{b}_{a,j}$  across the  $\beta$  granularities is

$$
\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {3} \gamma_ {k} ^ {c}}{\eta_ {k} ^ {2} 2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {c _ {3}}{2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {3} \gamma_ {\beta - k} ^ {c}}{\eta_ {\beta - k} ^ {2} 2 ^ {\beta - k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {c _ {3}}{2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon)
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {4} (2 ^ {\beta - k} \varepsilon) ^ {c} \varphi^ {2 k}}{\varepsilon^ {2} \cdot 2 ^ {\beta - k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {c _ {4}}{2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \\ \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {5} 2 ^ {- c k} \varphi^ {2 k}}{\varepsilon^ {2} \cdot 2 ^ {- k}} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {c _ {4}}{2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon), \\ \end{array}
$$

for some absolute constants  $c_3, c_4, c_5 > 0$ . since  $\varphi = 2^{(c - 1) / 4}$  for  $c > 1$ , then

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {3} \gamma_ {k} ^ {c}}{\eta_ {k} ^ {2} 2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {c _ {3}}{2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {5} 2 ^ {k - c k} 2 ^ {(c k - k) / 2}}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {c _ {4}}{2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \\ \leq \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon)\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

observe that for a stream with  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number  $\lambda = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)$ , we have  $a = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ , since the value of  $f$  increases by a constant factor each time  $a$  increases. hence, accounting for  $a = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ , then algorithm 1 uses

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log n \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log n \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon)\right)
$$

bits of space in total for  $c > 1$  across each of the strong trackers and difference estimators.

finally, we observe that because we run  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log n\right)$  instances of the subroutines, it takes  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log^2 n\right)$  total additional bits of space to maintain the splitting times for each of the instances over the course of a stream of length  $m$ , with  $\log m = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ , so that the total space is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log n \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log n \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {2} n\right).
$$

we now analyze the space complexity for algorithm 1 for a difference estimator with space dependency  $\frac{\gamma^c}{\varepsilon^2}$  with  $c = 1$ . instead of the accuracy parameter  $\eta_k = \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta - k}}$  for  $c > 1$ , we now have  $\eta_k = \frac{\eta}{\beta}$  for  $c = 1$ . thus for fixed  $a$ ,  $\beta \leq \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon} + 1$ , and  $\gamma_k = 2^{k - 1}\eta$ , there exist constants  $c_6, c_7 > 0$  such that the total space for each  $\mathcal{b}_{a,j}$  across the  $\beta$  granularities is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {3} \gamma_ {k} ^ {c}}{\eta_ {k} ^ {2} 2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {c _ {3}}{2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {6} (2 ^ {k - 1} \varepsilon)}{\eta^ {2} \beta^ {- 2} \cdot 2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {c _ {6}}{2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \\ \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {7} \beta^ {2}}{\eta^ {2}} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {c _ {7}}{2 ^ {k} \varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \\ \leq \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {3} \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon)\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

again accounting for  $a = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$  and the space it takes to maintain the splitting times, then algorithm 1 uses

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log n \log^ {3} \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log n \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {2} n\right).
$$

bits of space in total for  $c > 1$  across each of the strong trackers and difference estimators.

![](images/32664e53df015d03e623375047d63d27f17b95dbfe08761a14d5c5400f839db8.jpg)

# 3 robust  $f_{2}$  estimation

in this section, we use the previous framework of section 2 to give an adversarially robust streaming algorithm for  $f_{2}$  moment estimation. recall that to apply theorem 2.5, we require an  $f_{2}$  strong tracker and an  $f_{2}$  difference estimator. we present these subroutines in this section. we further optimize our algorithm beyond the guarantees of theorem 2.5 specifically for  $f_{p}$  moments, so that our final space guarantees in theorem 3.8 matches the best known  $f_{2}$  algorithm on insertion-only streams, up to lower order polylog  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon}$  terms. finally, we show that our algorithm naturally extends to the problem of finding the  $l_{2}$ -heavy hitters, along with producing an estimate for the frequency of each heavy-hitter up to an additive  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon) \cdot l_{2}$  error.

we first recall the following  $f_{2}$  strong tracker.

theorem 3.1 (oblivious  $f_{2}$  strong tracking). [bdn17] given an accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon >0$  and a failure probability  $\delta \in (0,1)$ , let  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} +\log \frac{1}{\delta} +\log \log n\right)\right)$ . there exists an insertion-only streaming algorithm estimator that uses  $\mathcal{o}(d\log n)$  space to provide  $(\varepsilon ,\delta)$ -strong  $f_{2}$  tracking of an underlying frequency vector  $f$ .

to define our difference estimator, we first note that "good"  $f_{2}$  approximation to two vectors  $u$  and  $v$  also gives a "good" approximation to their inner product  $\langle u, v \rangle$ .

lemma 3.2 (ams  $f_{2}$  approximation gives inner product approximation). let vectors  $u, v \in \mathbb{r}^n$  and  $m \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times n}$  be a sketching matrix so that each entry  $m_{i,j}$  of  $m$  is a four-wise independent random sign scaled by  $\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}$  for  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$ . then

$$
\pr \left| \langle u, v \rangle - \langle m u, m v \rangle \right| \leq \varepsilon \| u \| _ {2} \| v \| _ {2} \geq \frac {2}{3}.
$$

proof. the proof is a standard variance reduction argument that follows immediately from lemma 3.3 and lemma 3.4 and chebyshev's inequality, since  $m$  can be viewed as taking the arithmetic mean of  $d$  scaled sign vectors  $s_1, \ldots, s_d$ .

lemma 3.3 (strong tracking of ams inner product approximation). given vectors  $0^n \preceq u_1 \preceq u_2 \preceq \ldots \preceq u_m \in \mathbb{r}^n$  whose entries are bounded by a polynomial in  $n$ , there exists an algorithm that uses a sketching matrix  $m \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times n}$  with  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta} + \log \log n\right)\right)$  such that for  $m = \mathrm{poly}(n)$  and a fixed  $v \in \mathbb{r}^n$  with  $v \succeq 0^n$ ,

$$
| \langle u _ {i}, v \rangle - \langle m u _ {i}, m v \rangle | \leq \varepsilon \| u _ {i} \| _ {2} \| v \| _ {2},
$$

simultaneously for all  $i\in [m]$  with probability at least  $1 - \delta$

proof. consider a sequence of indices  $t_1 < t_2 < \ldots < t_q$  such that  $t_{i+1}$  is the minimal index such that  $\langle u_{t_{i+1}}, v \rangle \geq \left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right) \langle u_{t_i}, v \rangle$  and observe that  $q = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n\right)$  for  $m = \mathrm{poly}(n)$  since each vector  $u_i$  has polynomially bounded entries. since  $0^n \preceq u_1 \preceq u_2 \preceq \ldots \preceq u_m \in \mathbb{r}^n$  and thus both  $\|u_1\|_2 \leq \|u_2\|_2 \leq \ldots \|u_m\|_2$  and  $\langle u_1, v \rangle \leq \langle u_2, v \rangle \leq \ldots \leq \langle u_m, v \rangle$ , it suffices to show that

$$
| \langle u _ {t _ {i}}, v \rangle - \langle m u _ {t _ {i}}, m v \rangle | \leq \varepsilon \| u _ {t _ {i}} \| _ {2} \| v \| _ {2},
$$

for all  $i \in [q]$ . by lemma 3.4 and lemma 3.5, we have that for any

$$
\operatorname * {p r} \left| \langle u _ {t _ {i}}, v \rangle - \langle m u _ {t _ {i}}, m v \rangle \right| \leq \varepsilon \| u _ {t _ {i}} \| _ {2} \| v \| _ {2} \geq \frac {2}{3}
$$

for a sketching matrix with  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  rows. thus by taking a sketching matrix with  $d$  rows, for  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta} + \log \log n\right)\right)$ , and using a standard median-of-means approach, we can take a union bound over all  $i \in [q]$ . therefore,

$$
| \langle u _ {i}, v \rangle - \langle m u _ {i}, m v \rangle | \leq \varepsilon \| u _ {i} \| _ {2} \| v \| _ {2},
$$

simultaneously for all  $i \in [m]$  with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ .

lemma 3.4. let  $s \in \mathbb{r}^n$  be a sign vector  $\{-1, +1\}^n$  with four-wise independent entries. for any  $u, v \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \langle s, u \rangle \cdot \langle s, v \rangle \right] = \langle u, v \rangle .
$$

proof. by linearity of expectation, we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \langle s, u \rangle \cdot \langle s, v \rangle \right] = \mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} s _ {i} u _ {i}\right) \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} s _ {i} v _ {i}\right) \right] \\ = \mathbb {e} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} s _ {i} u _ {i} s _ {j} v _ {j} \right] = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {e} \left[ s _ {i} u _ {i} s _ {j} v _ {j} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

since the variables  $s_i$  are 4-wise independent sign variables  $\{-1, +1\}$ , then  $\mathbb{e}[s_is_j] = 1$  for  $i = j$  and  $\mathbb{e}[s_is_j] = 0$  for  $i \neq j$ . hence,

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \langle s, u \rangle \cdot \langle s, v \rangle \right] = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} u _ {i} v _ {i} = \langle u, v \rangle .
$$

lemma 3.5. let  $s \in \mathbb{r}^n$  be a sign vector  $\{-1, +1\}^n$  with four-wise independent entries. for any  $u, v \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , we have

$$
\operatorname {v a r} \left(\langle s, u \rangle \cdot \langle s, v \rangle\right) \leq \left(\langle u, v \rangle\right) ^ {2}.
$$

proof. since  $\operatorname{var}\langle s, u \rangle \cdot \langle s, v \rangle \leq \mathbb{e}\left[(\langle s, u \rangle \cdot \langle s, v \rangle)^2\right]$ , it follows by linearity of expectation that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v a r} \left(\langle s, u \rangle \cdot \langle s, v \rangle\right) \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} s _ {i} u _ {i}\right) ^ {2} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} s _ {i} v _ {i}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = \mathbb {e} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} s _ {i} s _ {j} s _ {k} s _ {\ell} u _ {i} v _ {j} u _ {k} v _ {\ell} \right] \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {e} \left[ s _ {i} s _ {j} s _ {k} s _ {\ell} u _ {i} v _ {j} u _ {k} v _ {\ell} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

since the variables  $s_i$  are 4-wise independent sign variables  $\{-1, +1\}$ , then  $\mathbb{e}[s_i s_j s_k s_\ell] = 1$  if  $i, j, k, \ell$  consists of two (possibly not distinct) pairs of indices, e.g.,  $i = j$  and  $k = \ell$ , and  $\mathbb{e}[s_i s_j s_k s_\ell] = 0$  otherwise. therefore,

$$
\operatorname {v a r} \left(\langle s, u \rangle \cdot \langle s, v \rangle\right) \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} u _ {i} v _ {i} u _ {j} v _ {j} = (\langle u, v \rangle) ^ {2}.
$$

![](images/a27f6e596fbad0134b69e2ba6a595ad95377f9f068c6abda30ff2bd4329e0ea8.jpg)

we now give our  $f_{2}$  difference estimator using the inner product approximation property.

lemma 3.6 ( $f_{2}$  difference estimator). there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f_{2}$  that uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma \log n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}\right)\right).
$$

proof. let  $\varepsilon' = \frac{\min(1, \varepsilon)}{32}$  and  $\delta' = \frac{\delta}{2}$ . given an oblivious stream  $s$ , let  $v$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_1$  to  $t_2$  and  $w_t$  be the frequency vector induced by updates from time  $t_2$  to  $t$  exclusive, with  $\gamma \cdot f_2(v) \geq f_2(w_t)$ . let  $m(x)$  represent the output of the algorithm estimator with accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon'$  and failure rate  $\delta'$  on an input vector  $x$  induced by a stream. hence we have  $m(x) \in \mathbb{r}^d$ , where  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} \left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)$ . we remark that the log log  $n$  term in the dimension of  $d$  from theorem 3.1 results from the analysis of [bdn17] breaking the stream into  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  times when the value of  $f_2$  on the stream doubles. we do not need this log log  $n$  term since  $\gamma \cdot f_2(v) \geq f_2(w_t)$  for an absolute constant  $\gamma > 0$  implies that the value of the difference can only double a constant number of times.

by lemma 3.2, we have that with probability at least  $1 - \delta'$ ,

$$
| \langle v, w _ {t} \rangle - \langle m (v), m (w _ {t}) \rangle | \leq \frac {4 \varepsilon^ {\prime}}{\sqrt {\gamma}} \| v \| _ {2} \| w _ {t} \| _ {2}.
$$

recalling the identity

$$
f _ {2} (v + w _ {t}) - f _ {2} (v) = 2 \langle v, w _ {t} \rangle + \| w _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2},
$$

we thus have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \left[ f _ {2} (v + w _ {t}) - f _ {2} (v) \right] - \left[ 2 \langle m (v), m (w _ {t}) \rangle + \| m (w _ {t}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right] \right| \\ = \left| \left[ 2 \langle v, w _ {t} \rangle - 2 \langle m (v), m (w _ {t}) \rangle \right] + \left[ \| w _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \| m (w _ {t}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right] \right| \\ \leq 8 \varepsilon^ {\prime} \| v \| _ {2} \| w _ {t} \| _ {2} + 3 \varepsilon^ {\prime} \| w _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $\varepsilon' = \frac{\min(1, \varepsilon)}{32}$ . because  $\gamma \cdot f(v) \geq f(w_t)$ , then we have  $\|w_t\|_2 \leq \sqrt{\gamma}\|v\|_2$ , so that the total error of the estimator is at most

$$
\frac {8 \varepsilon^ {\prime}}{\sqrt {\gamma}} \| v \| _ {2} \| w _ {t} \| _ {2} + 3 \varepsilon^ {\prime} \| w _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq 8 \varepsilon^ {\prime} \cdot f (v) + 3 \varepsilon^ {\prime} \gamma \cdot f (v).
$$

hence for  $\gamma \leq 2$ , the error is at most  $14\varepsilon' \cdot f(v) \leq \varepsilon \cdot f(v)$ . since theorem 3.1 and lemma 3.2 each hold with probability at least  $1 - \delta'$  for  $\delta' = \frac{\delta}{2}$ , then the total probability of success is at least  $1 - \delta$ .

by theorem 3.1, each of the two instances of estimator uses

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma \log n}{(\varepsilon^ {\prime}) ^ {2}} \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {\prime}} + \log \frac {1}{\delta^ {\prime}} + \log \log n\right)\right)
$$

bits of space, for  $\varepsilon' = \frac{\min(1,\varepsilon)}{32}$  and  $\delta' = \frac{\delta}{2}$ . however, the log log  $n$  term in the space requirement of estimator results from the need to union bound over  $\log n$  locations where the value of  $f_{2}$  on the stream increases by a factor of 2. since  $\gamma$  is upper bounded by a constant, we only need a union

bound over a constant number of locations. hence, the space complexity of the  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f_{2}$  is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma \log n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}\right)\right).
$$

theorem 3.7. given  $\varepsilon >0$ , there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation for  $f_{2}$  that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{2}n\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} +\log \log n\right)\right)$  bits of space and succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$ .

proof.  $f_{2}$  is a monotonic function with  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number  $\lambda = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n\right)$ , by observation 1.12. by lemma 3.6 and theorem 3.1, there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator that uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma \log n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}\right)\right)
$$

and there exists an oblivious strong tracker for  $f_{2}$  that uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\log n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta} + \log \log n\right)\right).
$$

therefore by using the  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator and the oblivious strong tracker for  $f_{2}$  in the framework of algorithm 1, then theorem 2.5 with  $s_{1} = \log n\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta} + \log \log n\right)$ ,  $c = 1$ , and  $s_{2} = 0$  proves that there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation for  $f_{2}$  that uses space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}} \log^{2} n \log^{3} \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n\right)\right)$  and succeeds with constant probability.

optimized  $f_{2}$  algorithm. instead of maintaining all sketches  $\mathcal{b}_a$  and  $\mathcal{a}_{a,j}$  simultaneously, it suffices to maintain sketches  $\mathcal{b}_a$  and  $\mathcal{a}_{a,j}$  for  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  values of  $a$  at a time. namely, the counter  $a$  in algorithm 1 tracks the active instances  $\mathcal{b}_a$  and  $\mathcal{a}_{a,j}$  output by the algorithm. because the output increases by a factor of 2 each time  $a$  increases, it suffices to maintain the sketches  $\mathcal{b}_i$  and  $\mathcal{a}_{i,c}$  for only the smallest  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  values of  $i$  that are at least  $a$ . any larger index will have only missed  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon)$  fraction of the  $f_{2}$  of the stream and thus still output a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation.

theorem 3.8 (adversarily robust  $f_{2}$  streaming algorithm). given  $\varepsilon > 0$ , there exists an adversarily robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation for  $f_{2}$  that succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$  and uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log n \log^{4} \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \left( \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n \right)\right)$  bits of space.

proof. at any point, there are  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active indices  $a$  and  $\mathcal{o}(1)$  active indices  $c$  corresponding to sketches  $\mathcal{b}_a$  and  $\mathcal{a}_{a,c}$ . recall from theorem 2.5 that for fixed  $a$ , the total space for each  $\mathcal{a}_{a,j}$  across the  $\beta$  granularities is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {3} \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon)\right),
$$

where we have  $s_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon) = \log n\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} +\log \frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}} +\log \log n\right)$  and  $s_{2} = 0$  for our  $f_{2}$  difference estimator and  $f_{2}$  strong tracker.

whereas theorem 2.5 bounds the counter  $a$  by  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  so that we maintain  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  simultaneous instances of  $i$  for the algorithms  $a_{i,j}$ , we observed that it suffices to maintain at most

$\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  simultaneous active values of  $i$ . since there are still  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  total indices  $a$  over the course of the stream, then each sketch must have failure probability  $\frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}\left(\log n, \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}$  for the entire algorithm to have failure probability  $\delta = \frac{2}{3}$ . finally, there are  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active indices of  $a$ , consisting of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  subroutines. thus, it takes  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  additional bits of space to store the splitting times for each of the  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active subroutines across a stream of length  $m$ , with  $\log m = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ . thus the total space required is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log n \log^4 \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n\right)\right)$ .

heavy-hitters. as a simple corollary, note that our framework also solves the  $l_{2}$ -heavy hitters problem. by running separate  $l_{2}$ -heavy hitters algorithms corresponding to each difference estimator  $\mathcal{a}$  and strong tracker  $\mathcal{b}$ , with the heavy-hitter threshold corresponding to the accuracy of each procedure, we obtain a list containing all the possible heavy-hitters along with an estimated frequency of each item in the list.

theorem 3.9.  $[bci^{+}17]$  for any  $\varepsilon >0$  and  $\delta \in [0,1)$ , there exists a streaming algorithm  $(\varepsilon ,\delta) - \mathrm{bptree}$ , that with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ , returns a set of  $\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ -heavy hitters containing every  $\varepsilon$ -heavy hitter and an approximate frequency for every item returned with additive error  $\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot l_2$ . the algorithm uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log \frac{1}{\delta\varepsilon}\right)\left(\log n + \log m\right)\right)$  bits of space.

theorem 3.10 (adversarily robust  $l_{2}$ -heavy hitters streaming algorithm). given  $\varepsilon > 0$ , there exists an adversarily robust streaming algorithm heavyhitters that solves the  $l_{2}$ -heavy hitters problem with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$  and uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\log n\log^{4}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n\right)\right)$  bits of space.

proof. for each integer  $j \geq 0$  and a fixed integer  $i$ , let  $u_{i,j}$  be the last round for which  $z_{i,j}$  in algorithm 1 is defined. as before, let  $t_i$  be the update time during which the counter  $a$  in algorithm 1 is first set to  $i$  for any integer  $i > 0$ . let  $t_i^{(b)}$  be the first round such that the counter  $b$  in algorithm 1 is first set to  $j$ , and we use the convention that  $t_i^{(0)} = t_i$ .

let  $\mathcal{e}_1$  be the event that  $x_{a}$  is a  $(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{32})$ -approximation to  $f_{2}(1,t_{a})$  and  $\mathcal{e}_2$  be the event that  $f(1,u_{a,j}) - f(1,t_{a,j}) \leq \frac{1}{2^{\beta - j - 3}} f(1,t_{a})$  for each positive integer  $j > 1$ . by lemma 2.2 and lemma 2.3, we have  $\mathbf{pr}[\mathcal{e}_1 \wedge \mathcal{e}_2] \geq 1 - \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\delta \log n}{\varepsilon}\right)$ .

suppose there exists  $r \in [n]$  such that  $(f_r)^2 \geq \varepsilon^2 f_2(1, m)$ . then coordinate  $r$  must either be  $\frac{\varepsilon^2}{32^2}$ -heavy with respect to  $f_2(1, t_a)$  or  $\frac{2^k \cdot \varepsilon^2}{32^2 \beta^2}$ -heavy with respect to  $f_2(t_{a,k}, t_{a,k-1})$  for some integer  $k \in [\beta]$ , where we use the convention that  $t_{a,0} = t_a$ . hence bptree with threshold  $\frac{2^k \cdot \varepsilon^2}{32^2 \beta^2}$  with respect to  $f_2(t_{a,k}, t_{a,k-1})$  will detect that  $r$  is heavy. moreover, note that if coordinate  $r$  is only reported as heavy in an algorithm corresponding to the level  $k$  difference estimator, then  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon^2) \cdot f_2$  of the contribution of coordinate  $r$  towards the overall  $f_2$  still appears after  $r$  is reported as a heavy-hitter. thus by keeping track of the frequency of  $r$  after it is reported, we obtain an estimate of the frequency of  $r$  up to an additive  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon) \cdot l_2$ . since this is also the accuracy parameter of  $a_{a,k}$ , then the space complexity follows.

# 4 robust  $f_{p}$  estimation for  $0 < p < 2$

in this section, we use the framework of section 2 to give an adversarially robust streaming algorithm for  $f_{p}$  moment estimation, where  $p \in (0,2)$ . as before, we require an  $f_{p}$  strong tracker and an  $f_{p}$  difference estimator in order to apply theorem 2.5. whereas the  $f_{2}$  difference estimator

was straightforward from the inner product approximation via  $f_{2}$  approximation property, it is not clear that such a formulation exists for  $f_{p}$  approximation. instead, we use separate sketches for our  $f_{p}$  strong tracker and our  $f_{p}$  difference estimator. we present these subroutines in this section. finally, we again optimize our algorithm beyond the guarantees of theorem 2.5 so that our final space guarantees in theorem 4.14 matches the best known  $f_{p}$  algorithm on insertion-only streams, up to poly  $(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n)$  terms.

we first require the following definition for  $p$ -stable distributions, which will be integral to both our  $f_{p}$  strong tracker and our  $f_{p}$  difference estimator.

definition 4.1 ( $p$ -stable distribution). [zol89] for  $0 < p \leq 2$ , there exists a probability distribution  $\mathcal{d}_p$  called the  $p$ -stable distribution so that for any positive integer  $n$  with  $z_1, \ldots, z_n \sim \mathcal{d}_p$  and vector  $x \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , then  $\sum_{i=1}^{n} z_i x_i \sim \|x\|_p z$  for  $z \sim \mathcal{d}_p$ .

the probability density function  $f_{x}$  of a  $p$ -stable random variable  $x$  satisfies  $f_{x}(x) = \theta \left( \frac{1}{1 + |x|^{1 + p}} \right)$  for  $p < 2$ , while the normal distribution corresponds to  $p = 2$ . moreover, [nol03] details standard methods for generating  $p$ -stable random variables by taking  $\theta$  uniformly at random from the interval  $\left[ -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2} \right]$ ,  $r$  uniformly at random from the interval [0,1], and setting

$$
x = f (r, \theta) = \frac {\sin (p \theta)}{\cos^ {1 / p} (\theta)} \cdot \left(\frac {\cos (\theta (1 - p))}{\log \frac {1}{r}}\right) ^ {\frac {1}{p} - 1}.
$$

these  $p$ -stable random variables are crucial to obtaining a strong  $f_{p}$  tracking algorithm.

theorem 4.2 (oblivious  $f_{p}$  strong tracking for  $0 < p < 2$ ). [bdn17] for  $0 < p < 2$ , there exists an insertion-only streaming algorithm  $\mathrm{pstable}(1, t, \varepsilon, \delta)$  that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta} \right)\right)$  bits of space and provides  $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ -strong  $f_{p}$  tracking.

unfortunately, pstable is based on the  $p$ -stable sketch of [ind06], which offers a conceptual promise for the existence of the quantile estimators needed to guarantee provable bounds, but not an explicit computation. thus adapting the analysis of pstable in [ind06, bdn17] for the purposes of our difference estimator seems to be a challenge. even for the case  $p = 1$ , it does not seem evident how to adapt the median estimator of pstable to obtain a difference estimator for  $f_{p}$ .

instead, we describe a formulation of li's geometric mean estimator [li08], which also provides a streaming algorithm for  $f_{p}$ , but was not previously known to offer strong tracking. for a positive integer  $q \geq 3$ , let  $d$  be a multiple of  $q$  and let  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times n}$  have independent  $p$ -stable random variables for the entries of  $a$ . then for a vector  $x \in \mathbb{r}^n$  and  $y = ax$ , let  $z_{i} := c_{q,p} \cdot \left( \prod_{j = q(i - 1) + 1}^{qi} |y_{j}|^{p / q} \right)$  be the geometric mean of the inner products of  $q$  random  $p$ -stable vectors with the vector  $x$ , where

$$
c _ {q, p} = \left[ \frac {2}{\pi} \cdot \gamma \left(1 - \frac {1}{q}\right) \cdot \gamma \left(\frac {p}{q}\right) \cdot \sin \left(\frac {\pi p}{2 q}\right) \right] ^ {- q}.
$$

we have the following characterization of the asymptotic behavior of  $c_{q,p}$ :

observation 4.3 (characterization of  $c_{q,p}$ ). [li08]  $c_{q,p} = \mathcal{o}\left(\exp (\gamma_e(q - 1))\right)$ , for the euler-mascheroni constant  $\gamma_{e}\approx 0.57721$ .

(1) let  $a$  be a  $d \times n$  random matrix whose entries are i.i.d from the  $p$ -stable distribution  $\mathcal{d}_p$ , for  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2/p}}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n\right)\right)$  
(2) for a parameter  $q = 3$ , let each  $z_{i} = \prod_{j = q(i - 1) + 1}^{q i}(a v + a w_{t})_{j}^{p / q}$  and  $z_{i}' = \prod_{j = q(i - 1) + 1}^{q i}(a v)_{j}^{p / q}$ .  
(3) output the arithmetic mean of  $(z_{1} - z_{1}^{\prime}), (z_{2} - z_{2}^{\prime}), \ldots, (z_{d / q} - z_{d / q}^{\prime})$ .

fig. 7: difference estimator for  $f_{p}(v + w_{t}) - f_{p}(w_{t})$  with  $0 < p < 2$

the value of  $c_{q,p}$  is chosen so that each  $z_{i}$  is an unbiased estimate of  $\| x\| _p^p$ , as a result of the following statement. we can then bound the expectation and the variance of each random variable.

lemma 4.4 (expectation and variance of li's geometric mean estimator, lemma 2.2 in [li08]). let  $d$  be an integer multiple of an integer  $q \geq 3$  and let  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times n}$  have independent  $p$ -stable random variables for the entries of  $a$ . for a vector  $x \in \mathbb{r}^n$  and  $y = ax$ , let  $z_i := c_{q,p} \cdot \left( \prod_{j=q(i-1)+1}^{qi} |y_j|^{p/q} \right)$  be the geometric mean of the inner products of  $q$  random  $p$ -stable vectors with the vector  $x$ . then  $\mathbb{e}[z_i] = \|x\|_p^p$  and for  $\xi := \frac{c_{\frac{q}{2},p}}{c_{q,p}}$ , we have  $\mathbb{e}[z_i^2] \leq (\xi^2 - 1) \cdot \|x\|_p^{2p}$ .

thus by taking the arithmetic mean of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  variables  $z_{i}$ , we obtain a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $\| x\| _p^p$  with constant probability by chebyshev's inequality.

# 4.1  $f_{p}$  difference estimator for  $0 < p < 2$

we now describe our  $f_{p}$  difference estimator and give the high-level details of the analysis. we use li's geometric mean estimator to maintain  $a(v + w_{t})$  and  $av$ , where  $a$  is the sketching matrix for li's geometric mean estimator, and  $v$  and  $w_{t}$  are frequency vectors. observe that if we computed  $a(v + w_{t}) - av$ , then we would obtain  $a(w_{t})$ , which is a sketch that allows us to recover  $f_{p}(w_{t})$ . however, we want to estimate  $f_{p}(v + w_{t}) - f_{p}(v)$  rather than  $f_{p}(w_{t})$ . instead, we use the sketches  $a(v + w_{t})$  and  $av$  to compute terms  $z_{1}, z_{2}, \ldots, z_{1}', z_{2}', \ldots$ , where each  $z_{i}$  is the geometric mean of  $q$  consecutive entries in  $a(v + w_{t})$  and similarly  $z_{i}'$  is the geometric mean of  $q$  consecutive entries in  $av$ . since  $z_{i}$  is an unbiased estimator of  $f_{p}(v + w_{t})$  and  $z_{i}$  is an unbiased estimator of  $f_{p}(v)$ , it follows that  $z_{i} - z_{i}'$  is an unbiased estimator of  $f_{p}(v + w_{t}) - f_{p}(v)$ . we take the average of the values  $z_{i} - z_{i}'$  across  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)$  indices of  $i$  to obtain a single estimate and take the median of all estimates. the challenge is achieving both the variance bounds on  $z_{i} - z_{i}'$  while also obtaining a strong tracking property. to bound the variance, we expand  $z_{i} - z_{i}'$  to be a sum of  $2^{q} - 1$  geometric means of  $q$  terms, each with at least one term  $(\langle a_{j}, w_{t} \rangle)^{p/q}$ . since  $a_{j}$  is a vector of  $p$ -stable entries, then  $(\langle a_{j}, w_{t} \rangle)^{p/q}$  has the same distribution as  $(\| w_{t} \|_p \cdot x)^{p/q}$  for a  $p$ -stable random variable  $x$ . we also have  $f_{p}(w_{t}) = \gamma \cdot f(v)$ , where  $\gamma$  is bounded by some absolute constant. thus we can bound the probability that  $(\langle a_{j}, w_{t} \rangle)^{p/q} \geq \| v \|_p^{p/q}$ .

lemma 4.5 (expectation and variance of difference estimator terms). let  $p \in (0,2)$  and  $s$  be an oblivious stream on which  $v$  is the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time

$t_1$  to  $t_2$  and let  $u$  is the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $\mathcal{s}$  from time  $t_2$ . suppose  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v)\leq \gamma f_{p}(v)$  and  $f_{p}(u)\leq \gamma f_{p}(v)$ . then for each  $i\in [d / q]$ ,  $z_{i}$ , and  $z_i^\prime$  as defined in figure 7, we have  $\mathbb{e}[z_i - z_i^{\prime}] = f_p(v + u) - f_p(u)$  and

$$
\operatorname {v a r} (z _ {i} - z _ {i} ^ {\prime}) \leq 2 ^ {2 q} \gamma^ {2 / p} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2 p} = 2 ^ {2 q} \gamma^ {2 / p} (f _ {p} (v)) ^ {2}.
$$

proof. for an oblivious stream  $s$ , let  $v$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_1$  to  $t_2$  and let  $u$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_2$  so that  $f_p(v + u) - f_p(v) \leq \gamma f_p(v)$  and  $f_p(u) \leq \gamma f_p(v)$ . for any integer  $d > 0$ , let  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times n}$  be a matrix whose entries are drawn i.i.d. from the  $p$ -stable distribution  $\mathcal{d}_p$ .

recall that li's geometric mean estimator first takes a geometric mean  $z_{i}$  of  $q$  separate inner products, where  $i \in \left[\frac{d}{q}\right]$ . explicitly, each variable  $z_{i}$  in the estimator takes the form

$$
\begin{array}{l} z_{i}:= c_{q,p}\cdot \prod_{j = q(i - 1) + 1}^{qi}(\langle a_{j},v + u\rangle)^{p / q} \\ = c _ {q, p} \cdot \left(\prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} \left(1 + \frac {\langle a _ {j} , u \rangle}{\langle a _ {j} , v \rangle}\right) ^ {p / q}\right) \cdot \left(\prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} (\langle a _ {j}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $a_{j}$  is the  $j$ -th column of  $a$ . we define

$$
t _ {i} := \prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} \left(1 + \frac {\langle a _ {j} , u \rangle}{\langle a _ {j} , v \rangle}\right) ^ {p / q}.
$$

similarly, li's geometric mean estimator for  $v$  is a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation for  $\| v \|_p^p = f_p(v)$  and each variable  $z_i'$  in the estimator takes the form

$$
z _ {i} ^ {\prime} := c _ {q, p} \cdot \prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} (\langle a _ {j}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q}.
$$

by lemma 4.4,  $z_{i}$  and  $z_{i}^{\prime}$  are unbiased estimators for  $f_{p}(v + u)$  and  $f_{p}(v)$  respectively, so that  $z_{i} - z_{i}^{\prime}$  is an unbiased estimator for  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v)$ ,

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ z _ {i} - z _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right] = f _ {p} (v + u) - f _ {p} (v).
$$

we can also expand  $z_{i} - z_{i}^{\prime}$  so that

$$
z _ {i} - z _ {i} ^ {\prime} = c _ {q, p} \cdot (t _ {i} - 1) \cdot \prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} (\langle a _ {j}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q}.
$$

to bound the variance of  $z_{i} - z_{i}^{\prime}$ , we first require the following structural inequality to handle the terms inside of the product of  $t_{i}$ :

claim 4.6. for  $a, b \geq 0$  and  $p \leq 2$ , we have  $(a + b)^{p / q} \leq a^{p / q} + 2b^{p / q}$ .

proof. for  $0 \leq a \leq b$  and  $p \leq 2 \leq q$ , we have

$$
(a + b) ^ {p / q} \leq (2 b) ^ {p / q} \leq 2 b ^ {p / q} \leq a ^ {p / q} + 2 b ^ {p / q}.
$$

for  $0 \leq b \leq a$  and  $p \leq 2 \leq q$ , we have from bernoulli's inequality that

$$
(a + b) ^ {p / q} = a ^ {p / q} \left(1 + \frac {b}{a}\right) ^ {p / q} \leq a ^ {p / q} \left(1 + \frac {p b}{q a}\right) \leq a ^ {p / q} + b ^ {p / q}.
$$

![](images/fa1a37c3452256017a9af33d6ed3563e987585544faf3f760e9983c6cc039ecb.jpg)

by claim 4.6, we have

$$
t _ {i} \leq \prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} \left(1 + \left(\frac {\langle a _ {j} , u \rangle}{\langle a _ {j} , v \rangle}\right) ^ {p / q}\right).
$$

hence,  $t_{i} - 1$  is a sum of  $2^{q} - 1$  terms and similarly,  $z_{i} - z_{i}^{\prime}$  is a sum of  $2^{q} - 1$  product of  $q$  terms, each with at least one term  $(\langle a_j,u\rangle)^{p / q}$ . for example, one term in  $z_{1} - z_{1}^{\prime}$  is

$$
c _ {q, p} (\langle a _ {1}, u \rangle) ^ {p / q} (\langle a _ {2}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q} (\langle a _ {3}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q} \dots (\langle a _ {q}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q},
$$

while another term is

$$
c _ {q, p} (\langle a _ {1}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q} (\langle a _ {2}, u \rangle) ^ {p / q} (\langle a _ {3}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q} \dots (\langle a _ {q}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q}.
$$

by lemma 4.4, each of these terms has second moment at most  $(\xi^2 - 1)\gamma^{2/p} \| v \|_p^{2p}$  for  $\xi = \frac{c_{q,p}}{c_{q,p}}$ . thus the sum of the  $2^q - 1$  terms has second moment at most  $(2^q)^2(\xi^2 - 1)\gamma^{2/p} \| v \|_p^{2p}$ , so that the variance of the sum is at most  $\mathcal{o}\left(2^{2q}\gamma^{2/p} \| v \|_p^{2p}\right)$ . that is,

$$
\operatorname {v a r} (z _ {i} - z _ {i} ^ {\prime}) \leq 2 ^ {2 q} \gamma^ {2 / p} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2 p} = 2 ^ {2 q} \gamma^ {2 / p} (f _ {p} (v)) ^ {2}.
$$

![](images/7ddb0ff0bab19031aa304281c7710a42d7b06989c45e7719df922815c96c2a16.jpg)

corollary 4.7 (pointwise  $f_{p}$  difference estimator for  $0 < p < 2$ ). let  $p \in (0,2)$ ,  $\delta, \varepsilon \in (0,1)$ , and  $s$  be an oblivious stream on which  $v$  is the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_1$  to  $t_2$  and let  $u$  is the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_2$ . suppose  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(u) \leq \gamma f_{p}(v)$  and  $f_{p}(u) \leq \gamma f_{p}(v)$ . then there exists an algorithm that uses a sketch of dimension  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2/p}}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)$  and outputs an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f_{p}(v)$  approximation to  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(u)$  with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ .

proof. for each  $i \in [d/q]$ , with  $z_i$ , and  $z_i'$  defined in figure 7, we have from lemma 4.5 that  $\mathbb{e}[z_i - z_i'] = f_p(v + u) - f_p(u)$  and

$$
\operatorname {v a r} \left(z _ {i} - z _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) \leq 2 ^ {2 q} \gamma^ {2 / p} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2 p} = 2 ^ {2 q} \gamma^ {2 / p} \left(f _ {p} (v)\right) ^ {2}.
$$

thus by chebyshev's inequality, the arithmetic mean of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{2^{2q}\gamma^{2/p}}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  differences  $z_i - z_i'$  suffice to obtain additive  $\varepsilon f_p(v)$  error of the difference  $f_p(v + u) - f_p(v)$  with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$ . the probability of success can then be boosted to  $1 - \delta$  by taking the median of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$  such instances.

to obtain the strong tracking property and avoid extra log  $n$  factors, this argument needs to be interleaved with carefully chosen values of  $w_{t}$  and bounding the supremum of  $(\langle a_j,w_t\rangle)^{p / q}$  across all values of  $w_{t}$ , rather than considering each inner product separately and taking a union bound. we split the stream into  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{16q / p^2}}\right)$  times  $u_{1},u_{2},\ldots$  between which the difference estimator increases by roughly  $\varepsilon^{16q / p^2}\cdot f_p(v)$  and apply a union bound to argue correctness at these times  $\{r_i\}$ , incurring a  $\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}$  term. to bound the difference estimator between times  $u_{i}$  and  $u_{i + 1}$  for a fixed  $i$ , we note that the difference estimator only increases by roughly  $\varepsilon^{16q / p^2}\cdot f_p(v)$  from  $u_{i}$  to  $u_{i + 1}$ , so that we still obtain a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f_{p}(v + w_{t}) - f_{p}(v)$  with a  $\varepsilon^{1 - 16q / p^2}$ -approximation to the difference, for a frequency vector  $w_{t}$  induced by updates between  $u_{i}$  and  $u_{i + 1}$ . we then bound the probability that the supremum of the error between times  $u_{i}$  and  $u_{i + 1}$  is bounded by  $\varepsilon^{1 - 16q / p}$  by applying chaining results from [bciw16, bci+17, bdn17] that bound the supremum of a random process.

we first require the following structural property that bounds the supremum of the inner product of a random process with a vector of independent  $p$ -stable random variables.

lemma 4.8. [bdn17] let  $x^{(1)}, x^{(2)}, \ldots, x^{(m)} \in \mathbb{r}^n$  satisfy  $0 \preceq x^{(1)} \preceq \ldots \preceq x^{(m)}$ . let  $z \in \mathbb{r}^n$  be a vector of entries that are i.i.d. sampled from the  $p$ -stable random distribution  $\mathcal{d}_p$ . then for some constant  $c_p$  depending only on  $p$ , we have

$$
\mathbf {p r} \left[ \sup _ {k \leq m} | \langle z, x ^ {(k)} \rangle | \geq \lambda \| x ^ {(m)} \| _ {p} \right] \leq c _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\lambda^ {2 p / (2 + p)}} + n ^ {- 1 / p}\right).
$$

lemma 4.9 ( $f_p$  difference estimator for  $0 < p < 2$ ). for  $0 < p < 2$ , it suffices to use a sketching matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times n}$  of i.i.d. entries drawn from the  $p$ -stable distribution  $\mathcal{d}_p$ , with dimension

$$
d = \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma^ {2 / p}}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}\right)\right)
$$

to obtain a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f_{p}$ .

proof. for an oblivious stream  $s$ , let  $v$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_1$  to  $t_2$  and let  $u_i$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_2 + 1$  to the last time when

$$
\max (f _ {p} (u _ {i}), f _ {p} (v + u _ {1} + \ldots + u _ {i}) - f _ {p} (v + u _ {1} + \ldots + u _ {i - 1})) \leq \frac {1}{2 ^ {q ^ {2}}} \varepsilon^ {1 6 q / p ^ {2}} \cdot f _ {p} (v),
$$

for some fixed constant  $q \geq 3$ . we first show that our difference estimator gives an  $\varepsilon \cdot f_p(v)$  approximation to  $f_p(v + u_i) - f_p(v)$  for each  $i = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{16q / p^2}}\right)$ . let  $\mathcal{e}$  be the event that we obtain a  $(1 + \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon))$ -approximation to both  $f_p(v + u_i)$  and  $f_p(v)$  for all  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{16q / p^2}}\right)$  vectors  $u_i$ . by corollary 4.7, we have  $\mathbf{pr}[\mathcal{e}] \geq 1 - \frac{\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,\delta)}{2}$  for an instance that uses  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2/p}}{\varepsilon^2} \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta}\right)$  rows in the sketching matrix  $a$  whose entries are i.i.d. sampled from the  $p$ -stable distribution  $d_p$ . we thus condition on the event  $\mathcal{e}$  and assume we have additive  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon) \cdot f(v)$  approximations at all times  $u_i$ . let  $w_t$  be a frequency vector induced by updates from time  $u_i$  and  $u_{i+1}$ , so that  $f_p(w_t) \leq \frac{1}{2^{q^2}} \varepsilon^{16q / p^2} \cdot f_p(v)$ . it then suffices to show that the output of the difference estimator of  $f_p(v + w_t) - f_p(v)$  at most

$\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon) \cdot f_p(v)$  for all values of  $w_t$  over the stream, since such a result proves that the difference estimator changes by at most  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon) \cdot f_p(v)$  between times  $u_i$  and  $u_{i+1}$  and conditioning on  $\mathcal{e}$ , we already have additive  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon) \cdot f(v)$  approximations at all times  $u_i$ . the claim would then follow by monotonicity of  $f_p$ .

we use the same computation as in corollary 4.7 to reduce the differences of the geometric means. that is, our difference estimator first takes a geometric mean  $z_{i}$  of  $q$  separate inner products, where  $i \in \left[\frac{d}{q}\right]$ , formed from the entries of  $a(v + w_t)$ . explicitly, each variable  $z_{i}$  in the estimator takes the form

$$
\begin{array}{l} z _ {i} := c _ {q, p} \cdot \prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} (\langle a _ {j}, v + w _ {t} \rangle) ^ {p / q} \\ = c _ {q, p} \cdot \left(\prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} \left(1 + \frac {\langle a _ {j} , w _ {t} \rangle}{\langle a _ {j} , v \rangle}\right) ^ {p / q}\right) \cdot \left(\prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} (\langle a _ {j}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $a_{j}$  is the  $j$ -th column of  $a$ . similarly, each variable  $z_{i}^{\prime}$  in the estimator formed from the entries of  $av$  takes the form

$$
z _ {i} ^ {\prime} := c _ {q, p} \cdot \prod_ {j = q (i - 1) + 1} ^ {q i} (\langle a _ {j}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q}.
$$

as in corollary 4.7 we can write  $z_{i} - z_{i}^{\prime}$  as a sum of  $2^{q} - 1$  geometric means of  $q$  terms, each with at least one term  $(\langle a_j, w_t \rangle)^{p/q}$ , e.g., one term in  $z_{1} - z_{1}^{\prime}$  is

$$
c _ {q, p} (\langle a _ {1}, w _ {t} \rangle) ^ {p / q} (\langle a _ {2}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q} (\langle a _ {3}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q} \dots (\langle a _ {q}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q},
$$

while another term is

$$
c _ {q, p} (\langle a _ {1}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q} (\langle a _ {2}, w _ {t} \rangle) ^ {p / q} (\langle a _ {3}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q} \dots (\langle a _ {q}, v \rangle) ^ {p / q}.
$$

thus by lemma 4.8 with  $\lambda = \frac{1}{\varepsilon^{8 / p}}$ , we have

$$
\pr \left[ \sup  _ {t \leq t _ {2}} | \langle a _ {i}, w _ {t} \rangle | \geq \lambda \| w _ {t} \| _ {p} \right] \leq c _ {p} \left(\varepsilon^ {1 6 / (2 + p)} + n ^ {- 1 / p}\right)
$$

and similarly

$$
\pr \left[ \sup  _ {t \leq t _ {2}} | \langle a _ {i}, v \rangle | \geq \lambda \| v \| _ {p} \right] \leq c _ {p} \left(\varepsilon^ {1 6 / (2 + p)} + n ^ {- 1 / p}\right).
$$

thus with probability at least  $1 - \mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^4\right)$ , none of the  $2^q - 1$  terms exceeds

$$
\lambda^ {p} \| w _ {t} \| _ {p} ^ {p / q} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {(q - 1) p / q} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {8}} \| w _ {t} \| _ {p} ^ {p / q} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {(q - 1) p / q} \leq \frac {\varepsilon^ {8}}{2 ^ {q}} \cdot f _ {p} (v),
$$
