since  $f_{p}(w_{t}) \leq \frac{1}{2^{q^{2}}} \varepsilon^{64q / p^{2}} \cdot f_{p}(v)$ . hence, the sum of the  $2^{q} - 1$  terms is at most  $\varepsilon^8 \cdot f(v)$ , with probability at least  $1 - \mathcal{o}\left(2^{q} \varepsilon^{4}\right)$ . in other words, some particular difference of geometric means is at most  $\varepsilon^8 \cdot f(v)$ , with probability at least  $1 - \mathcal{o}\left(2^{q} \varepsilon^{4}\right)$ . taking a union bound over  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  geometric

means, then the difference estimator  $f_{p}(v + w_{t}) - f_{p}(v)$  outputs a value that is at most  $\varepsilon^8 \cdot f(v)$ , with constant probability, for sufficiently small  $\varepsilon$ . finally, if we take the median of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$  such estimators, then we can increase the probability of success to at least  $1 - \delta$ . as previously noted, this shows that the difference estimator provides strong tracking over the entire stream.

because each entry takes  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  bits of space, then for constant  $q$  and a sketch with  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2/p}}{\varepsilon^2} \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$  rows, the total space required is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma^ {2 / p} \log n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}\right)\right),
$$

since we require a rescaling of  $\delta' = \frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}$  to union bound over the poly  $\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  times  $\{u_i\}$ .

derandomization of  $p$ -stable random variables. to handle the generation and storage of the entries of  $a$  drawn i.i.d. from the  $p$ -stable distribution  $\mathcal{d}_p$ , we first note the following derandomization.

lemma 4.10 (lemma 8 in [jw18]). let  $\mathcal{a}$  be any streaming algorithm that stores only a linear sketch  $a\cdot f$  on a stream vector  $f\in \{-m,\dots ,m\} ^n$  for some  $m = \mathrm{poly}(n)$ , such that the entries of  $a\in \mathbb{r}^{k\times n}$  are i.i.d., and can be sampled using  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  bits. then for any fixed constant  $c\geq 1$ ,  $\mathcal{a}$  can be implemented using a random matrix  $a^{\prime}$  using  $\mathcal{o}\left(k\log n(\log \log n)^2\right)$  bits of space, such that for all  $y\in \mathbb{r}^k$  with entry-wise bit complexity of  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$ ,

$$
\left| \mathbf {p r} [ a \cdot f = y ] - \mathbf {p r} [ a ^ {\prime} \cdot f = y ] \right| <   n ^ {- c k}.
$$

we cannot immediately apply lemma 4.10 because our difference estimator actually stores  $a \cdot (v + w_t)$  and  $a \cdot v$  and requires the entries of  $a$  to be i.i.d. we thus require the following generalization:

corollary 4.11. for a constant  $q \geq 1$ , let  $f_1, \ldots, f_q \in \{-m, \ldots, m\}^n$  for some  $m = \mathrm{poly}(n)$  be vectors defined by a stream so that  $f_i$  is defined by the updates of the stream between given times  $t_{i,1}$  and  $t_{i,2}$ , for each  $i \in [q]$ . let  $\mathcal{a}$  be any streaming algorithm that stores linear sketches  $a \cdot f_1, \ldots, a \cdot f_q$ , such that the entries of  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{k \times n}$  are i.i.d. that can be sampled using  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  bits, and outputs  $g(a \cdot f_1, \ldots, a \cdot f_q)$  for some composition function  $g: \mathbb{r}^q \to \mathbb{r}$ . then for any fixed constant  $c \geq 1$ ,  $\mathcal{a}$  can be implemented using a random matrix  $a'$  using  $\mathcal{o}\left(k\log n(\log \log n)^2\right)$  bits of space, such that for all  $y \in \mathbb{r}^k$  with entry-wise bit complexity of  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$ ,

$$
\left| \mathbf {p r} \left[ g (a \cdot f _ {1}, \dots , a \cdot f _ {q}) = y \right] - \mathbf {p r} \left[ g (a ^ {\prime} \cdot f _ {1}, \dots , a ^ {\prime} \cdot f _ {q}) = y \right] \right| <   n ^ {- c k}.
$$

proof. we first assume without loss of generality that all entries of  $a \cdot f_i$  are integers bounded by  $\mathrm{poly}(n)$  for each  $i \in [q]$ , due to the bit complexity of  $a$  and  $f_i$ . let  $w_i = a \cdot f_i$  for each  $i \in [q]$  and suppose the maximum entry, denoted  $\|a\|_{\infty}$ , satisfies  $\|a\|_{\infty} \leq n^{\alpha}$  for some constant  $\alpha$ . let  $n = m \cdot n^{\alpha}$  so that  $\|a \cdot f_i\|_{\infty} \leq n$  for all  $i \in [q]$ . define the vector  $v = \sum_{i=1}^{q} n^{2i} w_i$  so that  $\|v\|_{\infty} < n'$  for  $n' = n^{3q}$ . note that  $n' = \mathrm{poly}(n)$  so that all entries of  $v$  can be stored in  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  bits. moreover, observe that each coordinate  $v_j$  with  $j \in [n]$  has a unique  $n^2$ -ary representation. thus if  $v_j = \sum_{i=1}^{q} n^{2i} w_{i,j}$  where  $w_{i,j}$  represents the  $j$ -th coordinate of vector  $w_i$ , then the system  $v_j = \sum_{i=1}^{q} n^{2i} \alpha_i$  constrained to the condition that  $|\alpha_i| \leq n$  for all  $i \in [q]$  has a unique solution. hence, the vectors  $w_i = a \cdot f_i$  can be computed from the vector  $v$ .

by lemma 4.10, we can use a random matrix  $a'$  with  $\mathcal{o}\left(k\log n(\log \log n)^2\right)$  bits of space and have for all  $y \in \mathbb{r}^k$  with entry-wise bit complexity of  $o(\log n)$ , that:

$$
\left| \mathbf {p r} \left[ a \cdot v = y \right] - \mathbf {p r} \left[ a ^ {\prime} \cdot v = y \right] \right| <   n ^ {- c k}.
$$

since any  $\mathcal{a}$  that computes  $a\cdot v$  can compute the vectors  $a\cdot f_{1},\ldots ,a\cdot f_{q}$  by the above argument,  $\mathcal{a}$  can then compute the composition  $g(a\cdot f_1,\dots ,a\cdot f_q)$ . hence it follows that

$$
\left| \mathbf {p r} \left[ g (a \cdot f _ {1}, \ldots , a \cdot f _ {q}) = y \right] - \mathbf {p r} \left[ g (a ^ {\prime} \cdot f _ {1}, \ldots , a ^ {\prime} \cdot f _ {q}) \right] \right| <   n ^ {- c k}.
$$

we now argue that the pseudorandom generator of corollary 4.11 suffices to derandomize the correctness guarantees of our difference estimator.

lemma 4.12 ( $f_p$  difference estimator for  $0 < p < 2$ ). for  $0 < p < 2$ , there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f_p$  that uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma^ {2 / p} \log n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} (\log \log n) ^ {2} \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}\right)\right).
$$

proof. the difference estimator for  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v)$  is given an input splitting time  $t_1$ . we then need to argue correctness over all possible stopping times  $t$  with  $t > t_1$ , such that  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v) \leq \gamma f_{p}(v)$  for  $p < 1$  or  $f_{p}(u) \leq \gamma f_{p}(v)$  for  $p \geq 1$ . by corollary 4.11 applied with  $q = 2$ , there exists a pseudorandom generator that succeeds with high probability  $1 - \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$  over a specific value of  $t$ . since the stream has length  $m = \mathrm{poly}(n)$ , by taking a union bound over all  $\mathrm{poly}(n)$  choice of the stopping times  $t$ , we have that the pseudorandom generator is also correct with high probability over all possible stopping times  $t$ . thus from lemma 4.9, we obtain the following guarantees of our difference estimator.

in particular, we remark that the guarantees of the difference estimator only require that the marginal distribution is correct at all times, rather than the joint distribution is correct. recall that the difference estimator is used to choose a final stopping time for the purposes of the framework in theorem 2.5 when its output is sufficiently large. specifically, the framework takes a set of outputs  $s_{t_1 + 1}, \ldots, s_t$  from the difference estimator over the course of the stream and chooses a final stopping time  $t_2$  based on the first output that exceeds a certain threshold  $t$ . we emphasize that our argument does not rely on fooling the final stopping time  $t_2$  chosen by the framework, since it requires a conditional statement on the set  $s_{t_1 + 1}, \ldots, s_t$  of outputs from the difference estimator not exceeding the threshold  $t$ .

for example, consider a scenario where the probability over the choice of independent  $p$ -stable random variables that  $s_{t_1 + 1}$  exceeds  $t$  is  $\frac{1}{2}$  and the probability that  $s_{t_1 + 2}$  exceeds  $t$  is  $\frac{1}{2}$ , i.e.,  $\mathbf{pr}[s_{t_1 + 1} \geq t] = \frac{1}{2}$  and  $\mathbf{pr}[s_{t_1 + 2} \geq t] = \frac{1}{2}$ . however, suppose that conditioned on the event that  $s_{t_1 + 1} < t$ , we have that  $s_{t_1 + 2}$  cannot exceed  $t$ , so that  $\mathbf{pr}[s_{t_1 + 2} \geq t | s_{t_1 + 1} < t] = 0$ . then since theorem 2.5 chooses the first time that exceeds  $t$ , the framework can never choose  $t_1 + 2$ , i.e.,  $\mathbf{pr}[t_2 = t_1 + 2] = 0$ . since our derandomization does not use independent  $p$ -stable random variables and only fools the marginal probabilities (of a pair of times  $(t, t_1)$  for each  $t > t_1$ ), we need not fool the joint distribution, and thus not fool the choice of stopping time. hence, it could be that theorem 2.5 using our derandomized difference estimator selects  $t_2 = t_1 + 2$ . on the other

hand,  $t_1 + 2$  is still a valid stopping time if the difference estimator is correct at time  $t_1 + 1$ , and the output does not exceed  $t$ .

in other words, while the geometric mean estimator can be fooled by fooling a constant a number of half-space queries, we may need to condition on  $\mathrm{poly}(n)$  intermediate stream positions. instead, we note that the derandomization of our difference estimator provides the same correctness guarantees. thus, even though the difference estimator with the pseudorandom generator could induce a different distribution on the final stopping time, the final stopping time  $t_2'$  output by our derandomized algorithm and the algorithm itself can be used in our framework because  $t_2'$  corresponds to the first output of the algorithm that exceeds the threshold  $t$ . we also have correctness at all intermediate times between  $t_1$  and  $t_2'$ .

bit complexity and rounding of  $p$ -stable random variables. we remark that for each inner product in lemma 4.12 to be stored in  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  bits of space, then each randomly generated  $p$ -stable random variable must also be rounded to  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  bits of precision. due to the rounding, that each summand changes additively by  $\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ , so that the total estimate also changes by an additive  $\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ , so that the total error for the estimate of  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v)$  is  $\varepsilon \cdot f_{p}(v) + \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ . the additive  $\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$  can be absorbed into the  $\varepsilon \cdot f(v)$  term with a rescaling of  $\varepsilon$  unless  $f_{p}(v) = 0$ . however, in the case  $f_{p}(v) = 0$ , then our estimator will output 0 anyway, so that the rounding of the  $p$ -stable random variables still gives an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f(v)$ .

# 4.2  $f_{p}$  estimation algorithm

we now give an adversarially robust streaming algorithm for  $f_{p}$  moment estimation with  $p \in (0,2)$  by using theorem 2.5.

theorem 4.13. given  $\varepsilon >0$  and  $p\in (0,2)$ , there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation for  $f_{p}$  that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\log^2 n(\log \log n)^2\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} +\log \log n\right)\right)$  bits of space and succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$ .

proof. for  $p \in (0,2)$ ,  $f_{p}$  is a monotonic function with  $(\varepsilon ,m)$ -twist number  $\lambda = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)$ , by observation 1.12. by lemma 4.12 and theorem 4.2, there exists a  $(\gamma ,\varepsilon ,\delta)$ -difference estimator that uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma^ {2 / p} \log n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} (\log \log n) ^ {2} \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}\right)\right)
$$

and an oblivious strong tracker pstable for  $f_{p}$  that uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\log n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \left(\log \log n + \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}\right)\right).
$$

therefore by using the  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator and the oblivious strong tracker for  $f_{p}$  in the framework of algorithm 1, then consider applying theorem 2.5 with parameters  $c = 2 / p \geq 1$ ,  $s_{1}(n, \delta, \varepsilon) = \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n (\log \log n)^{2} \left( \log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta} \right)$ , and  $s_{2} = 0$ . namely, theorem 2.5 gives an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  for  $f_{p}$  that succeeds with constant probability and uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {2} n (\log \log n) ^ {2} \left(\log \log n + \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).
$$

![](images/f1c5b603ffb8a95d642ffcd154f4f8b38d94aff3af0c01fcfce6739f62465021.jpg)

optimized  $f_{p}$  algorithm. to improve the space dependency, we use the same technique as in section 3. that is, recall that the counter  $a$  in algorithm 1 tracks the active instances  $\mathcal{a}_{a}$  and  $\mathcal{b}_{a,c}$  output by the algorithm. then we only maintain the most sketches  $\mathcal{a}_{i}$  and  $\mathcal{b}_{i,c}$  for the smallest  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  values of  $i$  that are at least the value of the counter  $a$ , instead of maintaining  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  total sketches. because the output increases by a factor of 2 each time  $a$  increases, any larger index will have only missed  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon)$  fraction of the  $f_{p}$  of the stream and thus still output a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation.

theorem 4.14 (adversarially robust  $f_{p}$  streaming algorithm for  $p \in (0,2)$ ). given  $\varepsilon > 0$  and  $p \in (0,2)$ , there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  for  $f_{p}$  that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}} \log n (\log \log n)^{2} \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \left( \log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right)\right)$  bits of space and succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$ .

proof. using the above optimization, there are simultaneously  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active indices  $a$  and  $\mathcal{o}(1)$  active indices  $c$  corresponding to sketches  $\mathcal{a}_a$  and  $\mathcal{b}_{a,c}$ . recall from theorem 2.5 that for fixed  $a$  and  $c > 1$ , the total space for each  $\mathcal{b}_{a,j}$  across the  $\beta$  granularities is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \cdot s _ {1} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {2} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon)\right),
$$

where for our purposes,  $s_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon) = \log n(\log \log n)^{2}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} +\log \frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}} +\log \log n\right)$  and  $s_{2} = 0$  for our  $f_{p}$  strong tracker and  $f_{p}$  difference estimator. since there are  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  total indices  $a$  over the course of the stream, then each sketch must have failure probability  $\frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}\left(\log n,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}$  for the entire algorithm to have failure probability  $\delta = \frac{2}{3}$ . however, since we maintain at most  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  simultaneous active values of  $a$ , then the  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n(\log \log n)^{2}\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)$  bits of space are required to perform the sketching stitching and granularity changing framework. since there are  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active indices of  $a$ , consisting of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  subroutines, then it takes  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  additional bits of space to store the splitting times for each of the  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active subroutines across a stream of length  $m$ , with  $\log m = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ . hence, the total space required is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n(\log \log n)^{2}\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)$ .

similarly, we have:

theorem 4.15 (adversarily robust  $f_{p}$  streaming algorithm for  $p \in (0,1)$ ). given  $\varepsilon > 0$  and  $p \in (0,1]$ , there exists an adversarily robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  for  $f_{p}$  that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}} \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} (\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}) + \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n\right)$  bits of space and succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$ .

finally, we remark that our analysis for lemma 4.12 can be repeated to show that li's geometric mean estimator provides strong  $l_{p}$  tracking for  $p \in (0,2)$ . namely by first union bounding over all  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  times in the stream where the value of  $f_{p}$  on the underlying frequency vector induced by the stream roughly doubles, we again reduce the problem to maintaining an accurate estimation of  $f_{p}$  between two times in which the value of  $f_{p}$  roughly doubles. we can subsequently apply the techniques of lemma 4.9 by further breaking down the stream into times when the value of  $f_{p}$  increases by a factor of  $(1 + \mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon))$  and bounding the same terms  $z_{i}$ , as defined in figure 7. this analysis incurs an extra factor of  $\log \log n$  due to an initial union bound over poly  $(\log n, \frac{1}{\varepsilon})$  times in the stream rather than poly  $\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ . hence, the space required to implement li's geometric mean

estimator as a strong  $l_{p}$  tracker in the random oracle model is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)$ , which matches the strong  $l_{p}$  tracker of [bdn17]. to derandomize the algorithm, we incur an additional  $(\log \log n)^2$  factor due to corollary 4.11.

theorem 4.16. for  $p \in (0,2)$ , there exists a one-pass streaming algorithm that uses total space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log n (\log \log n)^2 \left( \log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta} \right) \right)$  bits and provides  $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ -strong tracking for the  $f_p$  moment estimation problem.

proof. consider an instance of li's geometric mean estimator with  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log \frac{1}{\delta'}\right)\right)$  rows. we define a sequence of times  $t_1, t_2, \ldots, t_w$  throughout an oblivious stream  $s$  so that each time  $t_i$  is implicitly defined as the last time that  $f_p(1:t_i) \leq 2^{i-1}$  and thus  $w = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ . for each  $i \in [w]$ , we further consider a sequence of times  $t_{i,1}, t_{i,2}, \ldots$  so that time  $t_{i,j}$  is defined as the last time  $t_i$  such that

$$
\max (f _ {p} (t _ {i, j - 1} + 1: t), f _ {p} (1: t) - f _ {p} (1: t _ {i, j - 1})) \leq \frac {1}{2 ^ {q ^ {2}}} \varepsilon^ {1 6 q / p ^ {2}} \cdot f _ {p} (1: t _ {i}).
$$

let  $\mathcal{e}_1$  be the event that li's geometric mean estimator gives a  $(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{2})$ -approximation to  $f_p(1: t_i)$  for all  $i \in [w]$  and let  $\mathcal{e}_2$  be the event that li's geometric mean estimator gives a  $(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{2})$ -approximation to  $f_p(1: t_{i,j})$  for all  $t_{i,j}$ . since there are poly  $\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$  times  $\{t_i\} \cup \{t_{i,j}\}$ , then we require  $\delta' = \delta/\text{poly} \left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$  to obtain success probability  $1 - \delta$  after taking a union bound over poly  $\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$  times. by lemma 4.4, the arithmetic mean of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  variables  $z_i$  achieves a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f_p(1: t)$  with constant probability for a fixed time  $t$ . thus by taking the median of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$  such means and taking a union bound, we have that  $\text{pr}\left[\mathcal{e}_1 \wedge \mathcal{e}_2\right] \geq 1 - \frac{\delta}{2}$ .

for times  $a, b \in [m]$ , let  $\mathcal{l}(a : b)$  be the output of li's geometric mean estimator for the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $\mathcal{s}$  from times  $a$  to  $b$ . from the chaining argument in lemma 4.9 and a sketching matrix with dimension  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \left(\log \frac{1}{\delta'}\right)\right)$  rows, we have that for a fixed  $i \in [w]$ , there does not exist  $t \in [t_{i,j} + 1, t_{i,j+1}]$  such that  $|\mathcal{l}(1:t) - \mathcal{l}(1:t_{i,j} + 1)| \geq \frac{\varepsilon}{2} f_p(1:t_{i,j})$ , with probability at least  $1 - \delta'$ . hence conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}_1$  and  $\mathcal{e}_2$ ,  $\mathcal{l}(1:t)$  outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  approximation to  $f_p(1:t)$  for all times  $t \in [t_{i,j} + 1, t_{i,j+1}]$ , with probability at least  $1 - \delta'$ . we again take a union bound over poly  $\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$  times  $\{t_i\} \cup \{t_{i,j}\}$ , so that setting  $\delta' = \delta/\text{poly} \left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$  implies that the  $\mathcal{l}(1:t)$  simultaneously gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to all  $f_p(1:t)$  with probability  $1 - \delta$ . hence, the total number of rows required in  $d$  is  $d = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \left(\log \frac{1}{\delta'}\right)\right)$ , which translates to  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \left(\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)$  total rows. each entry in the sketch uses  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  bits of space to store. moreover, we incur an additional  $(\log \log n)^2$  factor due to corollary 4.11 to derandomize the algorithm. therefore, the total space in bits is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log n (\log \log n) ^ {2} \left(\log \log n + \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}\right)\right).
$$

![](images/7632da699d0f4348cd470c76e00e49c8dd10be6abcb30d964af84e68aaee569b.jpg)

applications to entropy estimation. finally, we give an application to adversarially robust entropy estimation, similar to theorem 8.15.

theorem 4.17 (adversarily robust entropy streaming algorithm). given  $\varepsilon >0$ , there exists an adversarily robust streaming algorithm that outputs an additive  $\varepsilon$ -approximation to shannon entropy and uses  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\log^3 n\right)$  bits of space and succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$ .

proof. by observation 8.13 and lemma 8.14, it suffices to obtain adversarially robust  $(1 + \varepsilon')$ -approximation algorithms to  $f_{y_i}(v)$  for all  $y_i \in (0,2)$  in the set  $\{y_0,\dots,y_k\}$ , where  $k = \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log m$  and  $\varepsilon' = \frac{\varepsilon}{12(k+1)^3\log m}$ . by theorem 4.14 with accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon'$ , we can obtain such robust algorithms approximating each  $f_{y_i}(v)$ , using space

$$
\tilde {\mathcal {o}} \left(\frac {1}{(\varepsilon^ {\prime}) ^ {2}} \log n (\log \log n) ^ {2} \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \left(\log \log n + \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).
$$

moreover, the failure probability of each algorithm is  $1 - \mathrm{poly}\left(\varepsilon ,\frac{1}{\log n}\right)$ , due to the rescaling of the failure probability  $\delta^{\prime} = \frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)}$  in both lemma 4.12 and theorem 4.2. hence for  $\varepsilon^{\prime} = \frac{\varepsilon}{12(k + 1)^{3}\log m}$  and  $\log m = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ , each of the  $\mathcal{o}(k)$  algorithms use space  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\log^3 n\right)$ . since  $k = \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} +\log \log m$ , the overall space complexity follows.

# 5 robust  $f_{p}$  estimation for integer  $p > 2$

in this section, we use the framework of section 2 to give an adversarially robust streaming algorithm for  $f_{p}$  moment estimation where  $p > 2$  is an integer. we again require both an  $f_{p}$  strong tracker and an  $f_{p}$  difference estimator to use theorem 2.5. recall that for integer  $p > 2$ , the dominant space factor is  $n^{1 - 2 / p}$  and thus we will not try to optimize the log  $n$  factors. hence we obtain an  $f_{p}$  strong tracker by adapting an  $f_{p}$  streaming algorithm and a union bound over  $m$  points in the stream. the main challenge of the section is to develop the  $f_{p}$  difference estimator, since we have the following  $f_{p}$  strong tracker:

theorem 5.1 ([gan11], theorem 22 in [gw18b]). for integer  $p > 2$ , there exists an insertion-only streaming algorithm ghss(1, t, ε, δ) that uses o  $\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2/p} \log^2 n \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$  bits of space that gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to the  $f_p$  moment.

by setting  $\delta' = \frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ , we can apply theorem 5.1 across all  $m = \mathrm{poly}(n)$  times on a stream, thus obtaining a strong tracker.

theorem 5.2 (oblivious  $f_{p}$  strong tracking for integer  $p > 2$ ). [gan11, gw18b] for integer  $p > 2$ , there exists an insertion-only streaming algorithm  $\mathrm{ghss}(1,t,\varepsilon ,\delta)$  that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\log \frac{n}{\delta}\log^2 n\right)$  bits of space and provides  $(\varepsilon ,\delta)$ -strong  $f_{p}$  tracking.

to acquire intuition for our  $f_{p}$  estimation algorithm, we first recall the following definition of perfect  $l_{p}$  sampling.

definition 5.3 ( $l_{p}$  sampling). let  $f \in \mathbb{r}^{n}$  and failure probability  $\delta \in (0,1)$ . a perfect  $l_{p}$  sampler is an algorithm that either outputs a failure symbol  $\perp$  with probability at most  $\delta$  or an index  $i \in [n]$  such that for each  $j \in [n]$ ,

$$
\mathbf {p r} [ i = j ] = \frac {u _ {j} ^ {p}}{f _ {p} (u)} + \mathcal {o} \left(n ^ {- c}\right),
$$

for some arbitrarily large constant  $c \geq 1$ .

we use the expansion  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v) = \sum_{k = 1}^{p}\binom{p}{k}\langle v^{k},u^{p - k}\rangle$  for our difference estimator for  $f_{p}$  moment estimation for integer  $p > 2$ , where  $u^k$  denotes the coordinate-wise  $k$ -th power of  $u$ .

suppose we use a perfect  $l_{p}$  sampler to sample a coordinate  $a \in [n]$  with probability  $\frac{v_{a}^{k}}{\|v\|_{k}^{k}}$ . we then set  $z$  to be the  $a$ -th coordinate of the frequency vector  $v^{p - k}$ . observe that  $u$  arrives completely after the splitting time denotes the end of the updates to frequency vector  $v$ . thus we can sample  $a$  at the splitting time and then explicitly compute  $z$ . we can then obtain an unbiased estimate  $y$  to  $\| v\| _k^k$  with low variance, so that the expected value of  $yz$  would be exactly  $\langle v^k,u^{p - k}\rangle$  and moreover,  $yz$  is a "good" approximation to  $\langle v^k,u^{p - k}\rangle$ .

the downfall of this approach is that it requires a perfect  $l_{p}$ -sampler for  $p > 2$ , which is not known. perfect  $l_{p}$ -samplers are known for  $p \leq 2$  [jw18], but their constructions are based on duplicating each stream update  $\mathrm{poly}(n)$  times. hence adapting these constructions to build perfect  $l_{p}$ -samplers for  $p > 2$  would be space-inefficient, since the space dependence is  $\omega (n^{1 - 2 / p})$  for  $p > 2$ , rather than  $\mathrm{polylog}(n)$  for  $p \leq 2$ . thus after duplication the space required would be  $\omega ((\mathrm{poly}(n))^{1 - 2 / p})$  rather than  $\mathrm{polylog}(\mathrm{poly}(n))$ . note that the former requires space larger than  $n$  while the latter remains  $\mathrm{polylog}(n)$ . one possible approach would be to use approximate  $l_{p}$  samplers and their variants [mw10, jst11, ako11, mrwz20], but these algorithms already have  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}$  space dependency for each instance, which prohibits using  $\omega \left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  instances to reduce the variance of each sampler. instead, we use the following perfect  $l_{2}$ -sampler of [jw18] to return a coordinate  $a \in [n]$  with probability  $\frac{v_a^2}{\|v\|_2^2} \pm \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ .

theorem 5.4 (perfect  $l_{2}$  sampler). [jw18] given failure probability  $\delta \in (0,1)$ , there exists a one-pass streaming algorithm sampler that is a perfect  $l_{2}$  sampler and uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log^{3}n\log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$  bits of space.

we also obtain unbiased estimates  $x$  and  $y$  of  $v_{a}^{k - 2}$  and  $\| v\| _2^2$ , respectively. given  $a\in [n]$ , we then track the  $a$ -th coordinate of  $u^{p - k}$  exactly. we show that the product of these terms  $x$ ,  $y$ , and  $u^{p - k}$  forms an unbiased estimate to  $\langle v^k,u^{p - k}\rangle$ . we then analyze the variance and show that taking the mean of enough repetitions gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $\langle v^k,u^{p - k}\rangle$ . by repeating the estimator for each summand in  $\sum_{k = 1}^{p}\binom {p}{k}\langle v^k,u^{p - k}\rangle$ , it follows that we obtain a  $(\gamma ,\varepsilon ,\delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f_{p}$ .

we first require the well-known countsketch algorithm for identifying heavy-hitters, which consists of a table with  $\log \frac{n}{\delta}$  rows, each consisting of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  buckets, to identify  $\varepsilon \cdot l_2$  heavy hitters. for each row, each item  $i \in [n]$  in the universe is hashed to one of the  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  buckets along with a random sign. the signed sum of all items assigned to each bucket across all rows is tracked by the data structure and the estimated frequency of each item  $i$  is the median of the values associated with each bucket that  $i$  is hashed to, across all rows.

lemma 5.5 (moment estimation of sampled items). for integer  $p > 2$  and failure probability  $\delta \in (0,1)$ , there exists a one-pass streaming algorithm that outputs an index  $i \in [n]$  with probability  $\frac{u_i^2}{f_2(u)} + \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ , as well as an unbiased estimate to  $u_i^p$  with variance  $u_i^{2p}$ . the algorithm uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log^3 n \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$  bits of space.

proof. recall that sampler first duplicates each coordinate  $u_{i}$  for  $i \in [n]$  a total number of  $n^c$  times and for each  $(i,j) \in [n] \times [n^c]$ , sampler scales  $u_{i}$  to obtain random variables  $z_{i,j} = \frac{u_j}{\sqrt{e_{i,j}}}$  for an exponential random variable  $e_{i,j}$  (the exponential random variables in [jw18] are then derandomized). this linear transformation to the frequency vector  $u \in \mathbb{r}^n$  results in a vector  $z \in \mathbb{r}^{n^{c+1}}$ , which can also be interpreted as having dimensions  $n \times n^c$ . moreover, sampler only outputs an index  $i \in [n]$  if there exists an index  $j \in [n^c]$  if  $|z_{i,j}| \geq \mathcal{o}(1)|z|_2$ . we then run an

(1) find a list  $\mathcal{h}$  that includes all  $i\in [n]$  with  $v_{i}\geq \frac{\gamma^{1 / p}}{16}\| v\|_{p}$  
(2) using countsketch, obtain an estimate  $\widehat{v_i}$  to  $v_{i}$  with additive error  $\frac{\varepsilon\gamma^{1 / p}}{64\gamma}\| v\| _p$  for each  $i\in \mathcal{h}$  and let  $h\in \mathbb{r}^n$  be the vector such that  $h_i = \widehat{v}_i$  if  $i\in \mathcal{h}$  and zero otherwise.  
(3) perform perfect  $l_{2}$  sampling on  $v - h$  to obtain a set  $\mathcal{s}$  of size  $k = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$ .  
(4) obtain an estimate  $\widehat{s_i}$  to  $v_{i} - h_{i}$  for each  $i\in s$  
(5) let  $w$  be a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $\| v - h\| _2^2$  
(6) output  $w + \sum_{k=1}^{p-1} \binom{p}{k} \left( \sum_{i \in \mathcal{h}} \widehat{v}_i^k, u_i^{p-k} + w \cdot \sum_{i \in \mathcal{s}} \widehat{s}_i^{k-2}, u_i^{p-k} \right)$ .

fig. 8:  $f_{p}$  difference estimator for  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v)$  with integer  $p > 2$ .

instance of countsketch with constant factor approximation to obtain an estimate  $\widehat{z_{i,j}}$  for the frequency of  $z_{i,j}$  and set  $\widehat{u_i} = \sqrt{e_{i,j}}\cdot \widehat{z_{i,j}}$ . recall that each estimate  $\widehat{z_{i,j}}$  of  $z_{i,j}$  is

$$
\sum s _ {i, j} \mathbf {1} _ {h (a, b) = h (i, j)} s _ {a, b} z _ {a, b},
$$

where  $s_{a,b}$  is a uniformly random sign and  $\mathbf{1}_{h(a,b) = h(i,j)}$  is the indicator variable of whether  $h(a,b) = h(i,j)$ . that is, we define  $\mathbf{1}_{h(a,b) = h(i,j)} = 1$  if  $h(a,b) = h(i,j)$  and  $\mathbf{1}_{h(a,b) = h(i,j)} = 0$  if  $h(a,b) \neq h(i,j)$ . hence,  $\widehat{z_{i,j}}$  is an unbiased estimate of  $z_{i,j}$  so that  $\widehat{u_i}$  is an unbiased estimate of  $u_i$ . moreover, the variance of  $\widehat{z_{i,j}}$  is at most  $\mathcal{o}\left(\| z\| _2^2\right) = \mathcal{o}\left(z_{i,j}^2\right)$  so that the variance of  $\widehat{u_i}$  is  $\mathcal{o}\left(u_i^2\right)$ . thus if we use  $p$  independent instances of countsketch with estimates  $\widehat{u_i}^{(1)},\ldots ,\widehat{u_i}^{(p)}$ , their product is an unbiased estimate to  $u_j^p$  with variance  $u_j^{2p}$ , as desired.

each of the  $p$  instances of countsketch with constant factor approximation uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log n\log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$  bits of space. since the perfect  $l_{2}$  sampler uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log^{3}n\log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$  bits of space by theorem 5.4, then the total space used is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log^{3}n\log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$ .

unfortunately, perfect  $l_{2}$  sampling coordinates of  $v$  alone is not enough; the variance of the resulting procedure is too high to obtain space dependency  $\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2}$ . thus we also run a subroutine that removes a set of "heavy" coordinates  $\mathcal{h}$  of  $v$  and tracks the corresponding coordinates of  $u$ . although we have the exact values of  $u_{a}$  for  $a \in \mathcal{h}$ , we still do not have exact values of  $v_{a}$ ; instead, we have estimates  $\widehat{v_a}$  for each  $v_{a}$  with  $a \in \mathcal{h}$ . setting  $h$  to be the sparse vector that contains the estimates  $\widehat{v_a}$  for each  $a \in \mathcal{h}$  and  $w \coloneqq v - h$ , our algorithm perfect  $l_{2}$  samples from  $l_{2}$  sample from. to show correctness, we decompose

$$
f _ {p} (v + u) - f _ {p} (v) = \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {h}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} {\binom {p} {i}} v _ {a} ^ {i} u _ {a} ^ {p - i} + \sum_ {a \notin \mathcal {h}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} {\binom {p} {i}} v _ {a} ^ {i} u _ {a} ^ {p - i}.
$$

the heavy-hitter subroutine allows accurate estimation to the first term and the perfect  $l_{2}$  sampling subroutines allows accurate estimation to the second term. finally, we remark that perfect  $l_{2}$  sampling incurs a term of  $v_{a}^{2}$ . thus we further need to split the estimation of the second term into the cases where  $i = 1$  and  $i > 1$ .

estimation of  $\langle v^i, u^{p - i} \rangle$  for  $i > 1$ . we first show that our difference estimator gives an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f_p(v)$  approximation to  $\sum_{a \notin \mathcal{h}} \binom{p}{i} v_a^i u_a^{p - i}$  with  $i \geq 2$

lemma 5.6. for integer  $p > 2$ , there exists an algorithm that uses space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p} \log^3 n \log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$  and outputs an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f_p(v)$  approximation to  $\sum_{a \notin \mathcal{h}} \binom{p}{i} v_a^i u_a^{p-i}$  with  $i \geq 2$ .

proof. for an oblivious stream  $s$ , let  $v$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_1$  to  $t_2$  and  $u$  be the frequency vector induced by updates from time  $t_2$  to  $t$  exclusive, with  $f_p(u) \leq \gamma \cdot f_p(v)$ . by theorem 3.10, there exists an algorithm heavyhitters that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2 - 2 / p} \log^2 n}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$  space and outputs a list  $\mathcal{h}$  of coordinates and estimates  $\widehat{v_i}$  with additive error  $\frac{\varepsilon \gamma^{1 / p}}{64\gamma} \| v \|_p$ . moreover,  $\mathcal{h}$  includes all coordinates  $i \in [n]$  such that  $v_i \geq \frac{\gamma^{1 / p}}{16} \| v \|_p$  and no coordinate  $j$  such that  $v_j \leq \frac{\gamma^{1 / p}}{32} \| v \|_p$ . let  $h$  be the vector consisting of the estimates  $\widehat{v_i}$  for each  $i \in [n]$  with  $i \in \mathcal{h}$  and 0 in the positions  $i$  for which  $i \notin \mathcal{h}$ . we define the vector  $w := v - h$ .

we use sampler to sample indices  $j_1, \ldots, j_k \in [n]$  with so that each sample is a coordinate  $a \in [n]$  with probability  $\frac{w_a^2}{\|w\|_2^2} + \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ . we obtain unbiased estimates to  $\widehat{w_{j_1}^{p-i-2}}, \ldots, \widehat{w_{j_k}^{p-i-2}}$  of  $w_{j_1}^{p-i-2}, \ldots, w_{j_k}^{p-i-2}$  through lemma 5.5. we also obtain a  $(1 + \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon))$ -approximation unbiased estimate  $w$  of  $\|w\|_2^2$ . thus for each  $b \in [k]$ , the product  $\widehat{w_{j_b}^{p-i-2}} \cdot w \cdot u_{j_b}^i$  satisfies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {w _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - i - 2}} \cdot w \cdot u _ {j _ {b}} ^ {i} \right] = \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {h}} \langle \widehat {v _ {a}} ^ {p - i}, u _ {a} ^ {i} \rangle + \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {h}} \left(\frac {w _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot w _ {a} ^ {p - i - 2} \cdot (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot u _ {a} ^ {i} \\ + \sum_ {a \in [ n ] \backslash \mathcal {h}} \left(\frac {w _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot w _ {a} ^ {p - i - 2} \cdot (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot u _ {a} ^ {i}. \\ \end{array}
$$

observe that since  $\widehat{v_a}$  is a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $v_{a}$  for  $a\in \mathcal{h}$ , then  $|w_{a}|\leq \varepsilon |v_{a}|$ , so that the second summation is at most  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon)\langle v^{p - i},u^i\rangle$ . moreover, we have  $w_{a} = v_{a}$  for  $a\in [n]\setminus \mathcal{h}$ , so that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {w _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - i - 2}} \cdot w \cdot u _ {j _ {b}} ^ {i} \right] \in (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) | \langle w ^ {p - i}, u ^ {i} \rangle | + \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (n)}.
$$

similarly, the variance is at most

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v a r} \left(\widehat {w _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - i - 2}} \cdot w \cdot u _ {j _ {b}} ^ {i}\right) \leq \sum_ {a \in [ n ]} \left(\frac {w _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot w _ {a} ^ {2 p - 2 i - 4} \cdot (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {4} \cdot u _ {a} ^ {2 i} \\ \leq \sum_ {a \in [ n ]} (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) w _ {a} ^ {2 p - 2 i - 2} \cdot \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot u _ {a} ^ {2 i} \\ \leq \sum_ {a \in [ n ]} (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) w _ {a} ^ {2 p - 4} \cdot \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot u _ {a} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

by hölder's inequality, the variance is at most

$$
(1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \sum_ {a \in [ n ]} w _ {a} ^ {2 p - 4} u _ {a} ^ {2} \leq (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \left(\sum_ {a \in [ n ]} u _ {a} ^ {p}\right) ^ {2 / p} \left(\sum_ {a \in [ n ]} w _ {a} ^ {2 p}\right) ^ {1 - 2 / p}
$$

$$
= (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot \| u \| _ {p} ^ {2} \cdot \| w \| _ {2 p} ^ {2 p - 4}.
$$

since  $(1 + \varepsilon)\| w\| _2^2\leq 2\| v\| _2^2$  for  $\varepsilon \leq 1$  and  $\| u\| _p^p\leq \gamma \| v\| _p^p$ , then the variance is at most

$$
2 \| v \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot \gamma^ {2 / p} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2} \cdot \| w \| _ {2 p} ^ {2 p - 4}.
$$

recall that the vector  $w$  is formed by removing from  $v$  the coordinates  $i \in [n]$  such that  $v_i \geq \frac{\gamma^{1/p}}{16} \|v\|_p$ . thus,  $|w_i| \leq \frac{\gamma^{1/p}}{16} \|v\|_p$  for all  $i \in [n]$ . subject to these constraints, we have that

$$
\| w \| _ {2 p} ^ {2 p} \leq \frac {1 6 ^ {p}}{\gamma} \cdot \frac {\gamma^ {2}}{1 6 ^ {2 p}} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2 p}.
$$

thus we have

$$
\| w \| _ {2 p} ^ {2 p - 4} \leq \frac {\gamma^ {1 - 2 / p}}{1 6 ^ {p - 2}} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2 p - 4}.
$$

hence, the variance is at most

$$
\| v \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot \gamma^ {2 / p} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2} \cdot \gamma^ {1 - 2 / p} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2 p - 4} \leq \frac {\gamma}{n ^ {1 - 2 / p}} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2} \cdot \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2} \cdot \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2 p - 4}.
$$

thus by setting  $k = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$ , we obtain an additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{p} \cdot f_p(v)$  approximation to  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v)$  with constant probability. we can then boost this probability to  $1 - \frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$  by repeating  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$  times. hence, we have a  $(\gamma ,\varepsilon ,\delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f_{p}$ .

to analyze the total space complexity, first observe that we use  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2/p} \log n\right)$  instances of sampler, countsketch, and an  $f_2$  moment estimation algorithm. for countsketch, we only require constant factor approximation, so that each instance uses space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log^2 n\right)$ . similarly for an  $f_2$  moment estimation algorithm, we only require constant factor approximation, so that  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log^2 n\right)$  space suffices, by theorem 3.8. each instance of sampler uses space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log^3 n\right)$ . hence, the total space is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2/p} \log^3 n \log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$ .

estimation of  $\langle v, u^{p-1} \rangle$ . we first duplicate each coordinate of  $u$  and  $v$  a total number of  $m = n^c$  times, for some sufficiently large constant  $c > 0$ . for each  $i \in [n]$  and  $j \in [m]$ , we scale  $u_i$  and  $v_i$  by  $1/e_{i,j}^{1/2}$ , where  $e_{i,j}$  is a random exponential variable truncated at  $n^2$ , where  $n = nm$ . thus we have that the cdf of  $\frac{1}{e_{i,j}}$  satisfies  $\mathbf{pr}\left[\frac{1}{e_{i,j}} \geq x\right] \leq \theta\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)$  for  $x > 0$ , so that  $\mathbb{e}\left[\frac{1}{e_{i,j}}\right] = \int_0^{n^2} 1 - \left(1 - \theta\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)\right) dx = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ . let  $u \in \mathbb{r}^n$  and  $v \in \mathbb{r}^n$  be scaled and duplicated vector representations of  $u$  and  $v$ , respectively.

lemma 5.7. for integer  $p > 2$ , there exists an algorithm that uses space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p} \log^3 n \log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$  and outputs an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f_p(v)$  approximation to  $\langle v, u^{p-1} \rangle$ .

proof. for an oblivious stream  $s$ , let  $v$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_1$  to  $t_2$  and  $u$  be the frequency vector induced by updates from time  $t_2$  to  $t$  exclusive, with  $f_p(u) \leq \gamma \cdot f_p(v)$ . we use sampler to sample indices  $j_1, \ldots, j_k \in [n]$  with so that each sample is a coordinate  $a \in [n]$  with probability  $\frac{u_a^2}{\|u\|_2^2} + \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ . for each  $b \in [k]$ , we also obtain an unbiased estimate  $\widehat{v_{j_b}}$  of  $v_{j_b}$  from the countsketch data structure. we also obtain an unbiased estimate  $\widehat{u}$  of  $\| u \|_2^2$ . observe that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {\boldsymbol {u}} \cdot \widehat {\boldsymbol {v} _ {j _ {b}}} \cdot \widehat {\boldsymbol {u} _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - 3}} \right] = \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \sum_ {a \in [ n ]} \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {u} _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y} (n)}\right) \boldsymbol {v} _ {a} \cdot \boldsymbol {u} _ {a} ^ {p - 3} = \langle \boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {p - 1} \rangle .
$$

(1) use a set of exponential random variables to form a vector  $v$  of duplicated and scaled coordinates of  $v$ .  
(2) hash the coordinates of  $v$  into a countsketch data structure with  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  buckets.  
(3) use the same exponential random variables to perform perfect  $l_{2}$  sampling on  $u$  to obtain a coordinate  $(i,j)$  and an unbiased estimate  $\widehat{u_{i,j}}$  to  $u_{i,j}$ .  
(4) let  $\widehat{u}$  be an unbiased estimate of  $\| u\| _2^2$  with second moment  $\mathcal{o}\left(\| u\| _2^4\right)$ .  
(5) query countsketch for an unbiased estimate  $\widehat{v_{i,j}}$  to  $v_{i,j}$  and set an estimator as  $\widehat{u} \cdot \widehat{v_{i,j}} (\widehat{u_{i,j}})^{p-3}$ .  
(6) output the mean of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} \cdot n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$  such estimators.

fig. 9:  $f_{p}$  difference estimator for  $\langle v, u^{p - 1} \rangle$  with integer  $p > 2$ .

to analyze the variance of  $\widehat{u} \cdot \widehat{v_{j_b}} (\widehat{u_{j_b}})^{p-3}$ , we first observe that by the min-stability of exponential random variables, the maximum coordinate of the scaled vector  $v$  is distributed as  $\| v \|_2 / e^{1/2}$  for an exponential random variable  $e$ . if the coordinate  $a \in [n]$  from the unscaled vector  $v$  is sampled, this maximum coordinate is also  $v_a / e_{a,j}^{1/2}$  for an exponential random variable  $e_{a,j}$ . we have that  $v_a / e_{a,j}^{1/2} \geq \| v_{-a,j} \|_2 / e^{1/2}$ , where  $v_{-a}$  indicates the vector  $v$  with the  $a$ -th entry set to zero. since  $e = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$  with probability  $1 - \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ , then we have  $e_{a,j} \leq \frac{\mathcal{o}(\log n) \cdot v_a^2}{\|v\|_2^2}$ . hence  $e_{a,j}$  has expectation  $\frac{\mathcal{o}(\log n) \cdot v_a^2}{\|v\|_2^2}$  so that by using  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  buckets, countsketch outputs an estimate  $\widehat{v_{j_b}}$  with variance  $v_a^2$  if  $a \in [n]$  is sampled in iteration  $b$ . thus, there exists a constant  $c > 0$  such that the variance of  $\widehat{u} \cdot \widehat{v_{j_b}} (\widehat{u_{j_b}})^{p-3}$  is at most

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v a r} \left(\widehat {u} \cdot \widehat {v _ {j _ {b}}} \cdot \widehat {u _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - 3}}\right) \leq \sum_ {a \in [ n ]} \left(\frac {u _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| u \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot c \| u \| _ {2} ^ {4} \cdot v _ {a} ^ {2} \cdot u _ {a} ^ {2 p - 6} \\ \leq \sum_ {a \in [ n ]} 2 c \cdot u _ {a} ^ {2 p - 4} v _ {a} ^ {2} \cdot \| u \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

by hölder's inequality, the variance is at most

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v a r} \left(\widehat {u} \cdot \widehat {v _ {j _ {b}}} \cdot \widehat {u _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - 3}}\right) \leq 2 c \cdot \| u \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot \left(\sum_ {a \in [ n ]} v _ {a} ^ {p}\right) ^ {2 / p} \left(\sum_ {a \in [ n ]} u _ {a} ^ {2 p}\right) ^ {1 - 2 / p} \\ = 2 c \cdot \| u \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2} \cdot \| u \| _ {2 p} ^ {2 p - 4} \\ \leq 2 c \cdot n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \cdot \| u \| _ {p} ^ {2} \cdot \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2} \cdot \| u \| _ {2 p} ^ {2 p - 4} \\ \leq 2 c \cdot n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \cdot \| u \| _ {p} ^ {2 p - 2} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2} \\ \leq 2 c \cdot \gamma n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \cdot n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \| v \| _ {p} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality results from the fact that  $f_{p}(u) \leq \gamma f_{p}(v)$  and  $p > 2$ . thus by setting  $k = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$ , we obtain an additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{p} \cdot f_p(v)$  approximation to  $\langle v, u^{p - 1} \rangle$  with constant probability. we can then boost this probability to  $1 - \frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$  by repeating  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$  times.

putting it all together. we now give our  $f_{p}$  difference estimator for integer  $p > 2$  using the above subroutines to approximate  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v) = \sum_{i = 1}^{p}\binom {p}{i}\langle v^{i},u^{p - i}\rangle$ .

lemma 5.8 ( $f_p$  difference estimator for integer  $p > 2$ ). for integer  $p > 2$ , there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f_p$  that uses space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2/p} \log^3 n \log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$ .

proof. for an oblivious stream  $s$ , let  $v$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of  $s$  from time  $t_1$  to  $t_2$  and  $u$  be the frequency vector induced by updates from time  $t_2$  to  $t$  exclusive, with  $f_p(u) \leq \gamma \cdot f_p(v)$ . observe that for integer  $p$ , we can expand

$$
f _ {p} (v + u) - f _ {p} (v) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} {\binom {p} {i}} \langle v ^ {i}, u ^ {p - i} \rangle ,
$$

where we write  $v^p$  as the coordinate-wise  $p$ -th power of  $v$ . let  $\mathcal{h}$  be the set of coordinates output by heavyhitters. moreover,

$$
f _ {p} (v + u) - f _ {p} (v) = \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {h}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} {\binom {p} {i}} v _ {a} ^ {i} u _ {a} ^ {p - i} + \sum_ {a \notin \mathcal {h}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} {\binom {p} {i}} v _ {a} ^ {i} u _ {a} ^ {p - i}.
$$

by theorem 3.10, there exists an algorithm heavyhitters that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2 - 2 / p}\log^2n}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$  space and outputs a list  $\mathcal{h}$  of coordinates and estimates  $\widehat{v_{a}}$  with additive error  $\frac{\varepsilon\gamma^{1 / p}}{64\gamma}\| v\| _p$ . thus using the estimates  $\widehat{v_{a}}$  along with the corresponding coordinates  $u_{a}$  gives an additive  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon)f_p(v)$  approximation to  $\sum_{a\in \mathcal{h}}\sum_{i = 1}^{p}\binom {p}{i}v_a^i u_a^{p - i}$  using  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{\gamma\log^2n}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$  space, for  $p > 2$ . by lemma 5.7 and lemma 5.6, we similarly obtain an additive  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon)f_p(v)$  approximation to  $\sum_{a\notin \mathcal{h}}\sum_{i = 1}^{p}\binom {p}{i}v_a^i u_a^{p - i}$  using  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\log^3 n\log \frac{n}{\delta'}\right)$  space, where  $\delta' = \frac{\delta}{p}$ . thus by rescaling  $\varepsilon$ , we obtain an additive  $\varepsilon f_p(v)$  approximation to  $f_{p}(v + u) - f_{p}(v)$  using  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\log^3 n\log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$  bits of space.

using our difference estimator, we obtain a robust algorithm for  $f_{p}$  moment estimation for integer  $p > 2$ .

theorem 5.9 (adversarially robust  $f_{p}$  streaming algorithm for integer  $p > 2$ ). given  $\varepsilon > 0$  and integer  $p > 2$ , there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation for  $f_{p}$  that succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$  and uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2/p} \log^5 n \log^3 \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  bits of space.

proof. for  $p > 2$ ,  $f_{p}$  is a monotonic function with  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n\right)$ , by observation 1.12. by lemma 5.8 and theorem 5.2, there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator that uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\gamma}{\varepsilon^ {2}} n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \log^ {3} n \log \frac {n}{\delta}\right)
$$

and a strong tracker for  $f_{p}$  that each uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \log^ {2} n \log \frac {n}{\delta}\right).
$$

we use these subroutines in the framework of algorithm 1. therefore, theorem 2.5 with  $s_{1}(n,\delta ,\varepsilon) = n^{1 - 2 / p}\log^3 n\log \frac{n}{\delta}$ ,  $c = 1$ , and  $s_{2} = 0$  proves that there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  for  $f_{p}$  that succeeds with constant probability and uses space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\log^5 n\log^3\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ .

# 6 robust  $f_{0}$  estimation

in this section, we use the framework of section 2 to give an adversarially robust streaming algorithm for the distinct elements problem or equivalently, the  $f_0$  moment estimation. we again require an  $f_0$  strong tracker and an  $f_0$  difference estimator so that we can apply theorem 2.5. fortunately, we can again use similar sketches for both the  $f_0$  strong stracker and the  $f_0$  difference estimator, similar to the approach for  $f_2$  moment estimation. we can then optimize our algorithm beyond the guarantees of theorem 2.5 so that our final space guarantees in theorem 6.4 match the bounds in theorem 3.8. note that this does not quite match the best known  $f_0$  strong-tracking algorithm on insertion-only streams [bla20], which uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\log\log n}{\varepsilon^2} +\log n\right)$  bits of space:

theorem 6.1 (oblivious  $f_0$  strong tracking). [bla20] there exists an insertion-only streaming algorithm f0estimate(1, t, ε, δ) that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log \frac{1}{\delta} + \log n\right)$  bits of space and provides  $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ -strong  $f_0$  tracking.

theorem 6.1 and other  $f_0$  approximation algorithms use a balls-and-bins argument with increasing levels of sophistication [bjk+02b, knw10b, bla20]. we use a similar balls-and-bins argument, where each item is subsampled at a level  $k$  with probability  $\frac{1}{2^k}$ , to obtain an  $f_0$  difference estimator. by counting the number of items in a level with  $\theta\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  items that survive the subsampling process for the stream  $u$ , it follows that the expected number of the distinct items in  $v$  but not  $u$  is  $\theta\left(\frac{\gamma^2}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$ . thus we obtain a  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{\gamma}\right)$ -approximation to  $f_0(v) - f_0(u)$  from this procedure by first running the balls-and-bins experiment on  $u$  and counting the number of bins that are occupied at some level  $k$  with  $\theta\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  survivors. we then run the same balls-and-bins experiment on  $v - u$  by only counting the additional bins that are occupied at level  $k$ , and rescaling this number by  $2^k$ . note that additional bins only correspond to items in  $v$  but not  $u$ , which is exactly  $f_0(v) - f_0(u)$ . although level  $k$  does not necessarily give a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f_0(v) - f_0(u)$ , it does give a  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{\gamma}\right)$ -approximation to  $f_0(v) - f_0(u)$ , which translates to an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f_0(u)$  approximation to  $f_0(v) - f_0(u)$  and is exactly the requirement for the  $f_0$  difference estimator, since  $f_0(v) - f_0(u) \leq \gamma f(u)$ .

lemma 6.2 ( $f_0$  difference estimator). there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f_0$  that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} \left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right) + \log n\right)$  bits of space.

proof. suppose we would like to estimate  $f_0(v) - f_0(u) = \gamma \cdot f_0(u)$  for  $\gamma = \omega(\varepsilon)$  and we subsample with probability  $\frac{1}{2^k}$  so that  $\frac{f_0(u)}{2^k} = \theta\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$ . then in expectation, the number of sampled items in  $v$  that are not in  $u$  is  $\frac{f_0(v) - f_0(u)}{2^k} = \theta\left(\frac{\gamma^2}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$ . hence if  $x$  is the number of survivors from  $f_0(v) - f_0(u)$  at level  $k$ , then  $\mathbb{e}\left[2^k \cdot x\right] = f_0(v) - f_0(u)$  and the variance of  $2^k \cdot x$  is at most

$$
2 ^ {k} \cdot (f _ {0} (v) - f _ {0} (u)) \leq \frac {\varepsilon^ {2} \cdot f _ {0} (u)}{\gamma} \cdot (f _ {0} (v) - f _ {0} (u)) \leq \varepsilon^ {2} (f _ {0} (u)) ^ {2}.
$$

thus, the  $2^{k} \cdot x$  gives an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f_{0}$  approximation to  $f_{0}(v) - f_{0}(u)$  with constant probability. the probability can then be boosted to at least  $1 - \delta$  by taking the median of  $\log \frac{1}{\delta}$  parallel instances. since the algorithm maintains  $\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2}$  sampled items, the total space naively required is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma\log n}{\varepsilon^2}\log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)$ . by hashing to  $p = \mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n,\frac{1}{\delta}\right)$  buckets, the space bound can be further improved to  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} +\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right) + \log n\right)$ , e.g., by first composing a hash function  $h_1: [n] \to [p^3]$  and then a hash function  $h_2: [p^3] \to [p]$ , along the lines of [knw10b, bla20]. this suffices for a difference estimator at a single point in time; to obtain the strong tracking property, we note that both the estimator and  $f_{0}(v) - f_{0}(u)$  are monotonic. hence, it suffices to take a union bound over  $\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}$  times when the difference increases by a factor of  $(1 + \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon))$ . therefore, the total space required is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} +\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right) + \log n\right)$ .

note that the difference estimator in lemma 6.2 only requires pairwise independence and thus can be derandomized using a hash function that can be stored using  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  bits of space. we now use our difference estimator to get an adversarially robust streaming algorithm for the distinct elements problem.

theorem 6.3. given  $\varepsilon >0$ , there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f_{0}$  that succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$  and uses space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log n \log^ {3} \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n\right) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {2} n\right).
$$

proof. first note that  $f_0$  is a monotonic function with  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number  $\lambda = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log n\right)$  for  $m = \mathrm{poly}(n)$ , by observation 1.12. by theorem 6.1 and lemma 6.2, there exist a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\delta} \right) + \log n\right)$  bits of space and an oblivious strong tracker for  $f_0$  that uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log \frac{1}{\delta} + \log n\right)$  bits of space, which can be used in the framework of algorithm 1. we thus apply theorem 2.5 with  $s_1(n, \delta, \varepsilon) = \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\delta}$ ,  $c = 1$ , and  $s_2(n, \delta, \varepsilon) = \log n$ . thus the total space is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log n \log^ {3} \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot \left(\log \frac {1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log n\right) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {2} n\right).
$$

optimized  $f_{0}$  algorithm. to improve the space requirements, we can again observe that it suffices to maintain sketches  $\mathcal{a}_{a}$  and  $\mathcal{b}_{a,c}$  for  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  values of  $a$  at a time, instead of maintaining all sketches  $\mathcal{a}_{a}$  and  $\mathcal{b}_{a,c}$  simultaneously. by the same argument as before, it suffices to maintain only the most sketches  $\mathcal{a}_{i}$  and  $\mathcal{b}_{i,c}$  for only the smallest  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  values of  $i$  that are at least  $a$  since the output increases by a factor of 2 each time  $a$  increases and thus any larger index will have only missed  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon)$  fraction of the  $f_{0}$  of the stream. hence, any larger index still outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation once it becomes initialized.

theorem 6.4. given  $\varepsilon >0$ , there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation for  $f_{0}$  that succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$  and uses total space

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {4} \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \left(\log \log n + \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log n \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon}\right).
$$

proof. at any point, there are  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active indices  $a$  and  $\mathcal{o}(1)$  active indices  $c$  corresponding to sketches  $\mathcal{a}_a$  and  $\mathcal{b}_{a,c}$ . because there are  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  total indices  $a$  over the stream, we set  $\delta' = \frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}(\log n, \frac{1}{\varepsilon})}$ . thus from theorem 2.5 and theorem 6.3 that for fixed  $a$ , the total space that sketches  $\mathcal{a}_a$  and  $\mathcal{b}_{a,c}$  use across the  $\beta$  granularities is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \left(\log \log n + \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \log n\right),
$$

since we have  $s_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon) = \left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} +\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}\right), c = 1$ , and  $s_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon) = \log n$ . however, there are only  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active indices  $a$  simultaneously maintained by the optimized algorithm. for each active index  $a$ , the sketches use  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \log n\right)$  bits of space. as there are  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active indices of  $a$ , consisting of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  subroutines, then it takes  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  additional bits of space to store the splitting times for each of the  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  active subroutines across a stream of length  $m$ , with  $\log m = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ . therefore, the total space required is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {4} \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \left(\log \log n + \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \log n \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon}\right).
$$

![](images/70e46ee229f9da0b1e69eda194cc0114d8173904d91890ae03e9fb6ea0334343.jpg)

# 7 framework for streams with bounded twist number

recall that the previous framework of theorem 2.5 in section 2 was only suited for insertion-only streams. in this section, we modify the framework of algorithm 1 to handle  $f_{p}$  moment estimation on turnstile streams.

the main difference for turnstile streams is that the value of  $f$  on the underlying frequency vector induced by the stream can decrease as well as increase. thus if we assign an algorithm  $\mathcal{a}_j$  to be revealed when the value of  $f$  exceeds  $2^j$ , this may happen multiple times. however, once  $\mathcal{a}_j$  is revealed for the first time, then the input to the algorithm is no longer independent to the randomness of the algorithm, due to the adversary. hence, we can no longer use  $\mathcal{a}_j$  to estimate the value of  $f$  if it decreases and then exceeds  $2^j$  again.

nevertheless, note that each time the value of  $f$  increases or decreases by a factor of roughly  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ , the value of the  $(\varepsilon, m)$ -twist number  $\lambda$  increases. therefore,  $\lambda$  governs the number of times the value of  $f$  can exceed  $2^j$ , going from  $2^{j-1}$ . similarly,  $\lambda$  governs the number of times the value of  $f$  can exceed  $(1 + \varepsilon)^{c_i} 2^j$ , going from  $(1 + \varepsilon)^{c(i-1)} 2^j$  for any constant  $c$ . thus if  $\lambda$  is given in advance, the algorithm can maintain the appropriate number of independent instances of the difference estimators  $\mathcal{b}$  and the streaming algorithms  $\mathcal{a}$  to handle each time the value of  $f$  crosses a threshold. we give the algorithm in full in algorithm 3.

the proof of correctness on non-adaptive streams follows similarly from the same argument as lemma 2.4. the main difference is that because the value of  $f$  on the stream can both increase and decrease, we must be a little more careful in defining the times for which the difference estimators reveal their outputs. in addition, to ensure the correctness of the difference estimator, we must facilitate the conditions required for the difference estimator. thus we restart a difference estimator each time the value of the suffix has become sufficiently large even if the difference itself has not become large. this is a subtle issue for which we use the definition of twist number, rather than the simpler definition of flip number.

algorithm 3 framework for robust algorithms on turnstile streams  
input: stream  $u_{1},\ldots ,u_{m}\in [n]$  of updates to coordinates of an underlying frequency vector, accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$ , difference estimator  $\mathcal{b}$  for  $f$ , oblivious strong tracker  $\mathcal{a}$  for  $f$ ,  $\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{32},m\right)$ -twist number  $\lambda$   
output: robust  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f$   
1:  $\delta \gets \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)}$ ,  $\zeta \gets \frac{2}{2^{(c - 1) / 4} - 1}$ ,  $\eta \gets \frac{\varepsilon}{64\zeta}$ ,  $\beta \gets \lceil \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon} \rceil$ ,  $\tau \gets 0$   
2:  $a \gets 0$ ,  $\varphi \gets 2^{(c - 1) / 4}$ ,  $\gamma_{j} \gets 2^{j - 1}\eta$   
3: for  $j \in [\beta]$ ,  $\eta_{j} \gets \frac{\eta}{\beta}$  if  $c = 1$ ,  $\eta_{j} \gets \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta - j}}$  if  $c > 1$ .  
4:  $t_{i,j} \gets \infty$  for  $i \leq \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon \lambda)$ ,  $j \in [\beta]$   
5: for each update  $u_{t} \in [n]$ ,  $t \in [m]$  do  
6:  $j \gets \lceil \eta \tau \rceil$   
7:  $x \gets a_{a + 1,j}(1,t,\eta,\delta)$   
8: if  $x > 2^{a}$  then  
9:  $a \gets a + 1$ ,  $b \gets 0$ ,  $z_{a} \gets x$ ,  $t_{a,j} \gets \min(t,t_{a,j})$  for  $j \in [\beta]$ .  
10: if  $x < 2^{a - 1}$  then  
11:  $a \gets a - 1$ ,  $b \gets \left\lfloor \frac{8}{\varepsilon} \cdot (x - z_{a}) \right\rfloor$   
12:  $x \gets$  estimatef  
13: if  $x > \left(1 + \frac{(b + 1)\varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot z_{a}$  then  
14:  $\tau \gets \tau + 1$ ,  $b \gets b + 1$ ,  $k \gets \operatorname{lsb}(b,1)$ ,  $j \gets \left\lfloor \frac{\tau}{2^{k}} \right\rfloor$   
15:  $z_{a,k} \gets b_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\eta_{k},\delta)$   
16:  $t_{a,j} \gets t$  for  $j \in [k]$ .  
17: else if  $x < \left(1 + \frac{(b - 1)\varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot z_{a}$  then  
18:  $\tau \gets \tau + 1$ ,  $b \gets b - 1$ ,  $k \gets \operatorname{lsb}(b,1)$ ,  $j \gets \left\lfloor \frac{\tau}{2^{k}} \right\rfloor$   
19:  $z_{a,k} \gets b_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\gamma_{k},\eta_{k},\delta)$   
20:  $t_{a,j} \gets t$  for  $j \in [k]$ .  
21: else if there exist  $j,k$  with unfrozen  $b_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\gamma_{k},\eta_{k},\delta) > \frac{\gamma_{j}}{2} x$  then  
22:  $\tau \gets \tau + 1$ ,  $j \gets \left\lfloor \frac{\tau}{2^{k}} \right\rfloor$   
23:  $z_{a,k} \gets z_{a,k} + b_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\gamma_{k},\eta_{k},\delta)$   
24:  $t_{a,j} \gets t$  for  $j \in [k]$ .  
25: return  $(1 + \frac{b\varepsilon}{8}) \cdot z_{a}$   
output estimate for round  $t$

algorithm 4 subroutine estimatef, modified for algorithm 3  
1:  $x\gets z_{a},k\gets \mathrm{numbits}(b + 1),z_{i}\gets \mathrm{lsb}(b + 1,k + 1 - i)$  for  $i\in [k]$ $\triangleright z_1 > \dots >z_k$  are the nonzero bits in the binary representation of  $b + 1$    
3: for  $1\leq j\leq k - 1$  do  $\triangleright$  compile previous frozen components for estimator  $x$    
4:  $x\leftarrow x + z_{a,j}$    
5:  $j\gets \lfloor \frac{\tau}{2^{z_k}}\rfloor$    
6:  $x\gets x + \mathcal{b}_{a,j}\left(1,t_{a,z_k}^{(j)},t,\gamma_{z_k},\eta_{z_k},\delta\right)$ $\triangleright$  use unrevealed sketch for last component   
7: return  $x$

lemma 7.1 (correctness on non-adaptive streams). with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ -poly  $\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$ , algorithm 3 outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f$  at all times on a non-adaptive stream.

proof. let  $t_i^{(y)}$  be the time in the stream at which the counter  $a$  in algorithm 3 is set to  $i$  for the  $y$ -th time, for integers  $i, y > 0$ . for each integer  $j \geq 0$ , let  $u_{i,j}$  be the last round for which  $z_{i,j}$  in algorithm 3 is defined. let  $t_{i,\ell}$  be the last round such that the counter  $b$  in algorithm 3 is first set to  $\ell$ . let  $\mathcal{e}_1$  be the event that algorithm 3 outputs a  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)$ -approximation to the value of  $f$  at all times  $t_i^{(y)}$ , so that by lemma 2.2,  $\mathcal{e}_1$  holds with probability at least  $1 - \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\lambda\delta\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)$ , since there are at most  $\lambda$  times  $\{t_i^{(y)}\}$  by the definition of the twist number. for the remainder of the proof, we fix the integers  $i$  and  $y$  and show correctness between  $t_i^{(y)}$  and the first time  $t > t_i^{(y)}$  such that either  $t = t_{i+1}^{(y')}$  or  $t = t_{i-1}^{(y')}$  for some  $y'$ . without loss of generality, suppose  $t = t_{i+1}^{(y')}$ . we then show correctness at all times  $t_i^{(\ell,r)}$  for each value  $\ell$  obtained by the counter  $b$  between times  $t_i^{(y)}$  and  $t_{i+1}^{(y')}$ . the remainder of the argument proceeds similarly to lemma 2.4.

let  $\ell$  be a fixed value of the counter  $b$ ,  $k = \mathrm{numbits}(\ell)$ , and  $z_{x} = \operatorname{lsb}(\ell, x)$  for  $x \in [k]$  so that  $z_{1} > \ldots > z_{k}$  are the nonzero bits in the binary representation of  $\ell$ . by similar reasoning to lemma 2.3, we have for all  $x \in [k]$

$$
f (1, u _ {i, z _ {x}}) - f (1, t _ {i, z _ {x}}) \leq \frac {1}{2 ^ {\beta - x - 3}} f (1, t _ {i}),
$$

with probability at least  $1 - \delta \cdot \mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)$ . since  $u_{i,z_x}$  is the last round for which  $z_{i,z_x}$  in algorithm 3 is defined and all times  $t_{i,j}$  for  $j\leq z_{x}$  are reset at that round, then we have  $u_{i,z_x} = t_{i,z_{x + 1}}$  for all  $x\in [k]$ . thus for  $u_{i,z_k} = t_{i,\ell}$ , we can decompose

$$
f (1, t _ {i, \ell}) = \sum_ {x = 1} ^ {k} \left(f (1, u _ {i, z _ {x}}) - f (1, u _ {i, z _ {x - 1}})\right) = \sum_ {x = 1} ^ {k} \left(f (1, u _ {i, z _ {x}}) - f (1, t _ {i, z _ {x}})\right).
$$

recall that the value  $x_{\ell}$  of  $x$  output by estimatef at time  $t_{i,\ell}$  satisfies  $x_{\ell} = z_{i} + \sum_{x=1}^{k} z_{i,z_{x}}$ . by lemma 2.2,  $z_{i}$  incurs at most  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)$  multiplicative error to  $f(1,t_{i,\ell})$  since  $f(1,t_{i,\ell}) \leq 4f(t_{i})$ . moreover,  $z_{i,z_{k}}$  is an additive  $2\eta_{z_{k}}f(1,t_{i})$  approximation to  $f(1,u_{i,z_{k}}) - f(1,t_{i,z_{k}})$ . hence, the total additive error of  $x_{\ell}$  to  $f(1,t_{i,\ell})$  is at most  $\frac{\varepsilon}{4} \cdot f(1,t_{i,\ell}) + \sum_{x=1}^{k} 2\eta_{z_{k}}f(1,t_{i})$ . now if the  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator has space dependency  $\frac{\gamma^{c}}{\varepsilon^{2}}$  with  $c = 1$ , then  $\eta_{z_{k}} = \frac{\eta}{\beta}$ . otherwise if  $c > 1$ , then  $\eta_{z_{k}} = \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta - z_{k}}}$ , with  $\varphi > 1$ . in both cases, we have that the total additive error is at most  $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(1,t_{i,\ell})$ , since  $\beta = \left\lceil \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon} \right\rceil$ . therefore,  $x_{\ell}$  is a  $\left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)$ -approximation to  $f(1,t_{i,\ell})$ .

note that the final output at time  $t_{i,\ell}$  by algorithm 3 further incurs additive error at most  $\frac{\varepsilon}{8} \cdot z_i$  due to the rounding  $\left(1 + \frac{b\varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot z_a$  in the last step. by lemma 2.2,  $z_i \leq \left(1 + \frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right) \cdot f(1, t_{i,\ell})$ . hence for sufficiently small constant  $\varepsilon > 0$ , we have that algorithm 3 outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f(1, t_{i,\ell})$ , with probability at least  $1 - \delta \cdot \text{poly} \left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}, \log n\right)$ .

theorem 7.2 (framework for adversarially robust algorithms on turnstile streams). let  $\varepsilon, \delta > 0$  and  $f$  be a monotonic function with  $(\eta, m)$ -twist number  $\lambda$ , where  $\eta = \frac{\varepsilon}{32}$ . suppose there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator with space dependency  $\frac{\gamma^c}{\varepsilon^2}$  for  $c \geq 1$  and a strong tracker for  $f$  on dynamic streams that use  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} s_f(n, \delta, \varepsilon)\right)$  bits of space. then there exists an adversarially robust dynamic streaming algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  approximation for  $f$  that succeeds with

constant probability. for  $c > 1$ , the algorithm uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon} \cdot s_f(n, \delta', \varepsilon)\right)$  bits of space, where  $\delta' = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(\lambda, \frac{1}{\varepsilon})}\right)$ . for  $c = 1$ , the algorithm uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon} \log^3 \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s_f(n, \delta', \varepsilon)\right)$  bits of space.

proof. consider algorithm 3 and note that lemma 7.1 proves correctness over non-adaptive streams. we now argue that lemma 7.1 holds with over adversarial streams, using the same argument as theorem 2.5 and the switch-a-sketch proof by [bjwy20]. observe that each time either counter  $a$  or  $b$  either increases or decreases, the modified subroutine estimatef compels algorithm 3 to use new subroutines  $\mathcal{b}_{i,j}$  and  $\mathcal{a}_i$  that have not previously been revealed to the adversary. in particular, since estimatef sets  $j = \left\lfloor \frac{b + 1}{2^z k} \right\rfloor + \left\lceil \frac{\tau}{2^k} \right\rceil$ , then since the  $\eta$  twist number has only reached  $\tau$ , the value of  $j$  ensures that  $\mathcal{b}_{i,j}$  has not been previously revealed. hence, the input is independent of the internal randomness of  $\mathcal{b}_{i,j}$  until the counter  $b$  reaches  $b = j$  for the  $\left\lceil \frac{\tau}{2^k} \right\rceil$ -th time, at which point  $\mathcal{b}_{i,j}$  is revealed once and not used again.

it remains to analyze the space complexity of algorithm 3. we first analyze the space complexity for a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator with space dependency  $\frac{\gamma^c}{\varepsilon^2}$  and  $c > 1$ . observe that  $a = \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon \lambda)$  and across the  $\beta$  granularities, each value of  $\eta_k$  is invoked at most  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\lambda}{2^k}\right)$  times. since we require correctness of  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon \lambda)$  instances of  $\mathcal{b}$ , it suffices to set  $\delta' = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}\left(\lambda, \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}\right)$ . by assumption, a single instance of  $\mathcal{b}$  with granularity  $\eta_k$  uses at most  $\frac{c_1}{(\eta_k)^2} s_f(n, \delta', \varepsilon)$  bits of space, for some constant  $c_1 > 0$ . thus for fixed  $a$ ,  $\beta \leq \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon} + 1$ ,  $\gamma_k = 2^{k-1} \eta$ , and accuracy parameter  $\eta_k = \frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-k}}$ , the total space for each  $\mathcal{b}_{a,j}$  across the  $\beta$  granularities and the values of the counter  $a$  is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {1} \lambda \gamma_ {k} ^ {c}}{\eta_ {k} ^ {2} 2 ^ {k}} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {1} \lambda \gamma_ {\beta - k} ^ {c}}{\eta_ {\beta - k} ^ {2} 2 ^ {\beta - k}} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \\ \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {2} \lambda (2 ^ {\beta - k} \varepsilon) ^ {c} \varphi^ {2 k}}{\varepsilon^ {2} \cdot 2 ^ {\beta - k}} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \\ \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {3} \lambda 2 ^ {- c k} \varphi^ {2 k}}{\varepsilon \cdot 2 ^ {- k}} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon), \\ \end{array}
$$

for some absolute constants  $c_1, c_2, c_3 > 0$ . since  $\varphi = 2^{(c - 1) / 4}$  for  $c > 1$ , then there exists a constant  $c_4$  such that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {1} \lambda \gamma_ {k} ^ {c}}{\eta_ {k} ^ {2} 2 ^ {k}} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {4} \lambda 2 ^ {k - c k} 2 ^ {(c k - k) / 2}}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \\ \leq \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\lambda}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon)\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

moreover, since each strong tracker  $\mathcal{a}$  is used when the value of  $f$  increases by a constant factor, then  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon \lambda)$  instances of  $\mathcal{a}$  are required. hence, the total space required for  $c > 1$  is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon} \cdot s_f(n, \delta', \varepsilon)\right)$ .

for  $c = 1$ , we recall that  $\eta_k = \frac{\eta}{\beta}$ , with  $\beta \leq \log \frac{8}{\varepsilon} + 1$ . thus the total space is

$$
\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {1} \lambda \gamma_ {k} ^ {c}}{\eta_ {k} ^ {2} 2 ^ {k}} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {5} \lambda (2 ^ {k - 1} \varepsilon)}{\eta^ {2} \beta^ {- 2} \cdot 2 ^ {k}} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon)
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\beta} \frac {c _ {6} \lambda \beta^ {2} \varepsilon}{\eta^ {2}} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon) \\ \leq \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {\lambda}{\varepsilon} \log^ {3} \frac {1}{\varepsilon} \cdot s _ {f} (n, \delta^ {\prime}, \varepsilon)\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/2bea902ccce571da482a3db67c6f4f5a897ea83bb1921ceba17e347e52bd44d0.jpg)

as a direct application of theorem 7.2, we obtain the following:
