theorem 7.3. given  $\varepsilon >0$ , there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm on turnstile streams with  $(\varepsilon ,m)$  twist number  $\lambda$  that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation for  $f_{p}$  with  $p\in [0,2]$  that succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$  and uses  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon}\log^2 n\right)$  bits of space.

# 8 framework for sliding window algorithms

in this section, we describe a general framework for norm estimation in the sliding window model, using the sketch stitching and granularity changing technique. we first require the following background on sliding windows algorithms.

definition 8.1 (smooth function). given adjacent substreams  $a$ ,  $b$ , and  $c$ , a function  $f$  is  $(\alpha, \beta)$ -smooth if  $(1 - \beta)f(a \cup b) \leq f(b)$ , then  $(1 - \alpha)f(a \cup b \cup c) \leq f(b \cup c)$  for some parameters  $0 < \beta \leq \alpha < 1$ .

intuitively, once a suffix of a data stream becomes a  $(1\pm \beta)$ -approximation for a smooth function, then it is always a  $(1\pm \alpha)$ -approximation, regardless of the subsequent updates that arrive in the stream. smooth functions are integral to the smooth histogram framework for sliding window algorithms.

smooth histograms. braverman and ostrovsky introduced the smooth histogram, an elegant framework that solves a large number of problems in the sliding window model, such as  $l_{p}$  norm estimation, longest increasing subsequence, geometric mean estimation, or other weakly additive functions [bo07]. thus the smooth histogram data structure maintains a number of timestamps throughout the data stream, along with a streaming algorithm for each timestamp that stores a sketch of all the elements seen from the timestamp. the timestamps maintain the invariant that at most three checkpoints produce values that are within  $(1 - \beta)$  of each other, since any two of the sketches would always output values that are within  $(1 - \alpha)$  afterwards. hence if the function is polynomially bounded, then the smooth histogram data structure only needs a logarithmic number of timestamps.

we now define the following variant of a difference estimator.

definition 8.2 (suffix-pivoted difference estimator). given a stream  $s$  and fixed times  $t_1, t_2,$  and  $t_3$ , let frequency vectors  $u$  and  $v$  be induced by the updates of  $s$  between times  $[t_1, t_2)$  and  $[t_2, t_3)$ . given an accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon > 0$  and a failure probability  $\delta \in (0,1)$ , a streaming algorithm  $\mathcal{c}(t_1, t_2, t, \gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$  is a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -suffix difference estimator for a function  $f$  if, with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ , it outputs an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f(v + w_t)$  approximation to  $f(u + v + w_t) - f(v + w_t)$  for all frequency vectors  $w_t$  induced by  $[t_3, t)$  for times  $t > t_3$ , given  $\min(f(u), f(u + v) - f(v)) \leq \gamma \cdot f(v)$  for a ratio parameter  $\gamma \in (0,1]$ .

observe that the difference between definition 2.1 and definition 8.2 is that the fixed-prefix difference estimator approximates  $f(v + w_{t}) - f(v)$  when the contribution to  $f$  of the first frequency vector  $v$  that arrives in the stream is much larger than that of  $w_{t}$ , while the suffix-pivoted difference estimator approximates  $f(v + w_{t}) - f(w_{t})$  when  $f(w_{t})$  is larger than  $f(v)$ . nevertheless, we prove that our fixed-prefix difference estimators for  $f_{p}$  moment estimation with  $p \in (0,2]$  can also be adjusted to form suffix-pivoted difference estimators. unless otherwise noted, the difference estimators in this section refer to the suffix-pivoted difference estimators rather than the fixed-prefix difference estimators.

we adapt our sketch stitching and granularity changing technique to the sliding window model by focusing on the suffix of the stream, since prefixes of the stream may expire. we thus run the highest accuracy algorithms, the separate streaming algorithms  $\mathcal{a}$ , on various suffixes of the stream similar to the smooth histogram framework. it follows from smoothness that we maintain an instance of  $\mathcal{a}$  starting at some time  $t_0 \leq m - w + 1$ , whose output is within a factor 2 of the value of  $f$  on the sliding window. our task is then to remove the extraneous contribution of the updates between times  $t_0$  and  $m - w + 1$ , i.e., the starting time of the sliding window.

we partition the streamstream of these updates into separate blocks based on their contribution to the value of  $f$  by guessing  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  values for the final value of  $f$  on the sliding window and forming new difference estimators at level  $j$  when the value of  $f$  on each block has exceeded a  $\frac{1}{2^j}$  fraction of the corresponding guess. we terminate a guess when there are more than  $100 \cdot 2^j$  blocks in that level, indicating that the guess is too low. we can maintain separate sketches for these blocks, with varying granularities, and stitch these sketches together at the end. we give our algorithm in full in algorithm 5.

interpretation of algorithm 5. we now translate between the previous intuition and the pseudocode of algorithm 5. at each time, algorithm 5 only runs two subroutines: guessandupdate and mergesw. the first subroutine guessandupdate creates new instances of each algorithm (both a streaming algorithm approximating  $f$  on a suffix of the stream and a difference estimator starting at each time) at each time in the stream. moreover, guessandupdate partitions the stream into blocks for the difference estimator by using exponentially increasing guesses for the value of  $f$  at the end of the stream to assist with appropriate granularity for each block. the second subroutine mergesw performs maintenance on the data structure to ensure that there are not too many instances that have been created by the first subroutine that are simultaneously running. namely, mergesw deletes algorithms running on suffixes of the stream that output a similar value, so that the number of remaining algorithms is logarithmic rather than linear. similarly, mergesw merges two difference estimators when it is clear their combined contribution is still too small. finally at the end of the stream, stitchsw creates an estimate for the value of  $f$  on the stream by stitching together the estimates output by each difference estimator. although algorithm 1 is notationally heavy, each timestamp  $t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}$  should be associated with (1) a guess  $k \in [c\log n]$  for the value of  $f$  at the end of the stream, (2) an index  $i \in \mathcal{o}(\log n)$  roughly associated with the number of times  $f$  has actually doubled in the stream so far, (3) a granularity  $j$ , and (4) the number of the block  $\ell$  in granularity  $j$ .

recall that we abuse notation so that  $w$  represents the active updates in the stream. thus we use  $f_{p}(w)$  to denote the  $f_{p}$  moment of the active elements in the sliding window, i.e., if  $m < w$ , then  $f_{p}(w) = f_{p}(1:m)$  and if  $m \geq w$ , then  $f_{p}(w) = f_{p}(1:m - w + 1)$ . we first show that the timestamps  $t_1, \ldots, t_s$  mark suffixes of the stream in which the moment roughly double, so that

algorithm 5 moment estimation in the sliding window model  
input: stream  $u_{1},\ldots ,u_{m}$  of updates, an  $(\varepsilon ,\varepsilon^{q})$  -smooth function  $f$  , accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$  window parameter  $w > 0$    
output: robust  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  -approximation to  $f$    
1:  $\delta \leftarrow \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(m)},\eta \leftarrow \frac{\varepsilon}{2^{20}q\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}},\varphi \leftarrow \sqrt{2}$  be a parameter   
2:  $\beta \gets \lceil \log \frac{100\cdot 4^q}{\varepsilon^q}\rceil ,\gamma_j\gets 2^{3 - j}$  for all  $j\in [\beta ]$    
3: for each update  $u_{t}\in [n],t\in [m]$  do   
4: guessandupdate  $\triangleright$  create new subroutines for each update   
5: mergesw  $\triangleright$  removes extraneous subroutines   
6: return  $z\gets$  stitchsw  $\triangleright$  estimate  $f$  on sliding window

algorithm 6 subroutine guessandupdate of algorithm 5: create new subroutines for each update  
1: let  $s$  be the number of instances of  $\mathcal{a}$   
2:  $t_{s+1} \gets t$   
3: start a new instance  $\mathcal{a}(t_{s+1}, t, \eta, \delta)$ .  
4: for  $j \in [\beta]$  do  
5: for  $k \in [c \log n]$  do  
6: let  $r_k$  be the number of instances of timestamps  $t_{s+1, j, *}^{(k)}$ .  
7: if  $\mathcal{a}(t_{s+1, j, r_k}^{(k)}, t-1, 1, \delta) \in [n^c / 2^{j+k+11}, n^c / 2^{j+k+10}]$ ,  $\mathcal{a}(t_{s+1, j, r_k}^{(k)}, t, 1, \delta) > n^c / 2^{j+k+10}$ , and  $r < 100 \cdot 2^{j+10}$  then  
8: for  $\ell > k$  do  
9:  $t_{s+1, j, r_{\ell+1}}^{(\ell)} \gets t$ .  
10: demarcate sdiffest $(t_{s+1, j, r_\ell}^{(\ell)}, t_{s+1, j, r_{\ell+1}}^{(\ell)}, t, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta)$ .  
11: start a new instance  $\mathcal{a}(t_{s+1, j, r_{\ell+1}}^{(\ell)}, t, 1, \delta)$ .

$f_{p}(t_{1},m)\geq \ldots \geq f_{p}(t_{s},m)$  and  $2^{s - i}\cdot f_p(t_s,m)\geq f_p(t_i,m)$  for each  $i\in [s]$ . since  $w$  is oblivious to the algorithm until the stitchsw subroutine, it suffices to show that if  $t_i\leq m - w + 1\leq t_{i + 1}$ , then  $f_{p}(w)\leq f_{p}(t_{i},m)\leq 2f_{p}(w)$  and  $\frac{1}{2} f_p(w)\leq f_p(t_{i + 1},m)\leq f_p(w)$ .

we first show that the top level gives a constant factor approximation to  $f(w)$ . the intuition behind the lemma is that the maintenance procedure mergesw only deletes an instance of an algorithm if the instance is sandwiched between two other algorithms whose values are within a factor of  $(1 - 1/8^q)$ , where the function is  $(\varepsilon, \varepsilon^q)$ -smooth. thus by the definition of smoothness, we should expect the other two algorithms to always produce values that are within a factor of two, regardless of the subsequent updates of the stream. this is important because it allows us to translate between the values of various suffixes of the stream, e.g.,  $f(t_i, m) \leq 2f(w)$  implies that additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(t_i, m)$  error gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -multiplicative error to  $f(w)$ .

lemma 8.3 (constant factor partitions in top level). let  $i$  be the largest index such that  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$ . let  $k$  be the smallest integer such that  $n^c / 2^k \leq \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, 1, \delta)$ . let  $\mathcal{e}$  be the event that all subroutines  $\mathcal{a}$  in algorithm 5 succeed. then conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}$ ,  $f(w) \leq f(t_i, m) \leq 2f(w)$  and  $\frac{1}{2} f(w) \leq f(t_{i+1}, m) \leq f(w)$ .

proof. observe that in the mergesw subroutine, we only delete a timestamp  $t_{i'}$  if  $a(t_{i' + 2}, t, \eta, \delta) \geq$

algorithm 7 subroutine mergesw of algorithm 5: removes extraneous subroutines  
1: let  $s$  be the number of instances of  $\mathcal{a}$ .  
2: for  $i\in [s]$ ,  $j\in [\beta]$ , and  $k\in [c\log n]$  do ▷difference estimator maintenance  
3: let  $r_k$  be the number of instances of timestamps  $t_{s+1,j,*}^{(k)}$ .  
4: for  $\ell \in [r_k - 1]$  do ▷merges two algorithms with "small" contributions  
5: if  $\mathcal{a}(t_{i,j,\ell-1}^{(k)}, t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)}, 1, \delta) \leq n^c / 2^{k+j+10}$  then  
6: merge (add) the sketches for  $\mathcal{a}(t_{i,j,k-1}, t_{i,j,k}, 1, \delta)$  and  $\mathcal{a}(t_{i,j,k}, t_{i,j,k+1}, 1, \delta)$ .  
7: merge (add) the sketches for sdiffest $(t_{i,j,k-1}, t_{i,j,k}, t, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta)$  and sdiffest $(t_{i,j,k}, t_{i,j,k+1}, t, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta)$ .  
8: relabel the times  $t_{i,j,*}$ .  
9: for  $i\in [s-2]$  do ▷smooth histogram maintenance  
10: if  $\mathcal{a}(t_{i+2}, t, \eta, \delta) \geq (1 - 1/8^q)\mathcal{a}(t_i, t, \eta, \delta)$  then  
11: for  $j\in [\beta]$  and  $k\in [c\log n]$  do  
12: append the times  $t_{i+1,j,*}^{(k)}$  to  $\{t_{i,j,*}^{(k)}\}$ .  
13: delete  $t_{i+1}$  and all times  $t_{i+1,*}$ .  
14: relabel the times  $\{t_i\}$  and  $\{t_{i,j,*}^{(k)}\}$ .

algorithm 8 subroutine stitchsw of algorithm 5: output estimate of  $f_{p}$  on the sliding window  
1: let  $i$  be the largest index such that  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$ .  
2: let  $k$  be the smallest integer such that  $n^c / 2^k \leq \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, 1, \delta)$ .  
3:  $c_0 \gets t_i$ .  
4:  $x \gets \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, \eta, \delta)$ .  
5: for  $j \in [\beta]$  do  
6: let  $a$  be the smallest index such that  $t_{i,j,a}^{(k)} \geq c_{j-1}$   
7: let  $b$  be the largest index such that  $t_{i,j,b}^{(k)} \leq m - w + 1$   
8:  $c_j \gets t_{i,j,b}^{(k)}$   
9:  $y_j \gets \sum_{k=a}^{b-1} \text{sdiffest}(t_{i,j,k}^{(k)}, t_{i,j,k+1}^{(k)}, m, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta)$   
10: return  $z := x - \sum_{j=1}^{\beta} y_j$

$(1 - 1 / 8^{q})\mathcal{a}(t_{i^{\prime}},t,\eta ,\delta)$  at some point  $t$  . since  $\eta = \frac{\varepsilon}{2^{20}q\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\leq \frac{1}{1024}$  , we have that conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}$ $f(t_{i^{\prime} + 2},t)\left(1 + \frac{1}{1024}\right)\geq (1 - 1 / 8^{q})\left(1 - \frac{1}{1024}\right)f(t_{i^{\prime}},t)$  . thus,  $f(t_{i^{\prime} + 2},t)\geq (1 - 1 / 4^{q})f(t_{i^{\prime}},t)$

we have either  $t_i = m - w + 1$  and  $t_{i+1} = m - w + 2$  in which case the statement is trivially true or timestamps  $t_i$  and  $t_{i+1}$  at some point  $t \leq m$  must have satisfied  $f(t_{i+1},t) \geq (1 - 1/4^q)f(t_i,t)$  so that  $t_i + 1$  was removed from the set of timestamps. thus,  $f(t_{i+1},t) \geq (1 - 1/4^q)f(t_i,t)$  and recall that we assume  $f$  is  $(\varepsilon, \varepsilon^q)$ -smooth. by the definition of smoothness,  $f(t_{i+1},t) \geq (1 - 1/4^q)f(t_i,t)$  implies  $f(t_{i+1},m) \geq \frac{1}{2} \cdot f(t_i,m)$  for all  $m \geq t$ . since  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$ , then  $f(t_i,m) \geq f(m - w + 1,m) = f(w) \geq f(t_{i+1},m)$  and the conclusion follows.

we now show that our difference estimators are well-defined and thus give good approximations to the differences that they estimate. although this sounds trivial, we remark that a pre-condition to a  $(\gamma ,\varepsilon ,\delta)$ -difference estimator to succeed on the approximation of a difference  $f(v + u_{1}) - f(u_{1})$

we must have  $f(v + u_{1}) - f(u_{1}) \leq \gamma \cdot f(v + u_{1})$ , which requires proving properties about the specific partition of the stream. on the other hand, since guessandupdate partitions the stream into blocks for each difference estimator based on their contribution, we show that the pre-condition holds by construction.

lemma 8.4 (accuracy of difference estimators). let  $i$  be the largest index such that  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$ . let  $k$  be the smallest integer such that  $n^c / 2^k \leq \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, 1, \delta)$ . let  $\mathcal{e}$  be the event that all subroutines  $\mathcal{a}$  and sdiffest in algorithm 5 succeed. then conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}$ , we have that for each  $j \in [\beta]$  and all  $\ell$ , sdiffest  $(t_{i,j,\ell-1}^{(k)}, t_{i,j,\ell}, t, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta)$  gives an additive  $\eta \cdot f(t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)} : t)$  approximation to  $f(t_{i,j,\ell-1}^{(k)} : t) - f(t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)} : t)$ .

proof. recall that for a suffix-pivoted difference estimator to be well-defined, we require  $f(t_{i,j,\ell-1}^{(k)}: t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}) \leq \gamma_j \cdot f(t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}: x)$  at some time  $x$ . since  $\gamma_j = 2^{3-j}$  and guessandupdate creates a new partition at level  $j$  when  $\mathcal{a}(t_{s+1,j,r_k}^{(k)}, t-1,1,\delta) \leq n^c / 2^{j+k+10}$ , then it follows that  $f(t_{i,j,\ell-1}^{(k)}: t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}) \leq \gamma_j \cdot f(t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}: x)$ . thus the conditions for a suffix-pivoted difference estimator hold, so sdiffest( $t_{i,j,\ell-1}^{(k)}, t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}, m, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta$ ) gives an additive  $\eta \cdot f(t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}: t)$  approximation to  $f(t_{i,j,\ell-1}^{(k)}: m) - f(t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}: m)$  by the guarantees of the difference estimator.

we now lower bound the values of each difference. this is important for bounding the number of difference estimators that are used to stitch together the estimate at the end of the stream. for example, without a lower bound on the difference estimators at each level, it could be possible that we use a linear number of difference estimators to form our estimate of  $f(w)$ , in which case the space would be too large. using the following lower bound on the difference estimator, we ultimately show only a logarithmic number of difference estimators is used to form our estimate of  $f(w)$ . the intuition behind the proof of lemma 8.5 is that if the contributions of the difference estimators of two consecutive blocks are both small, then mergesw will end up merging the blocks. hence, we should expect the contribution of any two consecutive blocks to be sufficiently large.

lemma 8.5 (geometric lower bounds on splitting times). let  $i$  be the largest index such that  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$ . let  $k$  be the smallest integer such that  $n^c / 2^k \leq \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, 1, \delta)$ . let  $\mathcal{e}$  be the event that all subroutines  $\mathcal{a}$  and sdiffest in algorithm 5 succeed. for any  $\ell \in [r_k - 2]$ , let  $u$  denote the frequency vector between times  $t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}$  and  $t_{i,j,\ell+2}^{(k)}$  and let  $w$  denote the frequency vector between times  $t_{i,j,\ell+2}^{(k)}$  and  $t$ . finally, let  $w$  denote the frequency vector between times  $t_i$  and  $t$ . then conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}$ , we have that

$$
f (u + v) - f (v) \geq 2 ^ {- j - 8} \cdot f (w).
$$

proof. let  $u_{1}$  denote the frequency vector between times  $t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}$  and  $t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)}$  and  $u_{2}$  denote the frequency vector between times  $t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)}$  and  $t_{i,j,\ell+2}^{(k)}$ . recall that mergesw merges two instances of sdiffest when

$$
\mathrm {s d i f f e s t} (t _ {i, j, \ell - 1} ^ {(k)}, t _ {i, j, \ell} ^ {(k)}, t, \gamma_ {j}, \eta , \delta) + \mathrm {s d i f f e s t} (t _ {i, j, \ell} ^ {(k)}, t _ {i, j, \ell + 1} ^ {(k)}, t, \gamma_ {j}, \eta , \delta) \leq 2 ^ {- j - 1 0} \cdot \mathcal {a} _ {i} (t _ {i}, t, \eta , \delta).
$$

since we do not remove  $t_{i,j,\ell +1}^{(k)}$  from the list of timestamps, then we have

$$
\mathrm {s d i f f e s t} (t _ {i, j, \ell - 1} ^ {(k)}, t _ {i, j, \ell} ^ {(k)}, t, \gamma_ {j}, \eta , \delta) + \mathrm {s d i f f e s t} (t _ {i, j, \ell} ^ {(k)}, t _ {i, j, \ell + 1} ^ {(k)}, t, \gamma_ {j}, \eta , \delta) > 2 ^ {- j - 1 0} \cdot \mathcal {a} _ {i} (t _ {i}, t, \eta , \delta).
$$

since the difference estimators are well-defined by lemma 8.4, then  $\mathrm{sdiffest}(t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}, t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)}, t, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta)$  is at most a 2-approximation to  $f(u_1 + u_2 + v) - f(u_2 + v)$  and we similarly obtain a 2-approximation to  $f(u_2 + v) - f(v)$ . therefore,

$$
f (u) - f (v) = f \left(u _ {1} + u _ {2} + v\right) - f (v) \geq 2 ^ {- j - 8} \cdot f (w).
$$

![](images/e4d8fc5e0b84c8fb07881dda1098cf7549fc860f7fe4e425bc58214eee52ba57.jpg)

we next bound the number of level  $j$  difference estimators that can occur from the end of the previous level  $j - 1$  difference estimator to the time when the sliding window begins. we say a difference estimator sdiffest is active if  $\ell \in [a_j, b_j]$  for the indices  $a_j$  and  $b_j$  defined in algorithm 5. the active difference estimators in each level will be the algorithms whose output are subtracted from the initial rough estimate to form the final estimate of  $f_2(w)$ . the intuition behind the proof of lemma 8.6 is that lemma 8.5 lower bounds the contributions of the difference estimators of two consecutive blocks. hence there cannot be too many active difference estimators in a level or else their contributions will be too large for the level.

lemma 8.6 (number of active level  $j$  difference estimators). let  $i$  be the largest index such that  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$ . let  $k$  be the smallest integer such that  $n^c / 2^k \leq \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, 1, \delta)$ . let  $\mathcal{e}$  be the event that all subroutines  $\mathcal{a}$  and sdiffest in algorithm 5 succeed. for each  $j \in [\beta]$ , let  $a_j$  be the smallest index such that  $t_{i,j,a_j} \geq c_{j-1}$  and let  $b_j$  be the largest index such that  $t_{i,j,b_j} \leq m - w + 1$ . then conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}$ , we have  $b_j - a_j \leq 512$ .

proof. conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}$ , we have that for each  $j\in [\beta ]$  and all  $\ell$ ,

$$
2 ^ {- j - 8} \cdot f (w) <   f (t _ {i, j, \ell} ^ {(k)}, t) - f (t _ {i, j, \ell + 2} ^ {(k)}, t),
$$

by lemma 8.5. by lemma 8.3,  $f_{p}(t_{i},m) \leq 2f_{p}(w)$ . thus by a telescoping argument, we have that for  $j = 1$ ,  $a_{j} - b_{j} < 512$ .

for  $j > 1$ , suppose by way of contradiction that  $b_{j} - a_{j} \geq 512$ . let  $k = b_{j} - a_{j}$  and for  $x \in [k]$ , let  $u_{x}$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from time  $a_{j} + x - 1$  to  $a_{j} + x$ . let  $u$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from time  $t_{i,j,k}$  to  $m$  and  $v$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates from  $t_{i}$  to  $m$ . by lemma 8.5,

$$
f \left(u + \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {k} u _ {\ell}\right) - f (u) \geq 8 \cdot 2 ^ {- j - 8} \cdot f (v),
$$

since there are at least 8 disjoint pairs of tuples  $(i,j,\ell)$  and  $(i,j,\ell +1)$  if  $k > 512$

thus if  $b_{j} - a_{j} > 512$ , then the sum of the outputs of the active difference estimators at level  $j$  is more than  $8 \cdot 2^{-j-8} \cdot f(v)$ . in particular since  $t_{i,j,a_{j}}^{(k)} \geq c_{j-1}$ , the sum of the outputs of the active difference estimators at level  $j$  after  $c_{j-1}$  is at least  $8 \cdot 2^{-j-8} \cdot f(v)$ . however, by lemma 8.5, each difference estimator at level  $j-1$  has output at least  $2^{-j-7} \cdot f(u) > 8 \cdot 2^{-j-8} \cdot f(v)$ . specifically, the difference estimator from times  $t_{i,j,c_{j-1}}^{(k)}$  to  $t_{i,j,c_{j}}^{(k)}$  must have output at least  $2^{-j-7} \cdot f(u) > 8 \cdot 2^{-j-8} \cdot f(v)$ . therefore, there exists some other  $z > c_{j-1}$  such that  $t_{i,j-1,z}^{(k)} \leq m-w+1$ , which contradicts the maximality of  $c_{j-1}$  at level  $j-1$ .

we next upper bound the value of each difference estimator. this is important for upper bounding the error associated with the sliding window possibly not beginning exactly where a difference estimator begins. for example, if a difference estimator exactly computes  $f(1:10) - f(4:10)$  but the sliding window consists of the last eight updates, i.e.,  $f(3:10)$ , then the error of our estimator is still only lower bounded by the difference. thus it is crucial to not only upper bound the error of the difference estimator, but also the value of the difference estimator. the intuition behind the proof of lemma 8.7 is that the subroutine mergesw only creates a new block for each difference estimator when the contribution is in a particular range. specifically, the contribution cannot be too large or else a new block would have already been formed. then by smoothness of the function, the contribution of difference estimator cannot grow to be too large regardless of the subsequent updates in the stream.

lemma 8.7 (geometric upper bounds on splitting times). let  $i$  be the largest index such that  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$ . let  $k$  be the smallest integer such that  $n^c / 2^k \leq \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, 1, \delta)$ . let  $\mathcal{e}$  be the event that all subroutines  $\mathcal{a}$  and sdiffest in algorithm 5 succeed. then conditioned on  $\mathcal{e}$ , we have that for each  $j \in [\beta]$  and each  $\ell$  that either  $t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)} = t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)} + 1$  or  $f(t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}, t) - f(t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)}, t) \leq 2^{-j/q-7/q} \cdot f(t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)}, t)$ .

proof. suppose  $t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)} \neq t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)} + 1$ . then at some time  $x$ , the timestamp  $t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)} + 1$  must have been merged by subroutine mergesw. thus

$$
\mathrm {s d i f f e s t} (t _ {i, j, \ell} ^ {(k)}, t _ {i, j, \ell^ {\prime}} ^ {(k)}, x, \gamma_ {j}, \eta , \delta) + \mathrm {s d i f f e s t} (t _ {i, j, \ell^ {\prime}} ^ {(k)}, t _ {i, j, \ell + 1} ^ {(k)}, x, \gamma_ {j}, \eta , \delta) \leq 2 ^ {- j - 1 0} \cdot \mathcal {a} _ {i} (t _ {i}, x, \eta , \delta).
$$

let  $u_{1}$  be frequency vector representing the updates from time  $t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}$  to  $t_{i,j,\ell'}^{(k)} - 1$ ,  $u_{2}$  represent times  $t_{i,j,\ell'}^{(k)}$  to  $t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)}$ , and  $u$  represent times  $t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}$  to  $t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)}$ , so that  $u_{1} + u_{2} = u$ . let  $x_{1}$  represent the updates between time  $t_{i,j,\ell+1}^{(k)}$  and  $x$  and let  $x_{2}$  represent the updates between time  $x+1$  and  $t$ . since the difference estimators are well-defined by lemma 8.4, we have that sdiffest( $t_{i,j,\ell}^{(k)}, t_{i,j,\ell'}^{(k)}, x, \gamma_{j}, \eta, \delta$ ) is at most a 2-approximation to  $f(u_{1} + u_{2} + x_{1}) - f(u_{2} + x_{1})$  and we similarly obtain a 2-approximation to  $f(u_{2} + x_{1}) - f(x_{1})$ . thus,

$$
f (u + x _ {1}) - f (x _ {1}) = f (u _ {1} + u _ {2} + x _ {1}) - f (x _ {1}) \leq 2 ^ {- j - 7} \cdot f (x _ {1}).
$$

by the  $(\varepsilon, \varepsilon^q)$  smoothness of  $f$ , we thus have that for any vector  $x_2$ ,

$$
f (u + v) - f (v) = f (u + x _ {1} + x _ {2}) - f (x _ {1} + x _ {2}) \leq 2 ^ {- j / q - 7 / q} \cdot f (x _ {1} + x _ {2}) = 2 ^ {- j / q - 7 / q} \cdot f _ {p} (v).
$$

![](images/94030f22353d4bc6f4a6360fecd11d0848eabcee7819364e0304754e701c4276.jpg)

finally, we show the correctness of algorithm 5. the main intuition behind lemma 8.8 is that there are two sources of error. the first source of error originates from the boundaries of the blocks corresponding to the difference estimator not aligning with the beginning of the sliding window. this error, resulting from no difference estimator being assigned to compute the exactly correct value, corresponds to the elements marked by blue in figure 5 and cannot be accounted for even if all difference estimators have zero error. on the other hand, this error is upper bounded by the contribution of a difference estimator at the bottom level, which is bounded by lemma 8.7. the second source of error stems from the approximation error caused by each of the difference estimators. since lemma 8.6 bounds the total number of difference estimators being used in our output, we can also upper bound the total approximation error due to the difference estimators.

lemma 8.8 (correctness of framework). with high probability, algorithm 5 gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to the value of  $f(w)$ .

proof. let  $\mathcal{e}$  be the event that all algorithms throughout the stream succeed and note that  $\mathbf{pr}[\mathcal{e}] \geq 1 - \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$  since  $\delta = \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(m)}$ . thus we condition on  $\mathcal{e}$  throughout the remainder of the proof.

let  $f$  be the frequency vector induced by the window and let  $i$  be the largest index such that  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$  and let  $c_0 = t_i$ . let  $u$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from time  $t_i$  to  $m$ . let  $k$  be the smallest integer such that  $n^c / 2^k \leq \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, 1, \delta)$ . for each  $j \in [\beta]$ , let  $a_j$  be the smallest index such that  $t_{i,j,a_j}^{(k)} \geq c_{i-1}$  and let  $b_j$  be the largest index such that  $t_{i,j,b_j}^{(k)} \leq m - w + 1$ . note that by construction of the guessandupdate forming a new timestamp for all levels  $\ell > k$  each time a new timestamp at level  $k$  is formed, then it follows that  $t_{i,j,a_j} = c_{i-1}$ . let  $u_j$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from time  $a_j$  to  $b_j$ . let  $v$  be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from  $c_\beta$  to  $m - w + 1$ . thus, we have  $u = \sum_{j=1}^\beta u_j + v + f$ , so it remains to show that:

(1)  $f(v + f) - f(f)\leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot f(f)$  
(2) we have an additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(f)$  approximation to  $f(v + f)$ .

combined, these two statements show that we have a multiplicative  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation of  $f_{p}(f)$ .

to show that  $f(v + f) - f(f) \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(f)$ , let  $\ell$  be the index such that  $t_{i,\beta,\ell}^{(k)} = c_{\beta}$ , so that  $t_{i,\beta,\ell}^{(k)} \leq m - w + 1 \leq t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)}$ . note that if  $t_{i,\beta,\ell}^{(k)} = m - w + 1$  then  $v = 0$  and so the claim is trivially true. otherwise by the definition of the choice of  $c_{\beta}$ , we have that  $t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)} > m - w + 1$  so that  $t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)} > t_{i,\beta,\ell}^{(k)} + 1$ .

by lemma 8.7, it follows that  $f(t_{i,\beta,\ell}^{(k)}, m) - f(t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)}, m) \leq 2^{-\beta/q} \cdot f_p(t_{i,\beta,k+1}, m)$ . since  $\beta = \left\lceil \log \frac{100 \cdot 4^q}{\varepsilon^q} \right\rceil$ , then it follows that  $f(t_{i,\beta,\ell}^{(k)}, m) - f(t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)}, m) \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{4} \cdot f(t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)}, m)$ . by lemma 8.3, we have that  $\frac{\varepsilon}{4} \cdot f(t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)}, m) \leq 2f(w) = 2f(f)$ . thus,  $f(t_{i,\beta,\ell}^{(k)}, m) - f(t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)}, m) \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(f)$ . since the updates from the times between  $t_{i,\beta,\ell}^{(k)}$  and  $m$  form the vector  $v + f$ , we have  $f(v + f) - f(t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)}, m) \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(f)$ . since  $t_{i,\beta,\ell+1}^{(k)} > m - w + 1$  then by the monotonicity of  $f$ , we have that  $f(v + f) - f(f) \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(f)$ , as desired.

we next show that we have an additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(f)$  approximation to  $f(v + f)$ . if we define

$$
\delta_ {j} := f \left(u - \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {j - 1} u _ {k}\right) - f \left(u - \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {j} u _ {k}\right),
$$

then we have

$$
\sum_ {j = 1} ^ {\beta} \delta_ {j} = f (u) - f (v + f).
$$

observe that  $\delta_j$  is approximated by the active level  $j$  difference estimators. by lemma 8.6, there are at most 512 active indices at level  $j$ . since the difference estimators are well-defined by lemma 8.4 and each difference estimator sdiffest uses accuracy parameter  $\eta = \frac{\varepsilon}{2^{20}q\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}$ , then the additive error in the estimation of  $f(v + f)$  incurred by  $y_{j}$  is at most

$$
5 1 2 \cdot \frac {\varepsilon}{2 ^ {2 0} q \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon}} \cdot f (u) = \frac {\varepsilon}{2 ^ {1 1} q \log \frac {1}{\varepsilon}} \cdot f (u).
$$

summing across all  $\beta \leq 2^9 q\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}$  levels, then the total error in the estimation  $z$  of  $f(v + f)$  across all levels  $y_{j}$  with  $j\in [\beta ]$  is at most

$$
\frac {\varepsilon}{4} \cdot f (u) \leq \frac {\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f (f).
$$

thus, we have an additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(f)$  approximation to  $f(v + f)$  as desired. since  $f(v + f) - f(f) \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \cdot f(f)$ , then algorithm 5 outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f(w)$ .

theorem 8.9 (framework for sliding window algorithms). let  $\varepsilon, \delta \in (0,1)$  be constants and  $f$  be a monotonic and polynomially bounded function that is  $(\varepsilon, \varepsilon^q)$ -smooth for some constant  $q \geq 0$ . suppose there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -suffix pivoted difference estimator that uses space  $\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} s_f(m, \delta, \varepsilon)$  and a streaming algorithm for  $f$  that uses space  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} s_f(m, \delta, \varepsilon)$ , where  $s_f$  is a monotonic function in  $m$ ,  $\frac{1}{\delta}$ , and  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon}$ . then there exists a sliding window algorithm that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$  approximation to  $f$  that succeeds with constant probability and uses  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \cdot s_f(m, \delta', \varepsilon) \cdot \text{poly}(\log m, \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon})$  space, where  $\delta' = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\text{poly}(m)}\right)$ .

proof. we first observe that correctness follows from lemma 8.8 for  $\delta' = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(m)}\right)$ . to analyze the space complexity, note that we maintain  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  guesses for the value of  $f$  at the end of the stream, since  $f$  is monotonic and polynomially bounded. for each guess, we maintain  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  granularities whose space complexity in total forms a geometric series that sums to  $s_f(m, \delta', \varepsilon) \cdot \mathrm{polylog} \frac{1}{\varepsilon}$ . finally, we repeat this procedure for each of the  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  indices  $i$  corresponding to the timestamps maintained at the top level by the smooth histogram, again because  $f$  is monotonic and polynomially bounded. therefore, the total space used is  $\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \cdot s_f(m, \delta', \varepsilon) \cdot \mathrm{poly}\left(\log m, \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ .

# 8.1 moment estimation for  $p \in (0,2]$

to improve algorithm 5 for  $f_{p}$  moment estimation with  $p \in (0,2]$ , we remove the additional  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  overhead associated with making  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  guesses for the value of  $f$  at the end of the stream. instead, we note that since we maintain a constant factor approximation to the value of  $f_{p}$  due to the smooth histogram, it suffices to partition the substream into blocks for the difference estimators based on the ratio of the difference to the constant factor approximation to the value of  $f_{p}$ . we also recall the following useful characterization of the smoothness of the  $f_{p}$  moment function.

lemma 8.10. [bo07] the  $f_{p}$  function is  $\left(\varepsilon, \frac{\varepsilon^p}{p}\right)$ -smooth for  $p \geq 1$  and  $(\varepsilon, \varepsilon)$ -smooth for  $0 < p \leq 1$ .

moreover, we require constructions of suffix-pivoted difference estimators for  $f_{p}$  moment estimation with  $p \in (0,2]$ . however, we claim that the previous constructions, i.e., the fixed-prefix difference estimator based on li's geometric estimator for  $p \in (0,2)$  and the fixed-prefix difference estimator based on the inner product sketch for  $p = 2$  are already valid suffix-pivoted difference estimators. this is because the key property to approximating the difference  $f_{p}(v + u_{1}) - f_{p}(u_{1})$  in "small" space is that  $f_{p}(v)$  is small. we can express the variance of both li's geometric estimator and the inner product sketch in terms of  $f_{p}(v)$  so that smaller values of  $f_{p}(v)$  correspond to smaller variance for the estimators. hence even if  $f_{p}(v + u_{2}) - f_{p}(u_{2})$  is much larger than  $f_{p}(v + u_{1}) - f_{p}(u_{1})$  for some  $u_{2} \succeq u_{1}$ , as long as  $f_{p}(v + u_{1}) - f_{p}(u_{1}) \leq \gamma \cdot f_{p}(v + u_{1})$ , then  $f_{p}(v) \leq \gamma \cdot f_{p}(v + u_{1}) \leq f_{p}(v + u_{2})$ . therefore, the variance for our difference estimators is at most  $\gamma f_{p}(v + u_{2})^{2}$ , so we only need to run  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)$  independent copies of the difference estimators.

the remaining aspects of our algorithms are the same as algorithm 5; for completeness, we give the modified algorithms in algorithm 9.

algorithm 9 moment estimation in the sliding window model  
input: stream  $u_{1},\ldots ,u_{m}\in [n]$  of updates to coordinates of an underlying frequency vector, accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$ , window parameter  $w > 0$   
output: robust  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f_{p}$   
1:  $\delta \leftarrow \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n,m)}$ ,  $\eta \leftarrow \frac{\varepsilon}{1024\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}$ ,  $\varphi \leftarrow \sqrt{2}$  be a parameter  
2:  $\beta \leftarrow \left\lceil \log \frac{100\max(p,1)}{\varepsilon^{\max(p,1)}}\right\rceil$ ,  $\gamma_{j} \leftarrow 2^{3 - j}$  for all  $j \in [\beta]$   
3: for each update  $u_{t} \in [n]$ ,  $t \in [m]$  do  
4: updatesw ▷create new subroutines for each update  
5: mergesw ▷removes extraneous subroutines  
6: return  $z \leftarrow \text{stitchsw}$  ▷estimate  $f_{p}$  on sliding window

algorithm 10 subroutine updatesw for moment estimation  
1: let  $s$  be the number of instances of  $\mathcal{a}$   
2:  $t_{s+1} \gets t$   
3: start a new instance  $\mathcal{a}(t_{s+1}, t, \eta, \delta)$ .  
4: start a new instance hhest $(t_{s+1}, t, \eta, \delta)$ .  
5: for  $j \in [\beta]$  do  
6:  $t_{s+1, j, 0} \gets t$   
7: start a new instance sdiffest $(t_{s+1}, t_{s+1, j, 1}, t, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta)$ .  
8: start a new instance  $\mathcal{a}(t_{s+1, j, 0}, t_{s+1, j, 1}, 1, \delta)$ .

we show algorithm 9 gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f_{p}(w)$ . for  $p \in (0,1]$ , the algorithm is nearly identical to that of  $p \in [1,2]$ . however due to sub-additivity, the mergesw subroutine now merges two sketches when the sum of their contributions from their difference estimator is small, rather than the sum of their moments is small. we also show algorithm 9 with the modified mergesw subroutine gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f_{p}(w)$  for  $p \in (0,1]$ .

lemma 8.11 (correctness of sliding window algorithm). for  $p \in (0,2]$ , algorithm 9 outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f_{p}(w)$ .

theorem 8.12. given  $\varepsilon >0$  and  $p\in (0,2]$ , there exists a one-pass algorithm in the sliding window model that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to the  $l_{p}$  norm with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$ . the algorithm uses  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\log^3 n\log^3\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  bits of space for  $p = 2$ . for  $p\in (0,2)$ , the algorithm uses space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\log^3 n(\log \log n)^2\log^3\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ .

proof. consider algorithm 5 and note that it suffices to output a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to the  $f_{p}$ -moment of the window. thus correctness follows from lemma 8.11.

to analyze the space complexity for  $p = 2$ , we can use the strong tracker of theorem 3.1 and the difference estimator of lemma 3.6. observe that running a single instance of these subroutines with accuracy parameter  $\eta$  and failure probability  $\delta$  requires space  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\log n}{\eta^2}\left(\log \log n + \log \frac{1}{\eta} + \log \frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)$ .

algorithm 11 subroutine mergesw for moment estimation with  $p \in [1,2]$  
1: let  $s$  be the number of instances of  $\mathcal{a}$  and  $p\in [1,2]$    
2:  $\beta \gets \left\lceil \log \frac{100p}{\varepsilon^p}\right\rceil$    
3: for  $i\in [s],j\in [\beta ]$  do ▷difference estimator maintenance   
4: let  $r$  be the number of times  $t_{i,j,*}$    
5: for  $k\in [r - 1]$  do ▷merges two algorithms with "small" contributions   
6: if  $\mathcal{a}(t_{i,j,k - 1},t_{i,j,k + 1},1,\delta)\leq 2^{-j - 10}\cdot \mathcal{a}(t_i,t,\eta ,\delta)$  then   
7: if  $t_{i,j,k}\notin \{t_{i,j - 1,*}\}$  then   
8: merge (add) the sketches for  $\mathcal{a}(t_{i,j,k - 1},t_{i,j,k},1,\delta)$  and  $\mathcal{a}(t_{i,j,k},t_{i,j,k + 1},1,\delta)$    
9: merge (add) the sketches for sdiffest  $(t_{i,j,k - 1},t_{i,j,k},t,\gamma_j,\eta ,\delta)$  and sdiffest  $(t_{i,j,k},t_{i,j,k + 1},t,\gamma_j,\eta ,\delta)$    
10: relabel the times  $t_{i,j,*}$    
11: for  $i\in [s - 2]$  do ▷smooth histogram maintenance   
12: if  $\mathcal{a}(t_{i + 2},t,\eta ,\delta)\geq (1 - 1 / 4^q)\mathcal{a}(t_i,t,\eta ,\delta)$  then   
13: for  $j\in [\beta ]$  do   
14: append the times  $t_{i + 1,j,*}$  to  $\{t_{i,j,*}\}$    
15: delete  $t_{i + 1}$  and all times  $t_{i + 1,**}$    
16: relabel the times  $\{t_i\}$  and  $\{t_{i,j,*}\}$

algorithm 12 subroutine stitchsw of algorithm 5 for moment estimation  
1: let  $i$  be the largest index such that  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$ .  
2:  $c_0 \gets t_i$   
3:  $x \gets \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, \eta, \delta)$   
4: for  $j \in [\beta]$  do  
5: let  $a$  be the smallest index such that  $t_{i,j,a} \geq c_{j-1}$   
6: let  $b$  be the largest index such that  $t_{i,j,b} \leq m - w + 1$   
7:  $c_j \gets t_{i,j,b}$   
8:  $y_j \gets \sum_{k=a}^{b-1} \text{sdiffest}(t_{i,j,k}, t_{i,j,k+1}, m, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta)$   
9: return  $z := x - \sum_{j=1}^{\beta} y_j$

we set the probability of failure to be  $\delta = \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n,m)}$  so that by a union bound, all algorithms in the stream are simultaneously correct at all points in time, with probability  $1 - \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ .

by lemma a.6, we run  $\mathcal{o}\left(2^j\right)$  instances of the suffix difference estimator at level  $j$  for each  $i$ , with accuracy  $\eta = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right)$ , ratio parameter  $\gamma = 2^{3 - j}$ , and  $\delta = \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n,m)}$ . hence, the total space used by level  $j$  for a fixed  $i$  is

$$
\mathcal {o} \left(2 ^ {j} \cdot \frac {\gamma \log^ {2} n}{\eta^ {2}}\right) = \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \log^ {2} n \log^ {2} \frac {1}{\varepsilon}\right).
$$

summing across all  $j \in [\beta]$  for  $\beta = \mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  levels, then the total space is for a fixed  $i$  is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log^2 n \log^3 \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ . since  $i = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ , then the total space is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log^3 n \log^3 \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ .

for  $p < 2$ , we can use the strong tracker of theorem 4.2 and the difference estimator of

lemma 4.12. since a single instance of these subroutines with accuracy parameter  $\eta = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right)$  and failure probability  $\delta = \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n,m)}$  requires space at most  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\log^2 n}{\eta^2} (\log \log n)^2\right)$ , then the total space is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \log^3 n (\log \log n)^2 \log^3 \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ .

# 8.2 entropy estimation in the sliding window model

in this section, we show how our adversarially robust  $f_{p}$  estimation algorithms give a black-box algorithm for entropy estimation. for a frequency vector  $v \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , we define the shannon entropy by  $h = -\sum_{i=1}^{n} v_i \log v_i$ .

observation 8.13. any algorithm that gives an  $\varepsilon$ -additive approximation of the shannon entropy  $h(v)$  also gives a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -multiplicative approximation of the function  $h(v) \coloneqq 2^{h(v)}$  (and vice versa).

thus for the remainder of the section, we focus on obtaining a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -multiplicative approximation of  $h(v) = 2^{h(v)}$ .

lemma 8.14 (section 3.3 in [hno08]). for  $k = \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log m$  and  $\varepsilon' = \frac{\varepsilon}{12(k+1)^3 \log m}$ , there exists an efficiently computable set  $\{y_0, \ldots, y_k\}$  with  $y_i \in (0, 2)$  for all  $i$  and an efficiently computable deterministic function that takes  $(1 + \varepsilon')$ -approximations to  $f_{y_i}(v)$  and outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $h(v) = 2^{h(v)}$ .

the set  $\{y_0,\ldots ,y_k\}$  in lemma 8.14 can be computed as follows, described by [hno08]. let  $\ell = \frac{1}{2(k + 1)\log m}$  and  $f(z) = \frac{(k^2\ell)z - \ell(k^2 + 1)}{2k^2 + 1}$ . then for each  $y_{i}$ , we have  $y_{i} = 1 + f(\cos (i\pi /k))$ , so that the set  $\{y_0,\dots ,y_k\}$  in lemma 8.14 can be computed in linear time. a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -multiplicative approximation to  $h(v) = 2^{h(v)}$  can then be computed from  $2^{p(0)}$ , where  $p(x)$  is the degree  $k$  polynomial interpolated at the points  $y_0,\ldots ,y_k$ , so that  $p(y_{i}) = f_{y_{i}}(v)$  for each  $i$ .

theorem 8.15. given  $\varepsilon >0$ , there exists a sliding window algorithm that outputs an additive  $\varepsilon$ -approximation to shannon entropy and uses  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{\log^5n}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$  bits of space and succeeds with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$ .

proof. by observation 8.13 and lemma 8.14, it suffices to obtain adversarially robust  $(1 + \varepsilon')$ -approximation algorithms to  $f_{y_i}(v)$  for all  $y_i \in (0,2)$  in the set  $\{y_0,\dots,y_k\}$ , where  $k = \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log m$  and  $\varepsilon' = \frac{\varepsilon}{12(k+1)^3\log m}$ . by theorem 8.12 with accuracy parameter  $\varepsilon'$ , we can obtain such robust algorithms approximating each  $f_{y_i}(v)$ , using space  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{1}{(\varepsilon')^2}\log^2 n\right)$ . moreover, with a rescaling of the failure probability  $\delta' = \frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}(n,m)}$  in both lemma 4.12 and theorem 4.2, then all algorithms simultaneously succeed with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ . hence for  $\varepsilon' = \frac{\varepsilon}{12(k+1)^3\log m}$  and  $\log m = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ , each of the  $\mathcal{o}(k)$  algorithms use space  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2}\log^5 n\right)$ . since  $k = \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \log \log m$ , the overall space complexity follows.

# 8.3 moment estimation for integer  $p > 2$

in this section, we describe our algorithm to estimate the  $f_{p}$  moment in the sliding window model, for integer  $p > 2$ . suppose that we have vectors  $u$  and  $v$  such that the vector  $u$  arrives before the vector  $v$  and  $f_{p}(u)\leq \gamma f_{p}(v)\leq 2^{p}f_{p}(u)$ , for some  $\gamma \leq 1$ . a crucial subroutine in algorithm 5 is the

mergesw subroutine, which controls the number of blocks in which the stream is partitioned into, at each granularity, and thus gives efficient bounds on the space of the algorithm. algorithm 11 merges separate blocks based on their contribution to the value of the difference  $f_{p}(u + v) - f_{p}(v)$ . rather than using such a merge subroutine in this section, we will again create  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  parallel instances as in algorithm 7, corresponding to exponentially increasing guesses  $2^{i}$  for the value of  $f_{p}(v)$  and incur the additional  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  overhead in space. we will then partition blocks based on their  $f_{p}$  values in comparison to the guess of  $f_{p}(v)$ . now if each block  $u$  is required to satisfy  $f_{p}(u) \leq \gamma f_{p}(v) \leq 2^{p}f_{p}(u)$  for some  $\gamma \approx \frac{1}{2^j}$ , then we can assume our guess for  $f_{p}(v)$  is incorrect if the partitioning creates more than  $2^{j}$  blocks. hence, we can again assume that each block  $u$  satisfies  $f_{p}(u) \leq \gamma f_{p}(v) \leq 2^{p}f_{p}(u)$ , at the cost of an additional  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  overhead in space.

as in section 8.1, it remains to find a difference estimator for  $f_{p}(u + v) - f_{p}(v)$ , i.e., an algorithm with space dependency  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hat{\epsilon}^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$ . observe that  $f_{p}(u + v) - f_{p}(v) = \sum_{k=1}^{p} \binom{p}{k} \langle u^k, v^{p-k} \rangle$ . we estimate each term  $\langle u^k, v^{p-k} \rangle$  separately.

we first use a heavy-hitter algorithm hhest to simultaneously find all heavy-hitters  $a\in [n]$  such that  $u_{a}\geq \varepsilon \gamma^{1 / p}l_{p}(v)$  across all vectors  $u$  induced by the blocks. these coordinates form a set  $\mathcal{h}$  and we read off the corresponding coordinates of  $v$  to estimate  $\sum_{a\in \mathcal{h}}\langle u^k,v^{p - k}\rangle$  for each  $k$ . for  $a\notin \mathcal{h}$ , we analyze separate algorithms for estimating  $\sum_{a\notin \mathcal{h}}\langle u,v^{p - 1}\rangle$  and  $\sum_{a\notin \mathcal{h}}\langle u^k,v^{p - k}\rangle$  for  $k\geq 2$ , though the analysis is similar for both algorithms. thus we require the following modification to subroutine stitchsw.

algorithm 13 subroutine stitchsw of algorithm 5: output estimate of  $f_{p}$  on the sliding window  
1: let  $i$  be the largest index such that  $t_i \leq m - w + 1$ .  
2:  $c_0 \gets t_i$   
3:  $x \gets \mathcal{a}(t_i, m, \eta, \delta)$   
4: for  $j \in [\beta]$  do  
5: let  $a$  be the smallest index such that  $t_{i,j,a} \geq c_{j-1}$   
6: let  $b$  be the largest index such that  $t_{i,j,b} \leq m - w + 1$   
7:  $c_j \gets t_{i,j,b}$   
8:  $y_j \gets \sum_{k=a}^{b-1} \text{sdiffest}(t_{i,j,k}, t_{i,j,k+1}, m, \gamma_j, \eta, \delta)$   
9: for each heavy-hitter  $k$  output by  $\mathcal{h} := \text{hhest}(t_i, m, \eta, \delta)$  do  
10: let  $g_k$  be the estimated frequency of  $k$  in times  $[c_\beta, m]$ .  
11: let  $h_k$  be the estimated frequency of  $k$  in times  $[m - w + 1, m]$ .  
12:  $w_k \gets (g_k)^p - (h_k)^p$   
13: return  $z := x - \sum_{j=1}^{\beta} y_j - \sum_{k \in \mathcal{h}} w_k$

our analysis uses a level set argument. for a sufficiently large constant  $\alpha > 0$ , let  $i, j \in [\alpha \log n]$ . then we define the contribution  $c_{i,j,k}$  of level set  $l_{i,j}$  for  $\langle u^k, v^{p - k} \rangle$  by

$$
\sum_ {a \in n} \left[ u _ {a} ^ {k} v _ {a} ^ {p - k} {\bf 1} \left(u _ {a} \in \left[ \frac {\gamma^ {1 / p} l _ {p} (v)}{2 ^ {i}}, \frac {\gamma^ {1 / p} l _ {p} (v)}{2 ^ {i - 1}} \right]\right) {\bf 1} \left(v _ {a} \in \left[ \frac {l _ {p} (v)}{2 ^ {j}}, \frac {l _ {p} (v)}{2 ^ {j - 1}} \right]\right) \right].
$$

then we say contribution  $c_{i,j,k}$  of level set  $l_{i,j}$  is significant if  $c_{i,j,k} \geq \frac{\varepsilon}{p^{p+1} \log^2 n} f_p(v)$ .

lemma 8.16. if  $c_{i,j,k}$  is significant, then  $2^{|i - j|} \leq \frac{\gamma^{k / (p(p - k))} p^{(p + 1) / (p - k)} \log^{2 / (p - k)} n}{\varepsilon^{1 / (p - k)}}$ .

(1) find a list  $\mathcal{h}$  that includes all  $a\in [n]$  with  $u_{a}\geq \frac{\varepsilon}{100p^{p + 1}\log^{2}n}\| u\|_{p}$ .  
(2) using countsketch, obtain an estimate  $\widehat{u_a}$  to  $u_{a}$  with additive error  $\frac{\varepsilon}{100p^{p + 1}\log^2n}\| u\| _p$  for each  $a\in \mathcal{h}$  and let  $h\in \mathbb{r}^n$  be the vector such that  $h_a = \widehat{u_a}$  if  $a\in \mathcal{h}$  and zero otherwise.  
(3) perform perfect  $l_{2}$  sampling on  $w := u - h$  to obtain a set  $s$  of size  $k = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$ .  
(4) obtain an estimate  $\widehat{w_a}$  to  $w_{a}$  for each  $a\in s$  
(5) let  $\widehat{w}$  be a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $\| w\| _2^2$  
(6) output  $w + \sum_{k=1}^{p-1} \binom{p}{k} \left( \sum_{a \in \mathcal{h}} \widehat{v_a}^k, u_a^{p-k} + w \cdot \sum_{a \in \mathcal{s}} \widehat{w_a}^{k-2}, u_a^{p-k} \right)$ .

proof. suppose without loss of generality that  $i \leq j$ , so that  $j = i + \ell$  for some  $\ell \geq 0$ . because  $f_{p}(u) \leq \gamma f_{p}(v)$ , then there can be at most  $2^{ip}$  indices  $a \in [n]$  such that  $u_{a} \in \left[\frac{\gamma^{1/p} l_{p}(v)}{2^{i}}, \frac{\gamma^{1/p} l_{p}(v)}{2^{i-1}}\right]$ . hence, we have

$$
c _ {i, j, k} \leq 2 ^ {i p} \cdot \left(\frac {\gamma^ {1 / p} l _ {p} (v)}{2 ^ {i}}\right) ^ {k} \cdot \left(\frac {l _ {p} (v)}{2 ^ {j}}\right) ^ {p - k} = \frac {\gamma^ {k / p}}{2 ^ {\ell (p - k)}} f _ {p} (v).
$$

since  $c_{i,j,k}$  is significant, then  $c_{i,j,k} \geq \frac{\varepsilon}{p^{p+1} \log^2 n} f_p(v)$ , which implies that  $2^{\ell(p-k)} \leq \frac{\gamma^{k/p} p^{p+1} \log^2 n}{\varepsilon}$  and thus  $2^j \leq 2^i \frac{\gamma^{k/(p(k))} p^{(p+1)/(p-k)} \log^{2/(p-k)} n}{\varepsilon^{1/(p-k)}}$ .

we first run a heavy-hitters algorithm to find all indices  $a \in [n]$  such that

$$
u _ {a} \geq \frac {\varepsilon}{1 0 0 p ^ {p + 1} \log^ {2} n} \gamma^ {1 / p} l _ {p} (v) \geq \frac {\varepsilon}{1 0 0 p ^ {p + 1} \log^ {2} n} l _ {p} (u),
$$

which takes  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$  space, since  $f_{p}(u)\leq \gamma f_{p}(v)$ . thus we bound the variance for a significant level set  $l_{i,j}$  with  $2^{i}\geq \frac{\varepsilon}{100p^{p + 1}\log^{2}n}$ .

lemma 8.17. for integer  $p > 2$ , there exists an algorithm that uses space  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$  and outputs an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f_p(u)$  approximation to  $\langle u^k, v^{p - k} \rangle$  with high probability for any  $k \geq 2$ .

proof. we first show that our algorithm gives an additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{p^{p+1}} f_p(v)$  approximation to  $\langle u^k, v^{p-k} \rangle$  with  $k \geq 2$ . to that end, we show that our algorithm gives an additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{p^{p+1} \log^2 n} f_p(v)$  approximation to each contribution  $c_{i,j,k}$  of level set  $l_{i,j}$ . let  $h \in \mathbb{r}^n$  be the vector such that  $h_a = \widehat{u_a}$  if  $a \in \mathcal{h}$  and zero otherwise and let  $w := u - h$ .

we use sampler to sample indices  $j_1, \ldots, j_\ell \in [n]$  with so that each sample is a coordinate  $a \in [n]$  with probability  $\frac{w_a^2}{\|w\|_2^2} + \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ . we obtain unbiased estimates to  $\widehat{w_{j_1}^{k-2}}, \ldots, \widehat{w_{j_\ell}^{k-2}}$  of  $w_{j_1}^{k-2}, \ldots, w_{j_\ell}^{k-2}$  through lemma 5.5. we also obtain a  $(1 + \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon))$ -approximation unbiased estimate  $w$  of  $\|w\|_2^2$ . thus for each  $b \in [\ell]$ , the product  $\widehat{w_{j_b}^{k-2}} \cdot w \cdot v_{j_b}^{p-k}$  satisfies

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {w _ {j _ {b}} ^ {k - 2}} \cdot w \cdot v _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - k} \right] = \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {h}} \langle \widehat {u _ {a}} ^ {k}, v _ {a} ^ {p - k} \rangle + \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \cap \mathcal {h}} \left(\frac {w _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot w _ {a} ^ {k - 2} \cdot (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot v _ {a} ^ {p - k}
$$

$$
+ \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} \left(\frac {w _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot w _ {a} ^ {k - 2} \cdot (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot v _ {a} ^ {p - k}.
$$

observe that since  $\widehat{u_a}$  is an additive  $\varepsilon \gamma^{1/p} l_p(v)$ -approximation to  $u_a$  for  $a \in \mathcal{h}$ , then  $|w_a| \leq \varepsilon |u_a|$ , so that the second summation is at most  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon) \langle u^k, v^{p-k} \rangle$ . moreover, we have  $w_a = u_a$  for  $a \in [n] \setminus \mathcal{h}$ , so that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {w _ {j _ {b}} ^ {k - 2}} \cdot w \cdot v _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - k} \right] \in (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) | \langle w ^ {k}, v ^ {p - k} \rangle | + \frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y} (n)}.
$$

for  $a \in l_{i,j} \setminus \mathcal{h}$ , we have  $w_a \leq u_a \leq \varepsilon \gamma^{1/p} l_p(v)$ . thus, the variance is at most

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v a r} \left(\widehat {w _ {j _ {b}} ^ {k - 2}} \cdot w \cdot v _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - k}\right) \leq \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} \left(\frac {w _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot w _ {a} ^ {2 k - 4} \cdot (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {4} \cdot v _ {a} ^ {2 p - 2 k} \\ \leq \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) w _ {a} ^ {2 k - 2} \cdot \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot v _ {a} ^ {2 p - 2 k} \\ \leq \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \frac {\varepsilon^ {p} \gamma^ {2} (l _ {p} (v)) ^ {2 p - 2}}{2 ^ {2 i p}} \frac {\gamma^ {2 k / p} p ^ {p (2 p + 2)} \log^ {4} n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} \frac {c n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \varepsilon^ {p} \gamma (f _ {p} (v)) ^ {2} \log^ {4} n}{2 ^ {2 i p} \varepsilon^ {2}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

for some constant  $c > 0$ . hence, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v a r} \left(\widehat {w _ {j _ {b}} ^ {k - 2}} \cdot w \cdot v _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - k}\right) \leq \sum_ {i, j} 2 ^ {i p} \cdot \frac {c n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \varepsilon^ {p} \gamma (f _ {p} (v)) ^ {2} \log^ {4} n}{2 ^ {2 i p} \varepsilon^ {2}} \\ \leq \mathcal {o} \left(\log^ {2} n\right) \cdot \frac {c n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \varepsilon^ {p} \gamma (f _ {p} (v)) ^ {2} \log^ {4} n}{\varepsilon^ {2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

thus by setting  $\ell = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$ , we obtain an additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{p^{p + 1}} \cdot f_p(v)$  approximation to  $\langle u^k, v^{p - k} \rangle$  for  $k \geq 2$ , with constant probability. we can then boost this probability to  $1 - \frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$  by repeating  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$  times.

we use a similar approach to estimate  $\langle u,v^{p - 1}\rangle$

lemma 8.18. for integer  $p > 2$ , there exists an algorithm that uses space  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$  and outputs an additive  $\varepsilon \cdot f_p(u)$  approximation to  $\langle u, v^{p - 1} \rangle$  with high probability.

proof. we use sampler to sample indices  $j_1, \ldots, j_\ell \in [n]$  with so that each sample is a coordinate  $a \in [n]$  with probability  $\frac{v_a^2}{\|v\|_2^2} + \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$ . we obtain unbiased estimates to  $\widehat{v_{j_1}^{p-1}}, \ldots, \widehat{v_{j_\ell}^{p-1}}$  of  $v_{j_1}^{p-1}, \ldots, v_{j_\ell}^{p-1}$  through lemma 5.5. we also obtain a  $(1 + \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon))$ -approximation unbiased estimate  $v$  of  $\|v\|_2^2$ . thus for each  $b \in [\ell]$ , the product  $\widehat{w_{j_b}} \cdot v \cdot v_{j_b}^{p-3}$  satisfies

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {w _ {j _ {b}}} \cdot v \cdot v _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - 3} \right] = \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {h}} \langle \widehat {u _ {a}}, v _ {a} ^ {p - 1} \rangle + \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \cap \mathcal {h}} \left(\frac {v _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| v \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot w _ {a} \cdot (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| v \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot v _ {a} ^ {p - 3}
$$

(1) use a set of exponential random variables to form a vector  $w$  of duplicated and scaled coordinates of  $w$ .  
(2) hash the coordinates of  $w$  into a countsketch data structure with  $\mathcal{o}(\log n)$  buckets.  
(3) use the same exponential random variables to perform perfect  $l_{2}$  sampling on  $v$  to obtain a coordinate  $(i,j)$  and an unbiased estimate  $\widehat{v_{i,j}}$  to  $v_{i,j}$ .  
(4) let  $\widehat{v}$  be an unbiased estimate of  $\| v\| _2^2$  with second moment  $\mathcal{o}\left(\| v\| _2^4\right)$ .  
(5) query countsketch for an unbiased estimate  $\widehat{w_{i,j}}$  to  $w_{i,j}$  and set an estimator as  $\widehat{v} \cdot \widehat{w_{i,j}} (\widehat{v_{i,j}})^{p-3}$ .  
(6) output the mean of  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} \cdot n^{1 - 2/p}\right)$  such estimators.

$$
+ \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} \left(\frac {v _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| v \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot w _ {a} \cdot (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot v _ {a} ^ {p - 3}.
$$

observe that since  $\widehat{u_a}$  is a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $u_{a}$  for  $a\in \mathcal{h}$ , then  $|w_{a}|\leq \varepsilon |u_{a}|$  so that the second summation is at most  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon)\langle u,v^{p - 1}\rangle$ . moreover, we have  $w_{a} = u_{a}$  for  $a\in [n]\setminus \mathcal{h}$ , so that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {w _ {j _ {b}}} \cdot v \cdot v _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - 3} \right] \in (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) | \langle w, v ^ {p - 1} \rangle | + \frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y} (n)}.
$$

for  $a \in l_{i,j} \setminus \mathcal{h}$ , we have  $w_a \leq u_a \leq \varepsilon \gamma^{1/p} l_p(v)$ . thus, the variance is at most

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v a r} \left(\widehat {w _ {j _ {b}}} \cdot v \cdot v _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - 3}\right) \leq \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} \left(\frac {v _ {a} ^ {2}}{\| v \| _ {2} ^ {2}} + \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (n)}\right) \cdot w _ {a} ^ {2} \cdot (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \| v \| _ {2} ^ {4} \cdot v _ {a} ^ {2 p - 6} \\ \leq \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) w _ {a} ^ {2} \cdot \| v \| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot v _ {a} ^ {2 p - 6} \\ \leq \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} (1 \pm \mathcal {o} (\varepsilon)) \frac {\varepsilon^ {p} \gamma^ {2 / p} (l _ {p} (v)) ^ {2 p - 2}}{2 ^ {2 i p}} \frac {\gamma^ {2 k / p} p ^ {p (2 p + 2)} \log^ {4} n}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \| v \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {a \in l _ {i, j} \backslash \mathcal {h}} \frac {c n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \varepsilon^ {p} \gamma^ {2 / p} (f _ {p} (v)) ^ {2} \log^ {4} n}{2 ^ {2 i p} \varepsilon^ {2}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

for some constant  $c > 0$ . therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {v a r} \left(\widehat {w _ {j _ {b}}} \cdot v \cdot v _ {j _ {b}} ^ {p - 3}\right) \leq \sum_ {i, j} 2 ^ {i p} \cdot \frac {c n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \varepsilon^ {p} \gamma^ {2 / p} (f _ {p} (v)) ^ {2} \log^ {4} n}{2 ^ {2 i p} \varepsilon^ {2}} \\ \leq \mathcal {o} \left(\log^ {2} n\right) \cdot \frac {c n ^ {1 - 2 / p} \varepsilon^ {p} \gamma (f _ {p} (v)) ^ {2} \log^ {4} n}{\varepsilon^ {2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

hence for  $\gamma \geq \mathcal{o}(\varepsilon^p)$ , we have that by setting  $\ell = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$ , we obtain an additive  $\frac{\varepsilon}{p^{p + 1}} \cdot f_p(v)$  approximation to  $\langle u, v^{p - 1} \rangle$ , with constant probability. we can then boost this probability to  $1 - \frac{\delta}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$  by repeating  $\mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{n}{\delta}\right)$  times.

lemma 8.19 ( $f_{p}$  difference estimator). for integer  $p > 2$ , there exists a  $(\gamma, \varepsilon, \delta)$ -difference estimator for  $f_{p}$  that uses space  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{\gamma n^{1 - 2/p}}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$ .

by applying the same argument as lemma 8.8 using the difference estimators in this section, we have:

lemma 8.20 (correctness of sliding window algorithm). for integer  $p > 2$ , algorithm 5 outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to  $f_{p}(w)$ .

theorem 8.21. given  $\varepsilon >0$  and integer  $p > 2$ , there exists a one-pass algorithm in the sliding window model that outputs a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to the  $l_{p}$  norm with probability at least  $\frac{2}{3}$  and uses  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$  bits of space.

proof. consider algorithm 5 and note that it suffices to output a  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation to the  $f_{p}$ -moment of the window. thus correctness follows from lemma 8.20.

we use the strong tracker of theorem 5.2 and the difference estimator of lemma 8.19. by lemma a.6, we run  $\mathcal{o}\left(2^j\right)$  instances of the suffix difference estimator at level  $j$  for each  $i$ , with accuracy  $\eta = \mathcal{o}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right)$ , ratio parameter  $\gamma = 2^{3 - j}$ , and  $\delta = \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n,m)}$ . hence, the total space used by level  $j$  for a fixed  $i$  is

$$
\tilde {\mathcal {o}} \left(2 ^ {j} \cdot \frac {\gamma n ^ {1 - 2 / p}}{\eta^ {2}}\right) = \tilde {\mathcal {o}} \left(\frac {n ^ {1 - 2 / p}}{\varepsilon^ {2}}\right).
$$

summing across all  $j \in [\beta]$  for  $\beta = \mathcal{o}\left(\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$  levels and all values of  $i = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$ , then the algorithm uses  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} n^{1 - 2 / p}\right)$  bits of space in total.

# acknowledgements

we would like to thank zhili feng for discussions in the early stages of this work. this work was supported by national science foundation (nsf) grant no. ccf-1815840, national institute of health (nih) grant 5r01 hg 10798-2, and a simons investigator award.

we would also like to thank moshe shechner and uri stemmer for pointing out an error in our bounded flip number algorithm for dynamic streams in a previous version of this paper. in this version, we have introduced the notion of twist number to resolve this issue and refer to their follow-up work [acss21] for more details.
